# The Complete Library in Four Sections
<note>original: "Siku Quanshu." This was a massive 18th-century project commissioned by the Qianlong Emperor of the Qing dynasty to compile all known Chinese literature into a single, standardized collection.</note>

## The Masters Section
<note>original: "Zi Bu." This is one of the four main divisions of the library, specifically housing works on philosophy, military strategy, medicine, astronomy, and the arts.</note>

<image-desc>The cover is a deep indigo blue, typical of the "Masters Section" volumes in the imperial library. On the left side, a vertical white slip acts as a title label, featuring elegant hand-brushed calligraphy. The right edge shows the white silk thread used in the traditional four-hole "stab binding" method, which allows the book to lie flat when opened.</image-desc>

<summary>This is the title page of a volume from the Siku Quanshu, identifying it as part of the "Masters Section," which encompasses a wide range of philosophical and technical writings. The blue cover color was traditionally used to color-code this specific category within the imperial library.</summary>

### The Masters Section
<note>The "Masters Section" (*zi bu*) includes works on philosophy, science, and the arts, distinguishing them from Confucian classics, history, or literature.</note>
## Elucidations on the *Kinship of the Three* in Relation to the Book of Changes, Volume One
<note>original: "Zhouyi Cantong Qi Fahui." The *Cantong Qi* is often called the "Ancestor of Alchemy Books." This specific text is a commentary or "elucidation" (*fahui*) explaining the original alchemical theories using the symbolism of the *Zhouyi* (Book of Changes).</note>

->By Imperial Decree: The Complete Library in Four Sections<-

# Elucidations on the *Kinship of the Three* in Accordance with the Book of Changes

### Masters Section Fourteen
<note>The "Masters Section" (Zi Bu) is one of the four main divisions of the Imperial Library, housing works by philosophers, scientists, and specialized schools of thought.</note>
### Taoist School

## Summary Abstract

Your servants <note>The editors of the Siku Quanshu, writing to the Emperor.</note> respectfully note that *Elucidations on the Kinship of the Three in Accordance with the Book of Changes* <note>original: "Zhouyi Cantong Qi Fahui." The "Kinship of the Three" is a foundational text of Taoist alchemy.</note> consists of three volumes, along with one volume of *Resolving Doubts*.

The work was written by Yu Yan of the Song Dynasty. Yu Yan also authored the *Collected Explanations on the Book of Changes*, which has already been recorded in this catalog. This book uses the water, fire, Yin, and Yang found within the human body to explain the "Way of the Elixir" <note>original: "dandao," referring to internal alchemy or "Neidan," a practice of spiritual and physical transformation.</note>. 

While it may not be considered as specialized a Taoist study as the three famous commentaries by Peng Xiao, Chen Xianwei, and Chen Zhixu, the materials it draws upon are nonetheless extremely broad. Furthermore, the three chapters of its *Resolving Doubts* section examine textual differences and similarities with a level of detail and completeness that surpasses even the edition produced by Master Zhu <note>referring to the preeminent Neo-Confucian scholar Zhu Xi, who wrote a commentary on this text under a pseudonym.</note>.

<summary>This page presents the imperial editors' abstract for a Song Dynasty commentary on the "Kinship of the Three," a classic of Taoist alchemy. It highlights the author Yu Yan's approach of using the human body as a laboratory for spiritual cultivation and notes the text's superior scholarly rigor compared to other famous editions.</summary>
<keywords>Yu Yan, Zhouyi Cantong Qi, Taoism, Internal Alchemy, Siku Quanshu, Zhu Xi, Masters Section, Song Dynasty scholarship</keywords>

->By Imperial Decree: The Complete Library in Four Sections<-

## Summary Abstract

Bai Yunji’s *Catalogue of the Taoist Canon* <note>compiled during the Ming Dynasty, this was the definitive index of Taoist scriptures</note> from the Ming Dynasty states that these two books together consist of fourteen volumes. Jiao Hong’s *Bibliography of the National History* <note>a major bibliographic work of the late Ming dynasty</note>, however, lists it as twelve volumes. 

Mao Jin’s *Jindai Mishu* <note>original: "Jindai Mishu," a famous collection of rare books published by the renowned Ming bibliophile Mao Jin</note> combined Yu Yan’s commentary with the commentaries of Peng Xiao and two other authors into a single edition, which is no longer in its original form; furthermore, he merged the *Resolving Doubts* section and lost portions of it. 

This current edition contains diagrams in every volume and was printed in the third year of the Zhida era <note>1310 CE, during the Yuan Dynasty</note> by Zhang Yufeng, the Successor Celestial Master <note>the 39th leader of the Zhengyi school of Taoism</note>. It actually consists of only three volumes, with one volume of *Resolving Doubts* appended. 

Upon examining Yu Yan's own preface to his *Separate Biography Beyond the Book of Changes*, he also states that the subtle oral secrets of the <term>Way of the Elixir</term> <note>dandao: the internal alchemical practices of transforming life-energy and spirit</note> are all recorded within the three chapters of the *Elucidations on the Kinship of the Three*. Thus, the number Bai Yunji recorded perhaps included other books in his count, while what Jiao Hong recorded was entirely based on the circular trading of book merchants...

<summary>This page addresses historical discrepancies in the volume count of Yu Yan's work, tracing errors in Ming Dynasty catalogs and identifying a 1310 Yuan Dynasty edition as the authoritative three-volume version.</summary>
<keywords>Yu Yan, Cantong Qi, bibliography, Taoist Canon, Mao Jin, internal alchemy, Celestial Master Zhang Yufeng, book history</keywords>

# Preface to the Republication of the Elucidations on the Kinship of the Three

In ancient times, when the Sages created the *Book of Changes* <note>original: "Yi," referring to the I Ching</note>, it began with the sovereign Baoxi <note>Also known as Fuxi, the mythical first emperor who is credited with creating the trigrams</note> receiving the *River Map* <note>original: "Hetu," a mystical diagram revealed on the back of a dragon-horse that emerged from the Yellow River</note> and drawing the trigrams. King Wen doubled these trigrams and attached the hexagram judgments; the Duke of Zhou deduced the auspiciousness or misfortune of the six positions and attached the line statements. Confucius then followed the Great and Small Images to compose the *Ten Wings* <note>The canonical commentaries that explain the philosophical and symbolic depths of the original divinatory text</note>.

Its fundamental principle is suspended from the *Great Ultimate* <term>Taiji: the supreme, undifferentiated source of all existence</term>. Through movement, it generates *Yang*; through stillness, it generates *Yin*. Once these *Two Modes* <note>Yin and Yang</note> were divided, the *Four Images* <note>The major and minor stages of Yin and Yang</note> were established. Movement and stillness serve as each other's roots, giving rise to the *Five Elements* <note>Wood, Fire, Earth, Metal, and Water</note>. Through the intersection of Yin and Yang—of odd and even numbers—the *Eight Trigrams* were formed.

The Yellow Emperor <note>Huangdi, the legendary ancestor of the Chinese people</note> commanded his minister Da Nao to create the *Sexagenary Cycle* <note>original: "Jiazi," the system of 60 cyclical signs used to record days, months, and years</note>. Its *Ten Heavenly Stems* and *Twelve Earthly Branches* do nothing more than explore the essence of the Five Elements, and they never deviate from the spatial directions of the Eight Trigrams. The trigrams were doubled to form sixty-four hexagrams, and their lines were divided into three hundred and eighty-four. Within these, there exists the Way of Heaven, and there exists the Way of Earth...

<summary>This preface traces the legendary lineage of the Book of Changes from Fuxi to Confucius, establishing the text's cosmological foundation in the Great Ultimate (Taiji) and the Five Elements. It serves to frame the alchemical "Kinship of the Three" as a natural extension of these ancient, orthodox principles of the universe.</summary>

<keywords>Book of Changes, I Ching, Fuxi, Taiji, Yin and Yang, Five Elements, Eight Trigrams, Sexagenary Cycle, King Wen, Confucius, cosmology, Daoism</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion on the historical origins of the Book of Changes, moving from the mythological creation of the trigrams to the philosophical application of these principles in understanding the cosmos and the human body.</meta>

**By Imperial Order: The Complete Library of the Four Treasuries**

<margin>Preface</margin>

There is the Way [of Heaven], and there is the Way of Humanity. When the Way of the *Three Powers* <term>Sancai: The triad of Heaven, Earth, and Humanity, representing the totality of the universe</term> is complete, then among all things that fill the space between Heaven and Earth, there is none that does not possess Principle, Vital Force, Image, and Number <note>original: "Li, Qi, Xiang, Shu." In Chinese cosmology, these represent the underlying laws, the material energy, the symbolic forms, and the mathematical patterns of existence</note>. Their filling and emptying, their waxing and waning, their advancement and retreat, and their survival or destruction—none of these fail to fall within the scope of the Sage’s influence. The Sage observes and recognizes the reasons why these things are as they are.

Therefore, as a book, the *Changes* <note>The I Ching</note> is vast and all-encompassing, and it was used to establish teachings. By following the *Five Elements* <note>Wood, Fire, Earth, Metal, and Water</note> and the *Eight Trigrams*, the Sage established methods for astronomical calculation and divination. These were sufficient to "open up the knowledge of things and accomplish the tasks of the world" <note>A classic phrase from the I Ching meaning to provide the tools for civilization to function</note> for the use of the people.

For the *Changes* provides counsel for the gentleman; indeed, can anyone afford not to use the *Changes*? People of the world do not embody the principle of the ceaseless movement of Heaven and Earth. They do not explore the nature of how the Five Elements generate, overcome, or transform one another. They do not follow the positions of the hexagram lines as they come and go, or advance and retreat. They walk in darkness and act recklessly, failing to apply this knowledge to their own physical selves. They do not know what *internal cultivation* <term>Xiulian: The practice of refining one's internal energy and spirit to achieve longevity or spiritual transcendence</term> truly is. Among such people, how few are those who can protect their "seven-foot frame" <note>A traditional poetic term for the adult human body</note> and return it whole to the source at the end of their days?

<summary>This preface explains that the Book of Changes is a comprehensive map of universal laws (Principle, Vital Force, Image, and Number). It argues that ignoring these principles leads to a reckless life and the decay of the physical body, emphasizing the need for internal cultivation.</summary>

<keywords>Three Powers, Five Elements, Eight Trigrams, I Ching, Principle and Vital Force, internal cultivation, Siku Quanshu</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface's exploration of the Way of the Changes. Having established that the Sages used the I Ching to understand the cosmos, the text now transitions into why this knowledge is essential for those seeking immortality and how later masters like Wei Boyang corrected the dangerous misconceptions of their eras.</meta>

**Explication of the Truth of the Kinship of the Three in the *Book of Changes***

[Page Three]

How rare it is indeed! Even less can they hope to achieve long life and lasting vision <term>Changsheng jiushi: A classical phrase for immortality and enduring presence</term> within the realm of Heaven and Earth. As for those belonging to the lineage of the Immortals, they are able to achieve such longevity and enduring presence only because they possess the Way of the *Returned Elixir* <term>Huandan: The "Reverted Elixir," a central concept in internal alchemy representing the restoration of the original, primordial life force</term>.

However, the immortals of antiquity relied upon "heart-to-heart transmission and oral instruction." These secret keys of the moment were strictly guarded and rarely disclosed. For this reason, few accounts of them have been passed down to the world.

Occasionally, there are those who do not follow the Great Way, instead relying on their own narrow cleverness. They claim that winding paths offer easy shortcuts, or that deviant "side doors" <note>original: "pangmen." Refers to heterodox, superficial, or dangerous spiritual practices</note> can lead to the goal. The most extreme among them consume "parching drugs" and "heating potions" <note>This refers to the "Waidan" or external alchemy practice of consuming toxic minerals like mercury or lead, which were often mistakenly thought to grant immortality</note>, regarding them as divine elixirs. Ultimately, they ruin their health and shorten their lives. How, then, could they possibly preserve their longevity?

It was not until the Later Han dynasty that Lord *Wei Boyang* <note>Wei Boyang (c. 151–221 CE) is the legendary author of the *Cantong Qi*, often called the "ancestor of all alchemical texts"</note> recognized that the Way of the Returned Elixir must not be led astray by heterodox theories. He insisted that it must be rooted in the *Way of the Changes* <note>The philosophical and cosmological principles found in the I Ching</note> before it can be truly realized. This is because the *Changes* embodies the mechanics of *Yin and Yang* <term>Yin and Yang: The complementary forces of dark/receptive and light/active</term>, as well as the states of movement and stillness. It encompasses the subtle wonders of the hexagrams and their shifting lines <note>original: "Gua Yao." The symbols and individual lines that make up the I Ching</note>. It is a system of ceaseless generation and infinite transformation. Now, if we look back upon our own...

<summary>This page argues that true immortality (the "Returned Elixir") can only be achieved through the principles of the I Ching, warning against the dangerous "side doors" and toxic potions of misguided practitioners. It introduces Wei Boyang as the pivotal figure who reunited alchemical practice with the "Way of the Changes."</summary>

<keywords>Wei Boyang, Returned Elixir, Way of the Changes, Yin and Yang, Immortality, Internal Alchemy, Cantong Qi, Heterodoxy</keywords>

Having previously warned against "side doors" and toxic potions, the author now defines the core principles of the *Cantong qi* and distinguishes true internal cultivation from the physical laboratory work often mistaken for alchemy.</meta>

-># Imperially Commissioned Complete Library of the Four Treasuries<-
->## Preface<-

The human body is itself a miniature universe <term>Heaven and Earth: A microcosm that mirrors the laws and structures of the macrocosm</term>. The "Primal Breath" <term>Yuanqi: The original, primordial life force</term> that I possess is the very same breath that sustains Heaven and Earth. If Heaven and Earth are able to endure forever, why should I alone be unable to emulate their longevity? This is the reason why the book *The Kinship of the Three* <note>original: "Cantong qi." This text is considered the "ancestor of all alchemical classics"</note> was composed.

"Kinship" (literally "The Three") <gloss>Can</gloss> refers to consulting with the *Book of Changes* <note>original: "Yi." The *I Ching*</note> so as to remain free from error. "Accordance" (literally "The Same") <gloss>Tong</gloss> refers to merging with the *Book of Changes* so as to remain without contradiction. "Tally" <gloss>Qi</gloss> refers to forming a silent contract with the *Book of Changes*, grasping it intuitively until a natural understanding is reached.

In the past, I heard that the ancient Immortals possessed methods for refining elixirs. They claimed that once the elixir was perfected, one could achieve longevity and "soar into the heights" <note>A reference to spiritual ascension or becoming a "flying immortal"</note>. Because of this, the famous mountains and scenic peaks of the world are all filled with the strange remains of "furnaces and stoves" <note>Luzhao: The physical apparatus used in laboratory alchemy</note>. Alas! Those are merely strange stones in the mountains that happen to resemble furnaces and stoves.

It was only upon reading this book that I realized that using physical furnaces and stoves to refine elixirs is a delusion. The teachings of the Immortals regarding "guiding and refining" <term>Daoyin: Physical and breathing exercises meant to circulate internal energy</term> involve movements that take place within; they do not rely on anything outside the body. These methods take what is needed from within oneself and do not depend on external material objects. As for...

<summary>This page explains the philosophical title of the *Cantong qi*, linking its principles to the *Book of Changes*, and argues that true alchemy is an internal spiritual process (Neidan) rather than a physical chemical one (Waidan).</summary>

<keywords>Cantong qi, Internal Alchemy, Neidan, I Ching, Primal Breath, Microcosm, Immortality, Wei Boyang</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the general history of the *Cantong qi* to its specific internal alchemical theory, explaining how the language of external chemistry (gold, lead, mercury) is actually a metaphor for the cultivation of the human body and spirit. It introduces the scholarly lineage of the text, mentioning the famous Neo-Confucian Zhu Xi.</meta>

->### The Kinship of the Three in the Book of Changes, with Elaborations<-

Gold is valued as a treasure because we desire it to be truly pure, unadulterated, and free from falsehood. Yet, without the assistance of **lead and mercury** and the use of **fire** to refine it, how could one ever obtain its true, pure, and resilient essence? How could its substance become an "elixir" <term>Dan: Originally referring to cinnabar or a chemical pill, here it represents the perfected state of the internal spirit</term> that, like Heaven and Earth, becomes immortal?

The *Kinship of the Three* <note>original: "Cantong qi"</note> now borrows this logic for its own meaning. It uses the **Qian and Kun** <term>The two primary hexagrams of the I Ching, representing the Creative (Heaven) and the Receptive (Earth)</term> of our own bodies as the "cauldron and vessel." It uses **Kan and Li** <term>The hexagrams for Water and Fire, representing the Moon and Sun</term> as the "medicinal ingredients." 

By condensing my spirit and gathering my breath <note>Qi: The vital life force</note>, I cause the **Dragon and Tiger** <note>Metaphors for the energies of the Liver (Wood/Yang) and Lungs (Metal/Yin)</note> to couple their essences and interact. By advancing the **fire-phasing** <term>Huohou: The timing and intensity of meditative focus</term> to await the coming transformation, the "Way of the Elixir" can then be successfully accomplished.

However, although the "Master Zhu of Ziyang" <note>Zhu Xi (1130–1200), the most influential Neo-Confucian philosopher, who wrote a commentary on this Taoist text</note> once performed critical research and provided annotations for this book, many gaps and doubts still remain. Even if one says he found the "threads" of the teaching, he had not yet fully grasped its "ingredients and apertures" <note>Meaning the practical, secret "how-to" of the cultivation process</note>. If even someone of Master Zhu’s stature found it so difficult to grasp, how could those who come after him find it easy to research?

Coming to the Yuan dynasty, there was Master Yu Yuwu, known as **Quanyangzi** <note>Yu Yan (1258–1314), a scholar-practitioner who sought to bridge the gap between the Book of Changes and Taoist practice</note>. He was the first to truly devote his essence to researching this book. In the beginning, he did not find its "aperture" <gloss>Qiao: The secret key or energetic gate</gloss>, and seeking it was exceedingly difficult. However, through the accumulation of sincere effort...

<summary>This page explains the metaphorical nature of internal alchemy, where the body’s own energies act as the chemicals and vessels of a spiritual laboratory. It highlights the difficulty of the text, noting that even the great scholar Zhu Xi struggled to unlock its practical secrets.</summary>

<keywords>Internal Alchemy (Neidan), Lead and Mercury, Qian and Kun, Kan and Li, Dragon and Tiger, Fire-phasing (Huohou), Zhu Xi, Yu Yan (Quanyangzi)</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's personal journey with the *Cantong qi*, moving from academic study to a deeper, almost mystical realization of the text's alignment with both the Book of Changes and the secret oral traditions of Daoist masters.</meta>

# Imperial Edition of the Complete Library in Four Sections - Preface

As I believed in this with ever-increasing sincerity, it felt as though I received a divine revelation and reached a spontaneous understanding. I then extensively collected the secret formulas of various **True Men** <term>Zhenren: Daoist masters who have attained the Way and reached immortality</term> and compared them with this book. I found that nothing failed to align with the teachings of the *Book of Changes* <note>original: "Yi"</note>. Therefore, by elaborating upon this work and adding a commentary <note>literally "adding a footnote"</note>, the **Way of the Elixir** <term>Dandao: The path of internal spiritual and physical cultivation</term> becomes as clear as looking at the palm of one's hand.

It is said that the **Way of the Returned Elixir** <term>Huandan: The process of refining internal energies to return to a primordial, immortal state</term> is extremely simple and easy—it involves nothing more than looking within oneself. Yet, it is also said that one must wait through **three years and nine cycles** <term>Jiuzhuan: The traditional nine stages of alchemical refinement and transformation</term>. Thus, while it appears easy, it is actually difficult; it requires a practitioner to persist in their efforts for a long duration.

Therefore, those who study the Way and **aspire to immortality** <note>original: "Xixian"</note> cannot expect to grasp its depth or catch a glimpse of its truth in a mere moment. One must seek out its "doors and windows" and observe its "critical mechanism." By pumping the **bellows** <note>Tuoyue: A metaphor for rhythmic breathing and the creative void within the body</note>, advancing the **fire-phasing** <note>Huohou: The timing and intensity of meditative focus</note>, and guarding the **Dragon and Tiger** <note>Metaphors for the volatile internal energies of the spirit and the vital breath</note> to await their union, then the **Yellow Sprout** <term>Huangya: The first sign of the internal elixir beginning to grow</term> will naturally grow, and the **Jade Blossom** <term>Yuying: A poetic term for the manifestation of refined spiritual essence</term> will naturally appear. In this way, the Way of the Returned Elixir is nearly reached.

If one proceeds thus, they may achieve **ascension** <term>Chongju: The ultimate Daoist goal of rising into the heavens in broad daylight</term>, attain eternal life, and ride upon the drifting clouds to the eight [directions]...

<summary>The author explains that while the path to immortality is theoretically simple, it requires years of dedicated practice using specific internal techniques to cultivate the "elixir." He reconciles the text's philosophy with the Book of Changes and the secret teachings of Daoist immortals.</summary>

<keywords>Internal Alchemy, Nine Cycles, Immortality, Dragon and Tiger, Fire-phasing, Ascension, Yellow Sprout, Jade Blossom</keywords>

# Imperial Edition of the Complete Library in Four Sections

## Elucidation of the Kinship of the Three in the Book of Changes

->Page 4<-

...between the highest extremes, braving the **Fierce Winds** <term>Gangfeng: The powerful, sharp winds found in the highest reaches of the atmosphere in Daoist cosmology, which only the perfected can withstand</term> above the **Nine Heavens** <term>Jiuxiao: The nine celestial layers or the highest heights of heaven</term>. There, one is utterly separated from the mundane world, dwelling transcendently in the **Divine Continent** <note>original: "Shenzhou," a mythical land of the blessed or a poetic name for the sacred realm</note> and forever ascended to the **Immortal Throne** <term>Xianzu: The sovereign rank or station achieved by those who have attained immortality</term>. 

Only once every hundred generations does such a being descend to wander among the human realm, attaining long life and delivering others from their suffering; yet, no one can fathom their whereabouts or track their movements. From that height, they gaze down upon this dusty world, where human lives are like **mayflies** <note>original: "fuyou"</note> that live only from dawn to dusk, or like ants and butterflies rising and falling in their brief cycles. To the eyes of an immortal, the frantic activity of the world is truly not even worth a single laugh.

<summary>This page describes the transcendent state of an immortal who has ascended above the mundane world to the Nine Heavens, viewing human life as brief and insignificant as that of a mayfly.</summary>

<keywords>immortality, Nine Heavens, Fierce Winds, Divine Continent, transcendence, human condition, fuyou (mayfly)</keywords>

<meta>Following the previous discussion of the immortal realm and the insignificance of mortal life, this page transitions into the formal title for the commentary section that follows.</meta>

# **Preface to the Elucidation of the Kinship of the Three in the Book of Changes**

<note>original: "Zhouyi Cantong Qi Fahui Xu"</note>

# **By Imperial Decree: The Complete Library of the Four Treasuries** <note>original: "Qinding Siku Quanshu." This indicates the text was part of the massive 18th-century literary collection commissioned by the Qianlong Emperor of the Qing Dynasty.</note>
## **Elucidation of the Kinship of the Three in the Book of Changes, Volume One**
### **Written by Yu Yan of the Song Dynasty** <note>Yu Yan (1258–1314) was a scholar who specialized in interpreting alchemical texts through the lens of the I Ching (Book of Changes).</note>

->Upper Section<-

**Heaven** <term>Qian: The first hexagram, representing pure Yang, the creative principle, and the sky</term> and **Earth** <term>Kun: The second hexagram, representing pure Yin, the receptive principle, and the soil</term> are the gateway to the *Changes* and the parents of all the hexagrams.

Closing the door is called **Earth**; opening the door is called **Heaven**. One closing and one opening is called "change." Moving back and forth without end is called "continuity" <note>This passage paraphrases the "Appended Explanations" of the I Ching, describing the rhythmic oscillation of the universe as a cosmic door</note>. This is the reason why **Heaven** and **Earth** act as the gateway to the *Changes*. **Heaven** produces three sons and **Earth** produces three daughters <note>This refers to the derivation of the other six basic trigrams from the primary parents, Heaven and Earth</note>. When the male and female combine their essences, the myriad things are transformed and born. This is why **Heaven** and **Earth** are the parents of all...

<summary>This page marks the beginning of the actual commentary, establishing the hexagrams of Qian (Heaven) and Kun (Earth) as the fundamental origins of all cosmic movement and the other sixty-two hexagrams.</summary>
<keywords>Qian, Kun, Heaven and Earth, Yin and Yang, hexagrams, Book of Changes, Yu Yan, Siku Quanshu</keywords>

# **Oral Instructions on the Golden Truth, Volume One**

...are the parents. Indeed, a person’s body **models Heaven and mirrors Earth**; it shares the same **Yin** <term>The passive, receptive, cool, and feminine principle</term> and **Yang** <term>The active, creative, warm, and masculine principle</term> as the universe itself. 

If a person understands that this body shares the same **Yin** and **Yang** as Heaven and Earth, only then can they begin to discuss the Way of the **Restored Elixir** <term>Huandan: A central concept in internal alchemy referring to the refinement of internal energies to return to a state of primordial wholeness</term>.

### **Kan and Li as the Framework; Rotating the Hub and Aligning the Axle**

**Kan** <term>The hexagram representing Water and the Moon</term> is the Moon; **Li** <term>The hexagram representing Fire and the Sun</term> is the Sun. The Sun and Moon travel along the **Ecliptic** <note>original: "Huangdao," the path the sun appears to take across the sky</note>, coming and going through day and night in a cycle without end. 

This movement is like the surrounding walls of a city. The **hub** <note>original: "Gu," the center of a wheel</note> is like the body; the **axle** <note>original: "Zhou"</note> is like the heart. If you wish for the hub to rotate, you must first ensure the axle is straight and true. Those who cultivate the **Restored Elixir** rotate the Sun and Moon within their own bodies to travel the same path as the very creation of Heaven and Earth. If one does not first rectify their own heart, how could this possibly be achieved?

### **The Four Hexagrams of Male and Female as the Bellows; Governing the Way of Yin and Yang like a Charioteer Holding the Reins**

| Term | Explanation |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Restored Elixir** (Huandan) | The alchemical goal of returning energy to its source. |
| **Kan and Li** | The cosmic symbols of Water/Moon and Fire/Sun used in internal refinement. |
| **Yin and Yang** | The fundamental dual forces of the universe. |
| **Bellows and Pipes** (Tuoyue) | A metaphor for the rhythmic breathing and emptiness of the Dao. |
| **Male and Female** (Pinmu) | References to the hexagrams representing the generative forces. |
| **Framework** (Kuanguo) | The external structure or container of the alchemical process. |
| **Hub and Axle** | Metaphors for the body and the central, directing mind/heart. |
| **Ecliptic** (Huangdao) | The celestial path of the Sun, mirrored in the body's energy channels. |
| **Oral Instructions on the Golden Truth** | The title of this specific commentary by Yu Yan. |
| **Kinship of the Three** (Cantong Qi) | The foundational text of Taoist alchemy that this work explains. |

### **The Regulation of the Work and the Cycle of Hexagrams**

...ng the reins. There are plumb-lines and standards, rules and squares; one must follow the established tracks and ruts. Position yourself in the center to control what is outside. The calculations reside in the **laws of the calendar** <term>Lüli: The ancient Chinese system of mathematical astronomy used to harmonize the movements of the sun and moon</term>, recording the months and seasonal nodes. 

There are "five and six" <note>This refers to the five elements and six climatic factors, which together structure the cycles of time</note> acting as the warp and woof; these serve the Sun’s mandate, combining to form the **sixty hexagrams** <note>In this system, 60 of the 64 hexagrams of the I Ching are assigned to specific times of the month to guide the "firing process" of alchemy</note>. 

The **Hard** <term>Yang</term> and **Soft** <term>Yin</term> have an inner and outer relationship. At the dawn of the new moon, the hexagram **Tun** <term>Hexagram 3: Sprouting or Initial Difficulty</term> takes charge of affairs; by evening, **Meng** <term>Hexagram 4: Youthful Folly</term> must take its turn. Use one hexagram each for day and night, following the proper sequence. From the final hexagrams **Jiji** <term>Hexagram 63: Already Fulfilled</term> and **Weiji** <term>Hexagram 64: Not Yet Fulfilled</term> until the first light of dawn, when the cycle reaches its end, it begins all over again.

### **The Four Primary Hexagrams as the Bellows**

**Qian** <term>The Creative/Heaven</term> is the hexagram of pure Yang and represents the male; **Kun** <term>The Receptive/Earth</term> is the hexagram of pure Yin and represents the female. **Kan** <term>The Abysmal/Water</term> contains a spark of Yang within Yin; **Li** <term>The Clinging/Fire</term> contains a spark of Yin within Yang. 

These are the hexagrams of the "intercourse between male and female" <note>A metaphor for the blending of opposing internal energies to create the Elixir</note>. In the method of the **Elixir** <term>Danfa</term>, we position **Qian** and **Kun** at the top and bottom <note>Representing the head and the lower abdomen, or the vertical axis of the body</note>, while arranging **Kan** and **Li** to the East and West. 

The opening and closing of **Qian** and **Kun**, and the coming and going of **Kan** and **Li**, are exactly like the movement of a **bellows** <term>Tuoyue</term>. Therefore it is said: these four hexagrams of male and female serve as the bellows. A bellows is a hollow vessel; the **tuo** <note>original: "橐"</note> is the leather bag or sheath.

# **Imperial Edition of the Complete Library in Four Sections**

## **Volume One**

The **flute** <note>original: "籥" (yue). While often translated as a flute, in this context it refers to the pipe or nozzle of the bellows mechanism.</note> is its tube. The *Laozi Daodejing* says: "The space between Heaven and Earth, is it not like a **bellows** <term>Tuoyue: An ancient Chinese lung-like device used to blow air into a furnace; a central metaphor in Daoism for the emptiness and productivity of the universe</term>? It is empty yet inexhaustible; the more it moves, the more it yields." 

The vast, silent Great Void is like the physical body of the bellows, while the opening and closing, the coming and going of the **Breath** <term>Qi</term>, is like the bellows' function. If you understand this, then more than half of the **Way of the Reverted Elixir** <term>Huandan: The alchemical process of refining internal energies to return them to their original, primordial state</term> has been grasped. 

However, regarding this Way: "Hold it and it persists; let it go and it vanishes." Therefore, you must be cautious with what is inside and close off what is outside. It must be like the chariot driving of **Master Taidou** <note>A legendary master charioteer mentioned in the Daoist classic *Liezi*</note> as described in the *Liezi* (also known as the *True Classic of Perfect Virtue from the Empty Realm*). Only then can one engage in this work. 

The driving of Master Taidou is characterized thus: he achieved a perfect harmony between the reins and the bit, and balanced the pressure on the horse’s mouth. He maintained a correct posture within his chest and held the rhythm firmly within the palm of his hands. What he attained internally in his heart was aligned externally with the horse's will. For this reason, he could advance or retreat as straight as a **plumb-line** <note>A tool used to ensure perfect vertical or horizontal alignment</note>, and navigate curves and circles...

<summary>This page employs the famous Daoist metaphor of the bellows to explain the "breathing" or rhythmic energy work of alchemy, emphasizing that success depends on internal control and focus, much like a master charioteer.</summary>
<keywords>Bellows (Tuoyue), Daodejing, Reverted Elixir (Huandan), Liezi, Master Taidou, internal alchemy, Qi regulation</keywords>

# **Elucidations on the *Tripartite Unity* of the *Zhou Changes***

The method of **refining the elixir** <term>Liandan: The practice of internal or external alchemy to achieve spiritual transformation or longevity</term> according to the proper rules and standards is exactly like this. The numerical patterns found in the **Musical Standards, Calendars, and Chronicles** <note>original: "律歷紀" (Lü Li Ji). These represent the three pillars of ancient Chinese temporal science: music (harmonics), the calendar (seasonal cycles), and the chronicles (long-term astronomical cycles).</note> involve circulating through the twelve positions. These correspond to the twelve tones of the musical pitch-pipes, the twelve months of the year, and the twelve years of the Jovian cycle <note>The 12-year cycle of the planet Jupiter</note>. 

The reference to the month having "five and six" segments means dividing the thirty days of a month into six periods <note>of five days each</note>. The **warp** <term>Jing: The vertical threads in a loom, representing the fixed axis or "longitude"</term> refers to the fixed positions of **Qian** (Heaven) in the South and **Kun** (Earth) in the North. The **woof** <term>Wei: The horizontal threads, representing movement, function, or "latitude"</term> refers to the wondrous application of **Kan** (Water) in the West and **Li** (Fire) in the East. 

To "serve as the sun’s envoy" means to be directed by the movements of the Sun. This timing moves in unison with the heavens, rising and sinking along with the sun without the slightest deviation. 

The total count is combined to make sixty. The *Book of Changes* contains sixty-four hexagrams; if one sets aside the four primary hexagrams of **Qian, Kun, Kan, and Li**, there are exactly sixty hexagrams remaining. In the twelve double-hours <note>original: "時" (Shi). Each "hour" in the ancient Chinese system is equal to two modern hours</note> of a single day, two hexagrams are used to govern the work. Since each hexagram has six **lines** <term>Yao: The individual horizontal lines that comprise a hexagram</term>, two hexagrams together total twelve lines. Each line thus corresponds to one double-hour. This matches the numerical cycles of the musical standards, calendars, and chronicles when summed up over the thirty days of a month <note>30 days multiplied by 2 hexagrams per day equals the 60 functional hexagrams</note>.

<summary>This page explains the mathematical and astronomical timing of the alchemical process, aligning the sixty functional hexagrams of the I Ching with the days of the month and the hours of the day. It establishes the cosmological "warp and woof" using the four primary hexagrams to guide the practitioner's internal work in harmony with the natural cycles of the sun and stars.</summary>

<keywords>Cantong Qi, Internal Alchemy, I Ching Hexagrams, Qian and Kun, Kan and Li, Twelve Double-hours, Cosmological Timing, Refined Elixir</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of "fire-timing" (Huohou). Having established the cosmological axes of Qian (Heaven) and Kun (Earth) on the previous page, the text now explains how the remaining sixty hexagrams of the I Ching are mapped onto the lunar month, while simultaneously warning the practitioner not to take these mathematical models too literally.</meta>

### **Imperial Edition of the Complete Library of the Four Treasuries**
**Volume I**

Together, there are sixty hexagrams. By distributing these sixty hexagrams across the thirty days of a single month, then on the **first day of the new moon** <term>Shuo: The first day of the lunar month when the moon is invisible</term>, the morning is represented by **Tun** <note>Hexagram 3: Difficulty at the Beginning</note> and the evening by **Meng** <note>Hexagram 4: Youthful Folly</note>. The twelve double-hours of a full day and night correspond exactly to the twelve lines <term>Yao: The individual horizontal lines that make up a hexagram; each hexagram has six</term> found within these two hexagrams. 

From this point forward, the morning is **Xu** <note>Hexagram 5: Waiting</note> and the evening is **Song** <note>Hexagram 6: Conflict</note>; then morning is **Shi** <note>Hexagram 7: The Army</note> and evening is **Bi** <note>Hexagram 8: Union</note>. They are used in this specific sequence until the **final day of the month** <term>Hui: The last day of the lunar cycle</term>, when the hexagrams **Ji Ji** <note>Hexagram 63: After Completion</note> and **Wei Ji** <note>Hexagram 64: Before Completion</note> are used. This cycle between the end and the beginning of the month revolves and starts again.

During this process, one must not be stubbornly attached to the specific structures of the hexagram images—whether they are "inner" or "outer," "firm" or "yielding," or whether they appear "inverted" or "opposed." As Zhang Ziyang <note>Zhang Boduan (987–1082), a titan of internal alchemy and author of the *Wuzhen pian*</note> says in his *Awakening to Reality*:

> "Once you have grasped the meaning, stop seeking the images; if you scrutinize every line, you will only exhaust your spirit in vain." <note>original: "此中得意休求象，若究羣爻謾役情" (Ci zhong de yi xiu qiu xiang, ruo jiu qun yao man yi qing)</note>

For the true intent is not to literally spread sixty hexagrams across the thirty days of a month. Some people fail to realize this principle and cling stubbornly to the theory of hexagram images. They claim that using two specific hours a day constitutes the **fire-timing** <term>Huohou: The metaphorical 'heat' or intensity of meditative focus and breath work</term>, and they use minute measurements and the individual lines of the hexagrams to strictly calculate and predict their progress.

<summary>This page explains how the sixty hexagrams of the I Ching are symbolically mapped onto the thirty days of the lunar month to guide alchemical practice, while warning practitioners that these are metaphorical aids rather than rigid schedules to be followed to the letter.</summary>
<keywords>sixty hexagrams, lunar cycle, fire-timing (Huohou), Zhang Ziyang, Awakening to Reality (Wuzhen pian), Tun and Meng hexagrams, internal alchemy, symbolic time</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of "fire-timing" (huohou). The previous page concluded by warning the practitioner not to be obsessed with the literal hexagram images. This page deepens that warning, cautioning against the use of physical clocks and emphasizing that the true "timing" occurs within the practitioner’s own body by mirroring celestial cycles.</meta>

# **Exposition of the Hidden Meaning of the Token for the Agreement of the Three**
## **Page 4**

Is it not a great error to anxiously follow the **timing of the work** <term>Qihou: The stages of internal energetic circulation, often called "fire-timing"</term> by staring at a **sundial** <term>Tugui: An ancient instrument for measuring the sun’s shadow to determine the season and time</term> by day and watching a **water clock** <term>Kelou: A clepsydra used to measure time intervals at night</term> by night?

->**The sun and stars provide the measure of time; movement and stillness have their own dawn and dusk.**<-

The **Cuixu Pian** <note>The "Chapter of Emerald Emptiness," a work by the Daoist master Chen Nan</note> by **Chen Niwan** <note>Chen Nan (died 1213), a significant patriarch of the Southern Lineage of internal alchemy, known by the sobriquet "Muddy Ball" (Niwan)</note> states: "In the heavens, there are clearly twelve signs; people divide these to serve as the schedule for refining the elixir." This is what is meant by "the sun and stars providing the measure of time."

The "sun" represents the **fire** <note>The active, yang energy used in the internal transformation</note>, while the "stars" represent the **timing**. If a person can take the sun and stars of the heavens and seek them instead within their own body, then the position where the internal sun resides is itself the "double-hour" <note>Shi-chen: The traditional Chinese unit of time equal to two hours</note>.

The **Wuzhen Pian** <note>"Understanding Reality," the seminal 11th-century text on internal alchemy mentioned on the previous page</note> says: "To refine the medicine, one must know the dusk and the dawn." This is what is meant by "movement and stillness have their own dawn and dusk." 

"Movement" refers to the sun rising and beginning its work; "stillness" refers to the sun setting and going to rest. Within one's own body, there is naturally a dawn and a dusk, a sunrise and a sunset. The movement and stillness of one's internal **fire-timing** <term>Huohou: The precise control of internal heat and energy during meditation</term> correspond, one by one, to the celestial measures of the heavens.

<summary>This page warns practitioners against using external clocks to track their internal alchemical progress, arguing instead that the human body contains its own internal "sun" and "timing" that naturally mirror the movements of the cosmos.</summary>
<keywords>fire-timing (huohou), Chen Niwan, Cuixu Pian, internal cosmology, Wuzhen Pian, solar/lunar cycles, sundial, water clock</keywords>

# **Imperial Edition of the Complete Library in Four Sections, Volume One**

Is it not that one utilizes the **Pivot of Heaven** <term>Tianshu: The celestial axis or North Star; in alchemy, it represents the central, unmoving point of the practitioner's consciousness</term> and the **tidal cycles** <term>Chaohou: The rhythmic rising and falling of energy, compared to the tides of the sea</term> as the timing for the application of the work?

->**In Spring and Summer, rely on the inner body, from the hours of the Rat to the Dragon and Snake.**<-
->**In Autumn and Winter, manage the outer application, from the hours of the Horse until the Dog and Boar.**<-

A hexagram has six lines: the bottom three lines constitute the "inner" and the top three lines constitute the "outer." Let us illustrate this using the two hexagrams **Tun** <note>Hexagram 3, "Difficulty at the Beginning," symbolizing the initial push of growth</note> and **Meng** <note>Hexagram 4, "Youthful Folly" or "Enlightenment," symbolizing the cooling and stabilizing of energy</note> to make it clear. 

In the morning, one uses the hexagram *Tun*, which represents the stage where the **Yang Fire** <term>Yanghuo: The active, warming phase of internal alchemy where energy is circulated upward</term> rises. The "First Nine" <note>The first or bottom-most yang line of the hexagram</note> of *Tun* corresponds exactly to the **Rat** <note>Zi: The first Earthly Branch, corresponding to midnight (11 PM – 1 AM) and the North, representing the first stirrings of Yang energy</note> within the body. 

Movement proceeds from the inner to the outer; therefore, it is said: "In Spring and Summer, rely on the inner body, from the hours of the **Rat** <note>Zi</note> to the **Dragon** <note>Chen: 7 AM – 9 AM</note> and **Snake** <note>Si: 9 AM – 11 AM</note>." 

In the evening, one uses the hexagram *Meng*, which represents the stage where the **Yin Tally** <term>Yinfu: The receptive, cooling phase of internal alchemy where energy is brought back down to the center</term> descends. The "Top Nine" <note>The final or highest line of the hexagram</note> of *Meng* corresponds exactly to the **Horse** <note>Wu: The seventh Earthly Branch, corresponding to noon (11 AM – 1 PM) and the South, representing the peak of Yang and the beginning of Yin</note> within the body. 

Movement proceeds from the outer to the inner; therefore, it is said: "In Autumn and Winter, manage the outer application, from the hours of the **Horse** <note>Wu</note> until the **Dog** <note>Xu: 7 PM – 9 PM</note> and **Boar** <note>Hai: 9 PM – 11 PM</note>." 

However, the so-called "Spring, Summer, Autumn, and Winter," "Rat, Horse, Snake, and Boar," along with the theories of "Inner Body" and "Outer Application," are all **metaphors**. They are...

<summary>This page explains the metaphorical use of the I Ching hexagrams Tun and Meng to describe the internal circulation of energy (Yang Fire and Yin Tally). It emphasizes that practitioners must time their internal work according to these symbolic "seasons" and "hours" within the body rather than literal time.</summary>
<keywords>Pivot of Heaven, Fire-timing, Hexagram Tun, Hexagram Meng, Yang Fire, Yin Tally, Earthly Branches, Internal Alchemy metaphors</keywords>

# **Elucidation of the Seal of the Unity of the Three**

### **Volume One**

If one stubbornly clings to imagery and literal words to practice the "energy of the hexagrams," then one is merely treating **Heaven and Earth** <term>Qian and Kun: The primal creative and receptive forces; in this context, the practitioner's head and abdomen</term> as a physical **cauldron and vessel** <term>Dingqi: The spiritual and physical containers used in alchemical transformation</term>, and attempting to mold the **Crow and the Rabbit** <term>original: "Wutu"; symbols for the Sun and Moon, representing the refined essences of Yin and Yang</term> into medicinal ingredients for cooking. But how could this possibly refer to a Heaven and Earth that exist outside the body, or a Crow and Rabbit that exist outside the body?

The student must seek these things within their own body. Consider the cosmos: Heaven is positioned above and Earth is positioned below. The two energies circulate within them, one rising and one falling, coming and going in an endless cycle, much like a **bellows** <term>original: "Tuoyue"; an ancient forge blower used here as a metaphor for the rhythmic, creative movement of the breath and the void</term>.

Human beings receive the **energy of harmonious vacuity** <note>original: "Chonghe zhi qi"; the balanced, vital breath that arises from the union of Yin and Yang</note> and are born between Heaven and Earth; from the very beginning, we are of one substance with the universe. If one can realize the wonder of the "Bellows of Heaven and Earth," the mind will naturally become harmonious, empty, profound, and silent. In this state, the **Unified Energy** <note>The primordial Qi</note> naturally circulates through the upper and lower regions of the body. 

When the bellows opens, the energy flows out; when it closes, the energy flows in. The outgoing energy is like the vapor of the Earth rising upward; the incoming energy is like the breath of Heaven descending. By following this rhythm, one can naturally achieve longevity equal to that of Heaven and Earth themselves. As the Master **Zihua Zi** <note>A Daoist philosopher whose writings emphasize the harmony between the internal body and the cosmic order</note> says:

<summary>This page argues that the tools and ingredients of alchemy—the cauldron, the sun, and the moon—are metaphors for internal processes rather than external objects. It describes the human body and breath as a cosmic bellows that, when synchronized with the natural rising and falling of energy, allows the practitioner to achieve spiritual longevity.</summary>
<keywords>Internal Alchemy (Neidan), Cauldron and Vessel, Sun and Moon (Crow and Rabbit), Bellows (Tuoyue), Qi circulation, Zihua Zi, Microcosm, Macrocosm</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the technical requirements of the alchemical vessel discussed on the previous page to the internal state and spiritual discipline required of the practitioner. It emphasizes that true longevity is found in the internal circulation of Qi rather than external shortcuts or literal interpretations of "cooking" elixir ingredients.</meta>

# **Imperial Edition of the Complete Library of the Four Treasuries**

### **Volume One**

Those of old who knew the Way built ramparts to ward off evil and cleared the springs to nurture the Truth. They dwelt deep in quietude, refusing to be disturbed by external things; in every movement and rest, in every breath that entered or exited, they remained at one with the Spirit and the Qi. 

The **ethereal and corporeal souls** <term>Hun and Po: The two aspects of the human soul in Daoist thought; Hun is the spiritual/celestial soul, while Po is the physical/earthly soul</term> maintained a state of disciplined caution. They "closed the opening" <note>original: "shishi qidui"; referring to the Hexagram Dui, which represents the mouth or the senses. To "house the opening" means to withdraw from sensory distractions</note> with singular focus. When the mind is undivided, the **True Qi** <term>Zhenqi: The primordial life-force or vital energy inherent in the human body</term> is preserved. It pours through the body from top to bottom, and the Qi circulates freely, like the flowing of water or the unceasing movement of the sun and moon. 

Yin nurtures the hidden stores, and Yang fortifies the palaces. The source-flow is clear and bright; it is full without overflowing, and though it is hollow and surging, it never exceeds its bounds. This is what is called "long life." To abandon this path and claim that one possesses a "shortcut to the clouds" <note>A metaphorical reference to seeking immediate or magical transcendence without internal cultivation</note> is merely to follow a side-path or crooked learning; it is not the Way of our **Great Elixir** <term>Dadan: The "Golden Elixir" of internal alchemy, representing the perfected state of the soul and body</term>.

Heaven and Earth are the images of **Qian and Kun** <term>Qian and Kun: The two foundational hexagrams of the I Ching, representing pure Yang/Heaven and pure Yin/Earth</term>. "Establishing the positions" refers to the arrangement of the positions where Yin and Yang correspond. The *Book of Changes* speaks of **Kan and Li** <term>Kan and Li: The hexagrams for Water and Fire; in internal alchemy, they represent the "functional" aspects of Yin and Yang that must be harmonized</term>. Kan and Li are the two "functions" of Qian and Kun. These two functions have no fixed positions within the hexagram lines; they circulate throughout the **Six Voids** <note>original: "liuxu"; refers to the six positions of a hexagram or, more broadly, the spatial dimensions of the universe and the body</note>, coming and going in a constant cycle.

<summary>This page describes the internal discipline of the ancient Daoist masters, emphasizing the circulation of True Qi over external methods. It defines the cosmological hexagrams Qian, Kun, Kan, and Li as internal functions rather than external symbols.</summary>
<keywords>Daoism, internal alchemy, Qi circulation, Hun and Po, Qian and Kun, Kan and Li, Great Elixir, I Ching symbolism</keywords>

# **The Seal of the Unity of the Three, Volume One**

### **Elucidation of the Seal of the Unity of the Three**

->Neither fixed in the upper nor lower parts, nor staying in one constant state.<-

**Qian** <term>The trigram representing Heaven, pure Yang, and the creative principle</term> acts as Heaven, and **Kun** <term>The trigram representing Earth, pure Yin, and the receptive principle</term> acts as Earth; these are the **tripod and vessel** <term>Ding-qi: The metaphorical alchemical cauldron or laboratory established within the practitioner's own body</term> of my body. **Li** <term>The trigram representing Fire and the Sun</term> acts as the Sun, and **Kan** <term>The trigram representing Water and the Moon</term> acts as the Moon; these are the **medicinal ingredients** <term>Yaowu: The internal energies—essence, breath, and spirit—that are "refined" during meditation</term> of my body. 

In the **Pre-heaven arrangement of the Eight Trigrams** <note>original: "Xiantian Bagua"; an arrangement of symbols attributed to the mythical sage Fuxi, representing the primordial, balanced state of the universe before the world of shadows and change began</note>, Qian is positioned in the South and Kun in the North; Li is in the East and Kan is in the West. 

The North-South axis aligns the positions where Heaven and Earth join in cooperation; the East-West axis divides the gates through which the Sun and Moon exit and enter. If one looks inward to seek this within their own body, the principle is exactly the same. 

Qian and Kun are the **substance**; Kan and Li are the **function** <note>original: "ti" and "yong." This is a fundamental concept in Chinese philosophy: "Ti" refers to the latent, structural reality, while "Yong" refers to its active movement and application</note>. These two, Kan and Li, circulate, rising and falling through the **Six Voids** <term>Liuxu: A term from the Book of Changes referring to the six empty spaces in a hexagram, here representing the hollow, internal spaces of the human body through which energy flows</term>. They move back and forth, up and down, without being restricted to fixed **line-positions** <term>Yaowei: The specific positions of the six lines that make up a hexagram, used here to suggest that the internal energy is fluid and not static</term>. 

The Kan and Li of my own body operate within the "tripod and vessel." Submerged in the realm of "Heaven" they become Heaven; submerged in the realm of "Earth" they become Earth. How could they be confined to fixed positions? 

Deeply hidden and submerged, they sink and vanish, undergoing transformations within.

<summary>This page establishes the macrocosm-microcosm correspondence, mapping the trigrams of the I Ching onto the human body to define the alchemical vessel and its internal energies. It emphasizes that these internal energies (Kan and Li) are fluid and constantly transforming rather than fixed in place.</summary>
<keywords>Qian and Kun, Kan and Li, Internal Alchemy, Pre-heaven Bagua, Tripod and Vessel, Microcosm, Substance and Function</keywords>

# **By Imperial Order: Complete Library of the Four Treasuries**
## **Volume One**

**"Hidden and submerged"** refers to the Spirit entering the Breath, and the Breath entering the navel, sinking down until it returns to the **Bottom of the Sea** <term>Haidi: The lowest point of the torso’s central channel, often identified with the perineum or the lower reaches of the "Cinnabar Field" where energy is stored</term> and coming to rest.

**"Transformation within"** means that when the proper time arrives, the Breath transforms spontaneously; when stillness reaches its peak, the internal mechanism triggers itself, bringing forth the **rising Sun** <note>This refers to the "Birth of the Elixir," a moment of spiritual illumination and the generation of internal heat, likened to the sun rising over the horizon.</note>. Master **Chen Xiyi** <note>Chen Tuan (c. 871–989 CE), a legendary Daoist sage credited with developing influential cosmological diagrams and internal alchemy techniques.</note> says in his *Poem on Pointing to the Mystery*: "Vanishing without a trace, it returns to the **Dark Warrior** <note>original: "Xuanwu"; the mythical turtle-and-snake of the North, symbolizing the kidneys or the element of water in the body’s alchemical landscape.</note>; hidden within is the mechanism for forming the **Sacred Embryo** <term>Shengtai: The spiritual 'child' or refined essence born from the union of internal energies, representing the practitioner's new, immortal self.</term>" This is exactly what is meant here.

### **It wraps the ten thousand things, acting as the nexus and discipline of the Way.**

The *Chapters on Awakening to the Truth* <note>original: "Wuzhen pian"; one of the most influential texts of the Southern School of Daoism, written by Zhang Boduan in the 11th century.</note> says: "The Way arises from nothingness to produce the One Breath; from that One Breath, Yin and Yang are born. Yin and Yang then combine to produce the Three Bodies; from the Three, the ten thousand things expand." 

The **Mother of the Golden Elixir** is nothing more than this **Primordial One Breath** <term>Xiantian Yiqi: The pure, undifferentiated energy that exists before the physical world takes shape; it is the "raw material" for spiritual perfection.</term> It splits into two, divides into three, and scatters into the ten thousand—it all stems from this single source.

...coming from within the **One Breath** <term>Yiqi: The undifferentiated, primordial energy from which the universe arises and into which the practitioner seeks to return.</term>, it is therefore said to **"enwrap the ten thousand things, acting as the nexus and discipline of the Way."**

### **Using "Nothingness" to govern "Somethingness"; that which makes a vessel useful is its emptiness.**

The *Daodejing* <note>The "Classic of the Way and Virtue," the foundational text of Daoism attributed to the sage Laozi (c. 6th century BCE).</note> says:
"Thirty spokes share one hub; it is because of its **nothingness** <note>original: "wu"; the void or non-being at the center of the hub where the axle fits.</note> that the chariot is useful. Knead clay to make a vessel; it is because of its **nothingness** <note>the hollow space within the pot</note> that the vessel is useful. Cut out doors and windows to make a room; it is because of its **nothingness** <note>the empty space that allows for habitation</note> that the room is useful. Therefore, what is **present** <note>original: "you"; the material form</note> provides the benefit, but what is **absent** <note>original: "wu"; the void</note> provides the utility."

The commentary by **Lin Quzhai** <note>Lin Xi, a Song Dynasty scholar known for his "Humble Studio" (Quzhai) commentaries which emphasize the practical application of Daoist philosophy.</note> states:
The "hub" is the part of the chariot that receives the axle. The "spokes" are the legs of the wheel. The hub is only able to move the chariot because it is empty in the center. "Kneading clay" <term>Shanzhi: A classical term for the potter's work of tempering and shaping earth into form.</term> is the tool of the potter; because it is empty and round, it can become a functional vessel. "Doors and windows" are the passages for light and air within a room. These three examples are all metaphors for how emptiness functions. Thus, it is said that "what is present..." <note>The text cuts off here at the bottom of the page, trailing into the conclusion of the quote from the Daodejing: "...provides the benefit, but what is absent provides the utility."</note>

<summary>This page explains the Daoist principle of Emptiness (Wu) by quoting the Daodejing, arguing that the "One Breath" governs the world through the utility of the void. It uses the metaphors of the wheel hub, the clay pot, and the room to show that the empty space is what allows a form to be useful.</summary>

Providing benefit is the profit of the **existent** <note>original: "you"; material form or "somethingness"</note>, but that which makes them a chariot, a vessel, or a room is entirely the utility of the **void within** <note>original: "xu zhong"; the empty center</note>. Now, **Lord Wei** <note>Wei Boyang, the 2nd-century "Father of Alchemy" and author of the *Cantong qi*.</note> says that by using **nothingness** <note>original: "wu"</note> to govern **somethingness**, the utility of the vessel is its emptiness. 

This truly applies the teaching of **Laozi** <note>The legendary sage and author of the *Daodejing*.</note>. In the **Methods of Alchemy** <term>Danfa: The practice of internal alchemy (Neidan) which seeks to refine the body's internal energies to achieve longevity or spiritual transcendence.</term>, the **Great Void** <term>Taixu: The primordial, undifferentiated state of the universe; in alchemy, it refers to a state of mental emptiness required for practice.</term> is taken as the **Cauldron and Furnace** <note>original: "Dinglu"; while these are physical tools in external alchemy, here they represent the meditative space or the lower abdomen (dantian) where the internal transformation occurs.</note>. Within the Great Void, there is naturally a miraculous function. How wondrous! How wondrous! 

**Guanyinzi** <note>A Daoist text attributed to the Guardian of the Pass who received the *Daodejing* from Laozi; its philosophy emphasizes the fluidity of reality.</note> says: "Shaking a garment in the void produces wind; breathing onto an object produces water." 

The commentary by **Chen Baoyi** <note>A Song Dynasty Daoist master known for his integrative commentaries on alchemical theory.</note> states: "If one knows that shaking the void produces wind, then by working one's own **Bellows of Purity** <term>Suyue: A metaphor from the *Daodejing* (Chapter 5) for the lungs and the rhythmic nature of breathing or the movement of primordial energy (Qi) within the body.</term>, one can generate **Qi** <note>Vital breath or energy.</note>. If one knows that breathing onto an object produces water, then by gestating one's own Qi, one can transform it into **Essence** <term>Jing: The fundamental physical energy or 'seed' of life, which alchemists seek to refine into Qi and eventually Spirit.</term>."

If one is ignorant of this and seeks the so-called "cauldron and vessel" elsewhere <note>i.e., by looking for physical laboratory equipment or external materials</note>, then it is not the **Way of Emptiness and Nothingness**.

Therefore, by inferring the **waxing and waning** <term>Xiaoxi: The natural cycles of growth and decay, represented by the changing lines of the *I Ching* hexagrams.</term> of **Kan and Li** <term>Kan and Li: The hexagrams representing Water and Fire. In internal alchemy, they symbolize the 'true yin' and 'true yang' within the body that must be balanced and merged.</term>, they disappear...

<summary>This page explains that alchemical transformation relies on "emptiness" (the Great Void) rather than physical tools, using the bellows and breath as metaphors for generating internal energy (Qi) and essence (Jing).</summary>

"Growth" <note>original: "xi"; refers to the waxing phase of energy or exhalation.</note> is the timing for **advancing the fire** <term>jinhuo: a technical term for increasing the meditative heat or intensity of practice during the first half of the cycle.</term>, where the **Kun** hexagram <note>representing pure Yin or Earth</note> transforms through three stages to become **Qian** <note>representing pure Yang or Heaven</note>. "Diminution" <note>original: "xiao"; refers to the waning phase of energy or inhalation.</note> is the timing for **retreating the charms** <term>tuifu: the period of cooling or stabilizing the internal elixir during the second half of the cycle.</term>, where the **Qian** hexagram transforms through three stages to become **Kun**. 

Now, from the dawn of the new moon when the **Zhen** hexagram <note>Thunder; representing the first stirring of Yang</note> takes command, passing through **Dui** <note>Lake</note> until reaching **Qian** at the full moon; and after the full moon when the **Xun** hexagram <note>Wind; representing the first appearance of Yin</note> takes command, passing through **艮** <note>Mountain</note> until reaching **Kun**—during this entire sequence, the positions of **Kan** and **Li** <note>Kan (Water) and Li (Fire) are the two central hexagrams that represent the "functional" core of the alchemical process.</note> are not seen to occupy a specific place in the sequence. Does this mean that **Kan** and **Li** have vanished?

The answer is: **Kan** and **Li** have not vanished. They move within the **Six Voids** <term>liuxu: the six lines or positions of any hexagram; in a cosmological sense, it refers to the six directions of the universe.</term> and their coming and going is not fixed. 

These words are not constructed recklessly, nor are these arguments born out of thin air. They are tested for effectiveness, measured against the divine light, and categorized to form a coherent system where original principles serve as evidence. 

**Kan** is the stem **Wu** <note>one of the ten heavenly stems associated with "Yang Earth"</note> and the essence of the Moon; **Li** is the stem **Ji** <note>associated with "Yin Earth"</note> and the light of the Sun. The Sun and Moon together constitute the **Change** <note>original: "Yi"; the character for "Change" (易) is traditionally explained as being composed of the characters for Sun (日) and Moon (月).</note>. Firmness and softness are properly balanced here. **Earth** <note>the element associated with the center and the stems Wu and Ji</note> rules over the four seasons, enmeshing the beginning and the end. The colors blue-green, red, white, and black each reside in their respective directions <note>representing East, South, West, and North</note>; yet all receive their nature from the **Central Palace** and the influence of the **Wu** and **Ji** stems.

<header>Merit</header>

# Merit #

**Kan** <note>The hexagram representing Water.</note> is Yin on the outside and Yang on the inside; it is the image of the **Moon**. **Li** <note>The hexagram representing Fire.</note> is Yang on the outside and Yin on the inside; it is the image of the **Sun**.

**Kan** receives the celestial stem **Wu**, which is **Yang Earth**. **Li** receives the celestial stem **Ji**, which is **Yin Earth**. When these two "Earths" merge and join, then **Yin and Yang** assist one another, and the **Firm and the Yielding** correspond to each other. This is just like how the two characters for "Sun" and "Moon" are joined together to form the character for **Change** <term>Yi (易): the title of the Book of Changes; alchemists noted that the character is composed of the symbols for Sun (日) and Moon (月).</term>.

Green, Red, White, and Black correspond to the elements of **Wood, Fire, Metal, and Water**. While Wood, Fire, Metal, and Water each occupy their own direction <note>East, South, West, and North, respectively.</note>, only **Earth** resides in the center. It distributes its flourishing power through the transitional periods of **Chen, Xu, Chou, and Wei** <note>These are the Earthly Branches corresponding to the final months of the four seasons.</note>, weaving together the beginning and end of the entire year. 

For this reason, Wood obtains it to flourish, Fire obtains it to rest, Metal obtains it to be born, and Water obtains it to cease. All four elements derive their **merit** <note>original: "gong" (功); refers to the functional power or achievement of an element.</note> from it. Yet, this Earth pervades the entire universe and has no fixed position of its own.

<summary>This page explains that the Kan and Li hexagrams represent the Moon and Sun, containing the 'True Earth' (Wu and Ji) which acts as the central axis for the other four elements. Earth is described as the foundational force that weaves the seasons together and allows the cycle of the Five Elements to function.</summary>

This may be called profound, thorough, and clearly illuminated. How could he have been willing to carelessly create something without...

"Change" <note>original: *Yi* (易). The author is defining the title of the *I Ching* (Book of Changes).</note> refers to images. ...they come along the Ecliptic <note>original: *huangdao* (黃道). In traditional Chinese astronomy, this is the path the sun appears to take through the stars.</note>. Entering and exiting, they alternately rise and fall, expanding and contracting. Day and night they cycle like the rotation of a wagon wheel, never ending. --- Page 35 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the numerical and structural correspondence between the I Ching and alchemical practice, specifically linking the number of hexagram lines to the weight of the alchemical elixir ingredients mentioned on page ten.</meta>

<margin>The Seal of the Unity of the Three based on the Book of Changes, Volume One</margin>

The complete *Book of Changes* <note>The *I Ching*.</note> consists of three hundred and eighty-four lines <note>original: *yao* (爻). Each of the 64 hexagrams contains six lines; 64 multiplied by 6 equals 384.</note>. A single pound <note>original: *jin* (斤)</note> of the Great Medicine consists of three hundred and eighty-four *zhu* <note>original: *zhu* (銖). In ancient Chinese metrology, 24 *zhu* equaled one tael, and 16 taels equaled one pound. 24 x 16 = 384. This numerical synchronicity is a cornerstone of the author's argument that the laws of the universe and the laws of alchemy are identical.</note>. This is the point of connection between the Way of Alchemy <note>original: *dandao* (丹道)</note> and the Way of the *I Ching* <note>original: *yidao* (易道)</note>, and it is the reason why *The Seal of the Unity of the Three* was composed.

"Lines" are the individual strokes of the hexagrams. "Tallies" <note>original: *fu* (符). Historically, tallies were objects split in two, used as proof of identity or authorization when the halves were reunited. Here, they symbolize the harmonious "fit" of cosmic cycles.</note> are the combined bodies of the hexagrams. 

The *I Ching* contains sixty-four hexagrams. In alchemical methods, we use *Qian* and *Kun* <note>The hexagrams for Pure Yang/Heaven and Pure Yin/Earth.</note> as the cauldron and vessel <note>original: *dingqi* (鼎器). The physical and spiritual "container" in which the internal elixir is cultivated.</note>. We use *Kan* and *Li* <note>The hexagrams for Water and Fire.</note> as the medicinal ingredients <note>original: *yaowu* (藥物). In internal alchemy, these represent the vital energies (Essence and Spirit) that must be balanced.</note>. We use the remaining sixty hexagrams to represent the fire phasing <note>original: *huohou* (火候). This refers to the specific timing and intensity of the meditative heat applied during the refinement process.</note>.

If we observe the system through *Qian* and *Kun*, it begins with the hexagrams *Tun* and *Meng* <note>Hexagrams 3 (Difficulty at the Beginning) and 4 (Youthful Folly), representing the initial stages of growth.</note> and ends with *Jiji* and *Weiji* <note>Hexagrams 63 (After Completion) and 64 (Before Completion), representing the end of a cycle and the start of a new one.</note>. In between, the inverted and opposing forms of the hexagrams all harmonize with one another without exception. 

Herein one sees the truth of the saying: "The succession of one Yin and one Yang is called the Way." <note>A famous quote from the *Xici* (Appended Explanations) of the *I Ching*.</note> The ancient immortals, relying on the text of the *I Ching*, extracted these "tallies" to serve as metaphors for the vessel, the ingredients, and the fire phasing. It can truly be called a system both ingenious and complete.

From the darkness of the month's end to the dawn of the new moon <note>original: *hui zhi shuo dan* (晦至朔旦). This refers to the transition between the end of the lunar cycle and the very beginning of the next.</note>, the *Zhen* trigram <note>original: *Zhen* (震), symbolizing "Thunder" or "The Arousing." In this context, it represents the first spark of Yang energy returning to the world.</note> arrives to receive the tally. At this specific moment, Heaven and Earth commingle their essence, and the Sun and Moon interact and reflect one another.

<summary>Yu Yan explains the mathematical and symbolic parallels between the 384 lines of the I Ching and the weights used in alchemy. He establishes the roles of specific hexagrams as the tools, ingredients, and timing for the internal alchemical process.</summary>

## Volume One: Annotated Chapters of the Seal of the Unity of the Three based on the Book of Changes

<margin>The Seal of the Unity of the Three based on the Book of Changes, Volume One</margin>

"From the last day of the lunar month to the dawn of the new moon" <note>original: *hui zhi shuo dan* (晦至朔旦). *Hui* is the 30th day (the dark moon) and *shuo* is the 1st day (the new moon). This refers to the silent, dark transition where new life begins.</note> refers to the interval between the dark moon and the new moon. "The Thunder hexagram comes to receive the tally" <note>original: *Zhen lai shou fu* (震來受符). In the *I Ching*, the *Zhen* hexagram represents Thunder and the first stage of returning Yang energy.</note> means that Heaven <term>Qian (乾): the hexagram of pure Yang, representing the creative power of the sky</term> interacts with Earth <term>Kun (坤): the hexagram of pure Yin, representing the receptive potential of the ground</term> to form the hexagram of Thunder. This symbolizes the birth of a single spark of Yang energy within the practitioner’s own body.

At this moment, Spirit <note>original: *shen* (神)</note> and Breath <note>original: *qi* (氣)</note> intersect; Breath and Spirit merge. It is like the union of the essences of Heaven and Earth, or the "joining of the jade discs" <note>A metaphor for a perfect celestial alignment.</note> of the Sun and Moon. Therefore, it is said: "At this time, Heaven and Earth combine their essence, and the Sun and Moon guide one another."

->Maintaining Heaven and Earth<- <note>The character "Maintain" (持) is elevated in the original text as a "lifted character" (抬頭) to show profound respect for the cosmic principles that follow.</note>

"Heaven and Earth" are the *Qian* and *Kun* within our own bodies. "Sun and Moon" are the *Kan* <note>The hexagram representing Water and the inner essence.</note> and *Li* <note>The hexagram representing Fire and the external spirit.</note> within our own bodies. Just as Heaven, Earth, the Sun, and the Moon intersect at specific times to mold the myriad things and complete the cycle of the seasons, so too do *Qian*, *Kun*, *Kan*, and *Li* within us intersect at the proper moments. By doing so, the practitioner can seize the power of Creation <term>zaohua (造化): the natural transformative power of the universe</term> and form the Sacred Embryo <note>original: *shengtai* (聖胎). In internal alchemy, this is the spiritual "seed" of enlightenment or immortality cultivated within the practitioner's "cauldron."</note>.
