The breath gathers the **True Lead** <term>True Lead<note>original: 真鉛 (zhenqian); a symbolic term for the primordial, pre-celestial energy that serves as the "seed" of the internal elixir.</note>. It is also said that **Essence and Qi** <note>original: 精氣 (jing qi); the fundamental physical and vital energies of the human body.</note> are the root, while the Spirit and the Divine reside together as one family. If one can only capture the **Five Thieves** <note>original: 五賊 (wuzei); a Daoist metaphor for the five senses or the five worldly desires that "steal" one's vital energy.</note>, one can naturally form the **Three Flowers** <term>Three Flowers<note>original: 三花 (sanhua); refers to the "Three Flowers Gathering at the Peak," representing the sublimation and unification of Essence, Qi, and Spirit at the crown of the head.</note>. During the time when one contains the inner light in silence, the key is to remain **"continuous, as if it exists"** <note>original: 綿綿若存 (mianmian ruocun); a famous phrase from the *Daodejing* describing the subtle, effortless nature of the Dao and correct meditative breathing.</note>. One must allow it to be natural and must not weary the Spirit.

### The Hall of Light Disseminates Governance: The Kingdom is Unharmed by the Way

The *Treatise on Celestial Offices* in the **Records of the Grand Historian** <note>original: 史記天官書 (Shiji Tianguan Shu); a foundational Chinese historical text that includes astronomical and astrological correlations.</note> says: "The Azure Dragon of the Eastern Palace contains the constellations *Fang* and *Xin*. *Xin* [the Heart] is the **Hall of Light** <term>Hall of Light<note>original: 明堂 (mingtang); historically the ritual palace where an Emperor conducted state business; in alchemy, it refers to a specific center in the brain, usually located behind the point between the eyebrows.</note>." In the human body, this corresponds to the Grotto Chamber, the Purple Ultimate, and the Gate of the Spirit.

The **Yellow Court Scripture** <note>original: 黃庭經 (Huangting Jing); a seminal text of internal alchemy focusing on the visualization of deities within the body's organs.</note> says: "Between the Hall of Light, the Golden Casket, and the Purple Chamber, the **Perfected One of Highest Purity** <note>original: 上清真人 (shangqing zhenren); a high-ranking celestial being or a state of perfected consciousness within the practitioner.</note> stands before me. The Yellow-clad Child and the Cinnabar Qi appear frequently; may I ask where they reside? At the point between the two eyebrows." Shedding light downward from this point is like a sovereign sitting in the Hall of Light, disseminating governance and opening the Way.

# Volume One

Returning to the origin and **turning back the light to illuminate the interior** <note>original: 回光内照 (huiguang neizhao); a central Daoist meditative practice where the practitioner redirects their attention and "vision" inward toward the internal landscape of the body.</note> is the great foundation of cultivation. Former Confucian scholars remarked that the Daoist method of nourishing life—withdrawing the vision and looking inward—is like gold and water hiding their radiance within. These words are truly sincere!

### Close the Openings and Fortify the Spiritual Trunk

**Dui** <term>Dui<note>original: 兌 (dui); the trigram in the *I Ching* representing the Lake, which in internal alchemy corresponds to the mouth and the sensory openings through which energy can leak.</note></term> refers to the mouth. The **Spiritual Trunk** <note>original: 靈株 (lingzhu)</note> refers to the **Kan Palace** <term>Kan Palace<note>original: 坎宫 (kangong); the region associated with the Kan trigram (Water), typically referring to the lower elixir field or the kidneys, where the generative force is stored.</note></term>. If one closes the "Dui" openings, then there is no leakage above. If one fortifies the "Spiritual Trunk," then there is no leaking below. 

The *Classic of Supreme Mystery* <note>original: 太玄經 (Taixuanjing) by the Han dynasty scholar Yang Xiong.</note> says: "Hide the heart in the abyss; beautiful is its spiritual root." If the spirit does not gallop outward, then the **harmonious qi** <note>the balanced, vital breath/energy of the body</note> will fill the entire being, and beauty will be found within.

### The Three Lights Sink into the Earth to Nurture the Pearl of the Son

If a person can gather the Three Lights and shine them back within, then—

# Volume One

### Looking but Not Seeing; It is Near and Easy to Seek

The Great Medicine of the **Golden Elixir** <term>Golden Elixir<note>original: 金丹 (jindan); the ultimate goal of Daoist alchemy, representing a perfected, immortal state of being attained through the refinement of internal energies.</note></term> is right before one’s eyes; is it not near? Yet why is it that one looks but does not see it, and reaches for it but cannot grasp it? <note>This echoes Chapter 14 of the *Dao De Jing*, describing the elusive nature of the Dao.</note> Though it is near, it seems far away. 

If one obtains the proper method to seek it, then in no more than a **brief moment** <note>original: 片餉 (pianxiang); literally the time it takes to eat a meal, implying a very short duration.</note> of effort, "yellow flowers" <note>original: 黃華 (huanghua); a metaphorical description of the golden light or visions that appear during deep meditation as the elixir begins to form.</note> will fill the vision and reveal themselves. How could it be considered far? 

The *Chapters on Awakening to Truth* <note>original: 悟真篇 (Wuzhen pian); a foundational text of Song Dynasty internal alchemy by Zhang Boduan.</note> says: "The effort is easy and the medicine is not far; once it is explained clearly, people cannot help but laugh." They laugh because it is so near and easy to find, yet people search for it in distant and lofty places. The *Chapters on Returning to Life* <note>original: 復命篇 (Fuming pian); another influential alchemical text.</note> says: "Clearly, it is right before your eyes."

<summary>This page explains that the "Mysterious Pearl" (the elixir's seed) is generated internally through the alignment of spiritual centers. It argues that the "Great Medicine" is not a distant mystery but is immediately present within the practitioner, requiring only the correct method to perceive it.</summary>

At this time, people do not see. It is precisely because they do not see that they abandon what is internal to pursue what is external. Some gather the essence of the sun and the brilliance of the moon; some refine minerals, stones, herbs, and trees <note>This refers to **External Alchemy** (*Waidan*), the literal attempt to create elixirs from chemical substances, which Yu Yan critiques here in favor of internal cultivation.</note>; others practice the **Arts of the Bedchamber** <term>Arts of the Bedchamber<note>original: 房中之術 (fangzhong zhishu); sexual practices aimed at prolonging life, which many internal alchemists considered a distraction or a lower path.</note></term>. This is exactly what is meant by: "The Way is near, yet men seek it afar; the task is easy, yet men seek it in difficulty." How tragic!

### The Manifestation of Internal Harmony

**The Yellow Center gradually connects with the Principle; its moistening glow reaches the skin and flesh.**

When there is "something" within the **Elixir Field** <term>Elixir Field<note>original: 丹田 (dantian); the "fields" in the body where life-energy is refined and stored. Here, it refers specifically to the internal centers where the "seed" of the elixir resides.</note></term> and the **One Breath** <term>One Breath<note>original: 一氣 (yiqi); the primordial, unified energy or vital force that precedes the division into Yin and Yang.</note></term> flows through, then harmony and compliance accumulate within, and the radiant brilliance manifests on the outside.

**If the beginning is upright, the end will be cultivated; once the trunk is established, the branches can be maintained.**

If the gnomon <note>A vertical pillar used in ancient times to measure the sun's shadow.</note> is upright, then the shadow is straight; if the source is deep, then the stream flows long. If one attains the "One," then the myriad tasks are completed.

**This "One" is veiled and hidden, so that the people of the world do not know of it.**

# By Imperial Command: The Siku Quanshu Collection
## Four Hundred Words on the Golden Elixir: Volume One

The ancestor of the Great Way proceeds from nothing other than the **One Breath** <term>One Breath<note>original: 一氣 (yiqi); the primordial, unified vital energy that exists before the universe divides into the dualities of Yin and Yang.</note></term>, and it is through this that all transformations are accomplished. When analyzed, it is "black and white"; when divided, it is "azure and yellow." Allegorically, we call it the "Sun and Moon"; by name, we call it the "Dragon and Tiger." Though there is such a confusing multitude of terms, they are all ultimately encompassed by the two words **Yin and Yang**. In reality, they are but a single thing.

Whether one uses the names **Divine Water** and **Flowery Pool** <term>Divine Water and Flowery Pool<note>original: 神水華池 (shenshui huachi); poetic metaphors for the refined internal fluids or "elixir" generated within the body during meditation.</note></term>, or titles like the **Lead Furnace** and **Earthen Cauldron** <term>Lead Furnace and Earthen Cauldron<note>original: 鉛爐土釜 (qianlu tufu); internal "vessels" or locations in the body—usually the lower abdomen—where the alchemical heat is applied to refine energy.</note></term>, they all refer to the same single place.

Some practitioners speak of the **Winter Solstice** at the **Hour of the Rat** <term>Hour of the Rat<note>original: 子時 (zishi); the period between 11:00 PM and 1:00 AM. In traditional Chinese thought, this is the "midpoint of night" when the first spark of Yang energy returns.</note></term>; others speak of the interval between the dark moon and the new moon. Because people do not understand [the internal nature of the work], they believe that the **Return of the First Yang** <note>The "Return of the First Yang" refers to the moment life-force begins to stir and regenerate.</note> must occur exactly at the Winter Solstice during the Hour of the Rat, or that the "Joining of the Sun and Moon" must happen specifically during the dark of the moon. Consequently, they spend their time consulting calendars, rotating time-tracking wheels, and counting hours on their fingers—this is a profound error!

Who understands how to condense a year into a month, condense a month into a day, or condense a day into a single hour? Within a single hour, the creative transformations of an entire year or an entire month are naturally contained. Thus, one moment in time is the same as one place; and one place is the same as the One...

<summary>Yu Yan argues that the complex symbols of alchemy (Dragon, Tiger, Lead, Mercury) and specific astrological timings (Winter Solstice, Hour of the Rat) are merely metaphors for a singular internal process centered on the "One Breath." He critiques practitioners who take these timings literally rather than seeking the "creative transformation" within a single moment of focused meditation.</summary>

<keywords>One Breath (Yiqi), Yin and Yang, Dragon and Tiger, Internal Alchemy (Neidan), Divine Water, Lead Furnace, Winter Solstice, Hour of the Rat, timing vs. internal state</keywords>

...is a single thing. Therefore, the **Winter and Summer Solstices**, the **Spring and Autumn Equinoxes** <note>original: 冬夏二至春秋兩分 (dongxia erzhi chunqiu liangfen); these represent the four turning points of the solar year, used here to symbolize the peaks and transitions of Yin and Yang within the body.</note>, the directional markers of **Mao, You, Jia, and Geng** <note>These are stems and branches used to denote East, West, and specific celestial alignments.</note>, the lunar phases of the **new moon, full moon, and the quarters**, as well as the **Hour of the Rat, Horse, Snake, and Pig** and the **Hour of the Rat, Monkey, Tiger, and Dog**—including the **twenty-four solar terms** and the **seventy-two pentads** <term>Twenty-four solar terms and seventy-two pentads<note>original: 二十四氣七十二候 (ershisheng qi qishier hou); a traditional Chinese calendar system that divides the year into 15-day and 5-day intervals to track subtle seasonal changes.</term>—all these represent the yearly conjunctions and the monthly cycles.

The timing of the **Li and Kan** <note>Fire and Water hexagrams</note>, the months of the **Rabbit and Rooster** <note>East and West</note>, the cavities of **Xun and Qian** <note>Wind and Heaven</note>, and the **gates of two and eight** <note>referring to the number sixteen, symbolizing a complete cycle</note>; the practice of **Sprouting in the morning and Enveloping in the evening** <term>Sprouting and Enveloping<note>original: 朝屯暮蒙 (zhao Tun mu Meng); referring to Hexagram 3 (Tun/Sprouting) and Hexagram 4 (Meng/Youthful Folly), which represent the start of the alchemical process in the morning and the cooling/nourishing in the evening.</note></term>, and **Encountering by day and Returning by night** <note>Referring to Hexagram 44 (Gou) and Hexagram 24 (Fu)</note>—all of these are entirely contained within this **single center**.

The *Chapter of Emerald Emptiness* says: 
"Later, I returned to consult others as before, 
And saw through how many **wild fox spirits** <term>Wild fox spirits<note>original: 野狐精 (yehu jing); a derogatory term for false teachers or those who claim enlightenment but follow "heterodox" or deluded paths.</note></term> there are. 
Each and every one is ignorant of the **True One Place**; 
They are all following **side doors** and are not the truth." 
In this world, how many people truly know this **One**?

->**Superior virtue is non-action; it does not seek through observation. Inferior virtue is action; its application never rests.**<-

The *Classic of the Way and Virtue* <note>The *Dao De Jing*</note> says: "Superior virtue takes no action and has no ulterior motive; inferior virtue takes action and has a motive." <note>original: 上德無為而無以為下德為之而有以為; this is a foundational quote from Chapter 38 of the *Dao De Jing* explaining that true virtue is spontaneous and natural, while lesser virtue requires effort and intentionality.</note>

# Imperial Edition of the Complete Library of the Four Treasuries: Volume One
### Page Thirty-six

Now, **High Virtue** is non-action and does not seek through observation <note>original: 上德无為不以察求 (shangde wuwei buyi chaqiu); this refers to the state of a "superior person" whose spirit is naturally aligned with the Dao, requiring no forced practice.</note>. This means the **Spirit** guards the **Dark Palace** <term>Dark Palace<note>original: 玄宫 (xuangong); in internal alchemy, this typically refers to the Niwan or the upper elixir field located in the head.</note></term> and remains silent in non-action. **Low Virtue** acts, and its application never ceases; this means the **Qi** rises in the **North Palace** <term>North Palace<note>original: 北府 (beifu); corresponding to the direction of Water and the kidneys, this refers to the lower elixir field or the "gate of life."</note></term> and is continuous and unbroken. This further borrows the sayings of **Laozi** <note>The legendary founder of Daoism and purported author of the *Dao De Jing*.</note> to reveal the secrets of the two cavities of **Kan and Li** <note>The hexagrams for Water and Fire, representing the core polarities of the human body that must be reconciled.</note>.

When the upper is closed, it is called **Being**; when the lower is closed, it is called **Non-being**. **Non-being** serves the upper; the upper is where the **Spiritual Virtue** dwells.

->The method of the two apertures: Being and Non-being are mutually dependent<-

"The upper being closed is called **Being**" refers to the **Palace of Li** <note>The trigram for Fire, associated with the heart and the eyes.</note> having an image and harboring **True Water**. "The lower being closed is called **Non-being**" refers to the **Door of Kan** <note>The trigram for Water, associated with the kidneys and the lower abdomen.</note> having no form and hiding the **True Dragon** <note>A symbol for the hidden Yang energy within the Yin (Water) of the lower body.</note>. "Non-being serves the upper; the upper is where the Spiritual Virtue dwells" means that when the **Original Spirit** <term>Original Spirit<note>original: 元神 (yuanshen); the innate, primordial consciousness that exists before thought or sensory experience.</note></term> perches in its original palace, the **Qi** will follow it and rise upward. Therefore, the wonder of this practice lies in the **Eight Gates** <note>These are the various apertures or energetic "valves" of the body that the practitioner must learn to seal or open to direct the flow of internal energy.</note> being held firm and secure.

# Imperial Edition of the Complete Library of the Four Treasuries: Volume One
### Page Thirty-six

...refers to the **Palace of Light** <note>original: 靈宮 (linggong); the spiritual residence in the head.</note>, and the process of **Sealing**.

**Sealing** means to lock or close tightly <note>original: 鎖閉 (suobi)</note>.

> The *Chapters on Awakening to the Truth* <note>original: 悟真篇 (Wuzhen pian); a foundational 11th-century text on internal alchemy.</note> says: "The eldest son first drinks the wine of the West, and the young girl first opens the flower of the Northern lands; if you bring the 'moth-eyebrows' <note>original: 青蛾 (qing'e); a poetic term for a beautiful woman, here symbolizing the 'Young Girl' or the element of Metal.</note> to meet, then for a time, lock them within the **Yellow House** <term>Yellow House<note>original: 黃家 (huangjia); the central 'Earth' element in the body, typically the spleen or the middle elixir field, where the internal marriage of opposites occurs.</note></term>."

This refers to the palace where the **Original Spirit** <note>The innate, primordial consciousness.</note> of the **Upper Elixir Field** dwells. In the method of these two apertures, **Being** and **Non-being** are mutually dependent: if the lower is not closed, the **Fire** <note>Alchemical heat or energy.</note> will not gather; if the upper is not closed, the **Medicine** <note>The refined internal substances being transformed into the elixir.</note> will not rise.

> The *Chapters on Awakening to the Truth* says: "Return them to the **Earthen Cauldron** <term>Earthen Cauldron<note>original: 土釜 (tufu); a metaphor for the central vessel of the body where the 'ingredients' of the soul are cooked.</note></term> and seal it firmly; next, introduce the **Flowing Pearl** <term>Flowing Pearl<note>original: 流珠 (liuzhu); a common alchemical metaphor for liquid mercury, representing the volatile Spirit or the element of Fire.</note></term> to match them together."

Therefore, they must depend on each other before the work can be completed. What are the "Flowing Pearl" and "Earthen Cauldron"? They are simply different names for the two apertures of **Kan and Li** <note>The hexagrams representing Water/Kidneys and Fire/Heart.</note>.

**Know the White, keep to the Black, and the spiritual radiance will come of itself.** <note>A famous instruction from the *Dao De Jing*, used here to explain the relationship between hidden and manifest energies.</note> "White" is the **Essence of Metal**; "Black" is the **Foundation of Water**.

"White" is Metal; "Black" is Water. The method of the Elixir uses Water as its foundation, and the Essence of Metal is born from within the Water <note>In traditional Chinese cosmology, Metal (White) is the "mother" of Water (Black), but in internal alchemy, the "Golden" essence must be recovered from within the darkness of the Water/Kidney realm.</note>.

# Imperial Edition of the Complete Library of the Four Treasuries: Volume One

...the reason one can **know the white** is solely by **keeping to the black** <note>original: 知白守黑 (zhibai shouhei); a reference to the *Dao De Jing*. In alchemy, this refers to maintaining an inner, hidden stillness (the "black") to allow the pure, manifest light (the "white") to appear.</note>. If you keep to the black, then the white will naturally manifest. 

How does one "keep" to it? It is exactly like a cat guarding a burrow, waiting for a mouse to emerge: the body does not move, the eyes do not blink, and the heart is focused entirely on the mouse, with no other thoughts whatsoever. 

**Divine Brilliance** <term>Divine Brilliance<note>original: 神明 (shenming); the state of heightened spiritual clarity and the inner light that emerges when the spirit is refined.</note></term> is the **Heavenly Mechanism** <note>original: 天機 (tianji); the secret, subtle workings of nature and the universe.</note>. If you wish to "steal" this mechanism <note>A common alchemical term for grasping the secrets of creation to achieve immortality.</note>, you must first empty your heart. When the heart is empty, the **Spirit** congeals; when the Spirit congeals, the **Breath** becomes stable. When the breath is stable, then both sides rise together to form "one pound" <note>original: 共一斤 (gong yi jin); an alchemical metaphor for the perfect balance of Yin and Yang forces in the internal elixir.</note>, and the Divine Brilliance arrives of its own accord. 

The "Far Roaming" section of the *Songs of the South* <note>original: 離騷遠遊 (Li Sao Yuan You); a classic poem describing a shamanistic or spiritual journey through the heavens.</note> says: 

> "Do not disturb your soul; it will naturally return. Unify the breath until it is wondrously spiritual, and preserve it in the middle of the night. Wait for it in emptiness; do not act before it <note>original: 無滑而魂兮... (wu hua er hun xi...)</note>." 

This shares the same essential meaning as our text. 

Master **Zhu Xi** of Ziyang <note>original: 紫陽朱子 (Ziyang Zhuzi); the eminent Neo-Confucian scholar who investigated the "Unity of the Three" under a pseudonym.</note> noted: "Indeed, the advice given by the sage **Guangchengzi** to the **Yellow Emperor** <note>A legendary encounter where the ancient emperor sought the secret of long life; the advice centered on stillness and the preservation of the spirit.</note> was nothing more than this. It is truly the essential secret of the **Immortals**."

# Elucidation of the Seal of the Unity of the Three: Volume One

### Page 37

**Water** is the **Pivot of the Dao** <note>original: 道樞 (daoshu); the central axis or hinge upon which the transformations of the Way turn.</note>. Its number is **One**, the beginning of **Yin and Yang**. It is the **Darkness containing the Yellow Sprout** <term>Yellow Sprout<note>original: 黃芽 (huangya); a symbolic term for the first sign of the emerging internal elixir, representing the birth of pure Yang within the darkness of Yin.</note></term>, the master of the **Five Metals**, and the **Northern River Chariot**. Therefore, **Lead** is black on the outside but harbors the **Gold Flower** <term>Gold Flower<note>original: 金華 (jinhua); the refined essence or "spirit" of the lead, representing the hidden light within matter.</note></term> within. It is like **wearing coarse clothes while harboring jade** <note>A classic metaphor from the *Dao De Jing* meaning that true value is hidden beneath a humble or "mad" exterior.</note>, appearing as a madman to the outside world.

**Water** is the pivot of the Dao and the beginning of Yin and Yang. Why is this? **Heaven generates "One" as Water** <note>original: 天一生水 (tian yi sheng shui); a reference to the *He Tu* (Yellow River Map) numerology where the number 1 is associated with the direction North and the element Water.</note>, which stands at the head of the **Five Elements**. When the "One" of Water is added to the "Five" of Earth <note>In traditional numerology, 1 is the "generating" number of water and 6 is its "completion" number (1+5).</note>, it becomes the **Completion Number** of Water. Is this not the image of the **Darkness containing the Yellow Sprout**?

The phrase "the darkness containing the Yellow Sprout" refers to the production of **Lead** within Water. Lead is the master of the Five Metals. Within the **Dark Mystery** <note>original: 玄冥 (xuanming); a poetic name for the North, the direction associated with Water and winter.</note> of the North, it encounters **Earth** and produces the **Yellow Sprout**. 

The Yellow Sprout is exactly the **Gold Flower**. The Gold Flower is the refined essence of Lead. Therefore, the physical body of Lead is black on the outside, while the Gold Flower is hidden within it. This is just as a supreme treasure is hidden in the bosom of a man wearing coarse clothes. 

The **Northern River Chariot** <term>River Chariot<note>original: 河車 (heche); in internal alchemy, this refers to the mechanism by which energy is circulated through the body, often compared to the rotation of the stars.</note></term> is the **Emperor's Chariot**. It is called this because it follows the **Celestial River** <note>The Milky Way.</note> in its rotation...

### Volume One

It turns, and therefore it is called the **River Chariot** <term>River Chariot<note>original: 河車 (heche); a primary metaphor in internal alchemy for the circulation of internal energy—specifically the refined essence—up the spinal column to the head.</note></term>. Now, when **True Lead** <note>original: 真鉛 (zhenqian); representing the original, pre-natal energy or "True Yang" hidden within the body.</note> is born within the Water, it encounters **True Fire** <note>original: 真火 (zhenhuo); the focused heat of meditative concentration and breath.</note>, which smelts it until it emerges. Then, the River Chariot does not dare to pause for even a moment, carrying the essence upward to the summit of **Mount Kunlun** <note>original: 崑崙 (kunlun); in this context, the name for the highest point of the body—the crown of the head or the brain.</note>.

**Gold is the Mother of Water; the Mother is hidden within the Child's womb.**
**Water is the Child of Gold; the Child is stored within the Mother's embrace.**

Gold generates Water, which is why it is said that **Gold is the Mother of Water**. Water is the Child of Gold. Because there is Gold contained within Water, it is said that the **Mother is hidden within the Child's womb** and the **Child is stored within the Mother's embrace** <note>This refers to the "Gold within Water" (shui zhong jin), a core alchemical concept where the "True Yang" (Metal) must be recovered from the "Initial Yin" (Water/Kidneys).</note>. If scholars understand the meaning of "Gold within Water," they will understand the very source of the river from which the **Medicine** <note>The internal elixir or refined life-force.</note> is produced.

**Like a great abyss: suddenly sinking, suddenly floating.**

The **True Human** <term>True Human<note>original: 真人 (zhenren); a Daoist term for a perfected being. Here, the author explicitly identifies it with the *Original Spirit*.</note></term> is the **Original Spirit** <term>Original Spirit<note>original: 元神 (yuanshen); the primordial, unconditioned consciousness that exists before the "discriminating mind" or ego develops.</note></term>. That which is described as **appearing as if existing, yet as if not** is something that is "continuous, as if it remains, and its use is effortless" <note>A direct quote from Chapter 6 of the *Dao De Jing*, describing the subtle, everlasting nature of the Dao and the "Mysterious Female."</note>. Like... <note>The text ends mid-sentence on the word "髣" (resembling/like).</note>

# Elucidation of the Seal of the Unity of the Three
### Revised Edition from the Four Treasuries

"Vaguely, like the **Great Abyss**, now sinking, now floating." This means that the **True Person** <note>original: 真人 (zhenren); the "Perfected One" or the realized internal self, often identified with the refined Original Spirit.</note> travels back and forth within the **Great Abyss** <note>original: 大淵 (dayuan); a metaphor for the profound, deep state of meditative consciousness or the "Void" where the spirit dwells.</note>. This state is indistinct and blurry, with the spirit sinking and floating without a fixed position. Xiao Zixu’s <note>Xiao Zixu (Xiao Tingzhi) was a prominent 13th-century internal alchemist of the Southern Lineage.</note> *Collection of the Great Achievement of the Golden Elixir* states: "Upon the path of the **Yellow Flower** <note>original: 黃華 (huanghua); a poetic term for the refined essence or "clover" of the internal elixir.</note> on **Mount Kunlun**, the **Original Spirit** <note>original: 元神 (yuanshen); the innate, pre-natal consciousness that exists before the interference of thought and ego.</note> is frequently guided to depart and return." Therefore, we know that the Great Abyss is actually another name for **Mount Kunlun** <note>As noted previously, Kunlun represents the crown of the head or the brain, the highest point where energy is refined.</note>.

## Advancing and Retreating, Distributed to Guard Each Border and Corner

In the **Method of the Elixir** <note>original: 丹法 (danfa); the specific internal practices used to refine energy and consciousness.</note>, one "advances" at the hour of **Zi** <note>original: 子 (zi); 11 PM – 1 AM, the time of the first stirring of Yang energy in the daily cycle.</note> and "retreats" at the hour of **Wu** <note>original: 午 (wu); 11 AM – 1 PM, the peak of Yang and the beginning of the retreating Yin energy.</note>. These movements of advancing and retreating are distributed so that each process guards its own territory and boundary, ensuring they do not interfere with or encroach upon one another. Yang Xubai's <note>Yang Xubai (Yang Shibi) was a Southern Song dynasty alchemist known for his poetic explanations of internal cultivation.</note> *Chants on the Purity of the Golden Elixir* says: "In front of the furnace of **Zi and Wu**, distinguish the advancing and the retreating; within the tripod of **Qian and Kun** <note>original: 乾坤 (qiankun); the hexagrams representing Heaven and Earth, symbolizing the head and the lower abdomen (the Dantian) where the internal work takes place.</note>, differentiate between sinking and floating." This is exactly what the text refers to.

# Volume One
<margin>Fragmentary title: Four [Books] of the Golden Elixir...</margin>

"White" refers to **White Gold** <note>original: 白金 (baijin); a metaphor for "True Lead" or the primordial essence (Jing) that has been refined.</note>; "Cinnabar" refers to **Cinnabar Mercury** <note>original: 朱汞 (zhugong); a metaphor for the refined spirit (Shen).</note>. In terms of gathering these elements, "White" refers to gathering the **White Gold** from the **Palace of Kan** <note>original: 坎宮 (kangong); the trigram Kan represents Water and the North; in the body, it refers to the lower dantian (the "elixir field" below the navel) where the "True Lead" originates.</note> in the North to serve as the **Mother of the Elixir** <note>original: 丹母 (danmu); the essential foundation or "seed" from which the internal elixir grows.</note>. This White Gold and Cinnabar Mercury are of the same category and follow one another. To create the elixir, for the "Cinnabar" aspect, one must daily circulate the **Fire** to extract the **Lead-Qi** and add **Mercury** into the process <note>This refers to the core alchemical process of *chouqian tiangong* (extracting lead to supplement mercury), where the practitioner gradually replaces dense, worldly essence with refined, spiritual vitality.</note>.

Chen Momuo’s <note>Chen Momuo, also known as Chen Nan (died 1213), was a famous Taoist master of the Southern Lineage known for his mastery of internal alchemy and "Thunder Magic."</note> *Essay on Venerating the Correct* states:

->Internally guarding the **River Chariot**, dwelling within the Imperial Court.<-
->From the beginning, the **Water of Heaven One** takes precedence.<-
->To match the tallies, one must obtain the **Fire of the Universal Heaven**.<-
->The establishment of this meaning is like a boat used for crossing to the far shore.<-
->Moving and transporting it with dedicated merit, continuing day and night.<-
->The subtle turning of the mechanism is like the body of the **Celestial Pivot**.<-
<note>In this poem: the "River Chariot" (河車) is the path of energy circulation; "Water of Heaven One" (天一水) is the primordial vitality; "matching tallies" (合符) means synchronizing with cosmic timing; "Fire of the Universal Heaven" (周天火) is the full cycle of breath and focus; and the "Celestial Pivot" (璇璣) refers to the Big Dipper, symbolizing the central axis of the body's internal energy.</note>

Superior scholars who cultivate the Elixir, if they understand this, will then recognize that **True Lead** <note>original: 真鉛 (zhenqian)</note> does not involve the use of physical lead. Students often only know that the coupling of lead and mercury forms the "medicine," but they do not understand the **Fire Work** <note>original: 火功 (huogong); the precise timing and intensity of meditative focus used to transform the internal substances.</note> of **Extracting Lead and Adding Mercury**. Even if they succeed in forming the "Elixir Head," they cannot prevent it from being dissipated and lost.

<summary>This page defines "White Gold" (refined essence) and "Cinnabar Mercury" (refined spirit) as the core components of the elixir, emphasizing that "True Lead" is a spiritual substance rather than a physical metal. It cites the master Chen Nan to explain that the constant circulation of internal "Fire" is necessary to stabilize the elixir and prevent the loss of spiritual progress.</summary>

<margin>Thirty-nine</margin>

## Refinement Acts as the Outer Guard; the White Dwells Steadfastly Within

"Refinement as the outer guard" refers to the application of the **True Fire** <note>original: 真火 (zhenhuo); the focused intent and rhythmic breath-work used to drive the alchemical transformation.</note> in the **Outer Furnace**. "The White dwells steadfastly within" refers to the **Golden Elixir** <note>original: 金丹 (jindan); the refined spiritual essence attained through practice.</note> residing peacefully in the **Inner Furnace** <note>The "Inner Furnace" and "Outer Furnace" represent a dual aspect of the body: the inner is the passive vessel where the seed of immortality grows, while the outer represents the active energetic work required to "cook" and protect that seed.</note>. When the Elixir is present in the Inner Furnace, one must utilize the fire of the Outer Furnace to refine and nourish it day and night; only then will it reach maturity. 

Hao Guangning <note>Hao Datong (1140–1212), a famous adept of the Quanzhen "Complete Perfection" school of Taoism, known for his mastery of the Book of Changes.</note> says in his *Collected Works of Great Antiquity*: 

"At the crown, the **Dragon’s Pearl** <note>original: 驪珠 (lizhu); a black dragon's pearl, a metaphor for the concentrated radiance of the spirit.</note> is circulated, and its light manifests of itself; within the abdomen, the secret treasure is hidden, resting in brilliant splendor." 

Does this not mean that the Fire is circulated on the outside while the Elixir is hidden within?

Square and round, an inch in diameter, they blend and support one another. Born of the **Primal Heaven and Earth** <note>original: 先天天地 (xiantian tiandi); refers to the state of the universe or the human body before physical birth, representing pure, undifferentiated potential.</note>, it stands majestic, noble, and high. Beside it are walls and watchtowers, shaped like the **Gourd-mountain of Penglai** <note>original: 蓬壺 (penghu); one of the mythical isles of the immortals, often visualized in internal alchemy as a sacred, enclosed space within the body.</note>. It is enclosed and sealed all around, yet its passages branch out in the four directions; its defenses are firm and dense, cutting off all wicked and corrupting influences. Connected corridors and pavilions are linked together to guard against the unexpected <note>This architectural imagery describes the "Alchemical Cauldron" or the "Elixir Field" (Dantian). It must be a perfectly sealed environment to prevent the "leakage" of vital energy, yet it remains the central hub of the body's spiritual circulation.</note>.

It uses architectural metaphors to describe the Dantian as a fortified, sacred palace that prevents the loss of spiritual energy.</summary>

# Four Hundred Words on the Golden Elixir
## Volume I

->一<-

"Square and round, an inch in diameter" refers to the **Mud Pill Palace** <note>original: 泥丸宮 (niwan gong); the upper Cinnabar Field located in the center of the brain, considered the seat of the spirit and the destination of the refined essence.</note>. People today only refer to the heart as the "square inch," but they do not realize that within the **Three Cinnabar Fields** <note>original: 三田 (santian); the three major energy centers of the body located in the head, chest, and abdomen. Each acts as a "field" where the elixir is planted and harvested.</note> of the human body, each contains a void of exactly one inch, and the channels of breath and blood are all interconnected through them. 

"Blending and mutually supporting" indicates that the head contains the **Nine Palaces** <note>original: 九宮 (jiugong); a Taoist internal map that divides the brain into nine distinct chambers or spiritual residences.</note>, and the Mud Pill resides in the center of them. The *Yellow Court Scripture* <note>original: 黃庭經 (Huangting Jing); a foundational 4th-century Taoist text focusing on the visualization of deities within the body's organs.</note> says: "The nine perfected spirits of the Mud Pill each have their own chamber." This "square and round, one inch" space is precisely this location. 

"Born before Heaven and Earth" refers to the single aperture of the Mud Pill; it is the **Ancestral Aperture** <note>original: 祖竅 (zuqiao); the "opening of all openings," believed to be the point where the primordial spirit enters the body and the primary gateway for alchemical transformation.</note> of the ten thousand openings of the entire body. When this aperture opens, all other openings open in unison. 

"Looming tall and exceedingly high" means that the Mud Pill Palace is situated at the summit of the **Kunlun Peak** <note>original: 崑崙峯 (kunlun feng); in internal alchemy, Kunlun—the mythical mountain at the center of the world—is a metaphor for the crown of the head.</note>; it is the seat where the **Primordial Spirit** <note>original: 元神 (yuanshen); the original, undying consciousness that exists before thought and ego.</note> resides. Above, it corresponds to the **Mysterious Metropolis** <note>original: 玄都 (xuandu); a celestial city in the highest Taoist heavens, here mirrored within the practitioner's own anatomy.</note>; it is the homeland where the myriad spirits assemble. 

"Flanked by walls and watchtowers" means the **Seven Apertures** <note>The eyes, ears, nostrils, and mouth, which act as the external "gates" to the internal palace.</note> are arranged around its exterior, while the **Grotto Chamber** and the **Yellow Watchtower** <note>original: 洞房 (dongfang) and 黃闕 (huangque); names for specific meditative chambers located within the Nine Palaces of the brain.</note> are both located in front of it. "Its shape resembles the **Peng-pot**" <note>original: 蓬壺 (penghu); a reference to the gourd-shaped island of the immortals.</note> means it is like the three divine mountains upon the sea, with **Penglai** <note>original: 蓬萊; a mythical island of immortality, used here to describe the appearance and spiritual atmosphere of the inner brain during deep meditation.</note> residing...

<summary>This page identifies the Mud Pill Palace in the brain as the "Ancestral Aperture" and the seat of the Primordial Spirit, using the geography of mythical mountains to describe internal anatomy.</summary>

# Four Hundred Words on the Golden Elixir
## Volume I

"Above, it is encircled and closed" refers to withdrawing one's vision and hearing into the self, and carefully sealing the openings of the senses <note>original: 窒其兌 (zhi qi dui); a reference to the *Daodejing*, meaning to block the sensory apertures—like the mouth and eyes—to prevent vital energy from leaking out.</note>.

"Four directions connected and lingering" means that the **Primordial Harmony** <note>original: 元和 (yuanhe); the original, balanced state of vital energy or *qi* before it is scattered by worldly desires.</note> moves internally. The single breath circulates, and the **Vermilion Bird** <note>original: 朱雀 (zhuque); a celestial animal representing the element of fire and, in internal alchemy, the spirit or the heart’s energy.</note> soars and hovers around the **Jade Terrace** <note>original: 瓊臺 (qiongtai); a metaphorical name for the spiritual center within the head.</note>.

"Guarding and defending firmly" is like a dragon nurturing its pearl—the heart never forgets it; it is like a hen brooding over her eggs—the breath is never interrupted <note>These two metaphors are classic alchemical tropes for "Concentrated Mindfulness" (the dragon) and "Continuous Breathing" (the hen).</note>. 

"Closing off evil and depravity" means that even if one allows "demonic realms" <note>original: 魔境 (mojing); refers to the distracting thoughts, hallucinations, or mental projections that arise during deep meditation.</note> to invade, one simply wields the "ax" <note>original: 斤斧 (jinfu); a metaphor for the power of discernment and the "Sword of Wisdom" used to cut through mental attachments.</note> of the mind to clear them away.

"Curving corridors connected to guard against the unexpected" means that if the spirit begins to wander out, it should be immediately gathered back in, allowing the **Twin Boys** <note>original: 雙童 (shuangtong); symbolic guardian spirits or "inner clerks" often associated with the eyes or the refined essences that watch over the "cave gate" of the upper Cinnabar Field.</note> to perpetually guard the gate of the cavern. In this state, one can reach a state of "no-thought," yet it is difficult to endure the toil of worldly worry.

When the breath is harmonized, the **Ultimate Treasure** <note>original: 至寶 (zhibao); the Golden Elixir or the perfected state of being.</note> is formed. If the heart engages in active striving, then the breath..."

If the vital energy <note>original: 氣 (qi)</note> is thrown into chaos, then the **floral brilliance** <term>英華 (yinghua): The most refined, radiant essence of a person's internal energy and spirit.</term> will scatter. For this reason, one must not be without focused thought, yet one must also not succumb to anxiety or exhaustion. 

When the spirit and energy fill the "inner chamber" <note>A metaphor for the dantian or the meditative space within the body.</note>, nothing can force them to stay. He who guards them prospers; he who loses them perishes. In movement or stillness, in rest or pause, they must always 

->accompany the person.<-

When truth is accumulated through long-sustained effort, the **Great Harmony** <term>太和 (taihe): The state of supreme cosmic and internal balance where all elements are in perfect proportion.</term> overflows. In movement or stillness, in rest or pause, one must always guard this state diligently. To guard it is to prosper;

->to lose it is to perish; one must not be separated from it for even a moment.<-

This "Guarding of the One" is not the same as the methods of "passing through the organs" <note>A practice where the practitioner visualizes energy moving through the five viscera.</note>, nor is it "Inner Vision" <note>original: 內視 (neishi); a meditative technique involving visualizing the internal deities or organs.</note> where specific thoughts are projected. It is not "Pacing the Dipper" <term>步罡 (bugang): A ritual Taoist dance where the practitioner mimics walking on the stars of the Big Dipper to command cosmic forces.</term>, nor "lodging with the Six Jia" <note>A ritual practice involving the spirits of the sexagenary cycle to gain protection or power.</note>, nor the calculation of days and hours. It is not the "Yin Way" of suppressing the "Nine-One" <note>original: 厭九一 (yan jiuyi); likely a reference to specific sexual alchemy practices or complex breath retention techniques that were often criticized by the Quanzhen school.</note>, which only serves to muddy and disturb the **Primordial Womb** <note>original: 元胞 (yuanbao); the energetic center where the "Immortal Embryo" is said to be conceived.</note>. 

Other false practices include "eating breath" <note>aerophagia; the practice of swallowing air into the stomach.</note> until the intestines rumble, or exhaling the "correct" breath while accidentally inhaling external evils. Some practitioners do not sleep day or night, 

remaining awake even during the dark and new moons <note>original: 晦朔 (huishuo); the transition from the end of one lunar month to the start of the next.</note> without rest. Their bodies become more exhausted by the day, until they fall into a trance-like state, looking like idiots. In such a state, the "hundred vessels" and the internal cauldron...

# Imperial Edition of the Complete Library of the Four Treasuries
## Elucidation of the Seal of the Unity of the Three | Volume I

When the internal energies boil and race about, they cannot achieve clarity and stillness. Such a practitioner might dwell among piles of earth, erecting altars and shrines <term>壇宇 (tanyu): Specifically constructed ritual spaces or platforms for Daoist ceremonies.</term>, offering sacrifices with reverence from morning until evening. 

Under these conditions, ghosts and spirits may manifest their forms, and the practitioner feels deeply moved or sighing in their dreams. Though the mind is scattered, the intent feels a false sense of joy, and the person tells themselves they will surely extend their lifespan. Yet, suddenly, they die according to the mandate of Heaven, and their corpse is left to rot and be exposed. 

Every action they took was in violation of the Way; they rebelled against the truth and lost hold of the "central mechanism" <note>The "central mechanism" (樞機 shuji) refers to the pivot of cosmic and internal transformation.</note>. These various techniques are extremely numerous—there are over a thousand branches and ten thousand variations. Whether advancing or retreating, they contradict the teachings of **Huang-Lao** <term>黃老 (Huang-Lao): A school of thought named after the Yellow Emperor and Laozi, focusing on the harmony between natural law and human governance/cultivation.</term>. Their twists and turns offend the spirits of the **Nine Capitals** <note>The celestial abodes or administrative centers of the heavens in Daoist cosmology.</note>.

There are three thousand six hundred such methods and dozens of schools for **Nourishing Life** <term>養命 (yangming): Practices focused on health, longevity, and the preservation of vital essence.</term>. They are so confused and tangled that they are impossible to list individually. 

For instance, some practitioners close their eyes for **Inner Vision** <term>內視 (neishi): A meditative technique of turning the gaze inward to visualize the internal landscape of the body.</term>, passing through the five viscera to practice visualization <note>original: 存思 (cunsi); a technique of focused mental imagery to "preserve" the presence of internal deities.</note>. Others engage in **Pacing the Dipper and the Ridge** <term>履斗步岡 (lv dou bu gang): A ritual dance or walk where the practitioner mimics the pattern of the stars in the Big Dipper to harness celestial power.</term>, following the days and hours to offer sacrifices to the first celestial stem <note>original: 祭甲 (jijia); a ritual timing method based on the sexagenary cycle.</note>. 

Is *this* what is called the Way of the **Golden Elixir** <term>金丹 (jindan): The "Philosopher’s Stone" of Chinese alchemy, representing the perfected, immortal state of being.</term>? 

As for those who practice sexual alchemy, they use "nine shallow thrusts and one deep" as their **Fire Timing** <note>original: 火候 (huohou); usually refers to the timing of heat in alchemical refinement, here misappropriated as a sexual technique.</note>, which only results in the disturbance of the "Primal Womb." Those who "eat breath" use the method of **expelling the old and inhaling the new** <term>吐故納新 (tugu naxin): Ancient breathing exercises designed to purge "stale" air and take in "fresh" cosmic qi.</term> as their medicinal substance, but this only causes...

### Page 41

When the intestines and stomach rumble with emptiness, and one sits in "stubborn emptiness" <term>**頑空 (wankong):** A derogatory term for a state of blank, unproductive meditation that lacks the "True Seed" of internal energy.</term>, they suffer through sleepless days and nights. They push themselves with exhausting labor, utterly disregarding the fatigue of their own bodies. 

Some shake their heads and vibrate their brains, or lift their fists and exert great effort. Consequently, the "hundred vessels" <note>The complete network of meridians and blood vessels in the body.</note> boil and race out of control, which can lead to the development of carbuncles and ulcers <note>These are physical manifestations of "pathological heat" or misdirected qi causing skin infections and internal rot.</note>. 

Others pile up earth to establish altars, offering sacrifices at the shrines from morning until evening. In such states, they may dream of "intercourse with ghosts" <note>original: 鬼交 (guijiao). This refers to nocturnal emissions or delusions interpreted as sexual encounters with spirits, seen as a drain on one's vital essence.</note> and fall prey to the influence of demons and enchantments. 

At the beginning, the heart is filled with joy, and the practitioner tells themselves that they will surely extend their lifespan. Yet, suddenly, they meet a premature death in the middle of their journey, and they cannot avoid the rot and decay of their physical frame. 

All of these are instances of perverse and erroneous practice. They clearly violate the writings of the **Yellow Emperor** and completely lose the original intent of the **Old Master** <note>Laozi, the legendary author of the *Tao Te Ching*.</note>. Persisting in delusion without awakening until they reach this end—whose fault is it that they have incurred such misfortune? 

For this reason, **Wang Liangqi** wrote his *Song of Breaking Delusions* and **Chen Niji** <note>Also known as Chen Nan (d. 1213), a famous Daoist master of the Southern Lineage.</note> wrote his *Chant of Mount Luofu*. In these works, they enumerate one by one the errors of the various "side-door" arts <term>**旁門 (pangmen):** Literally "side doors," referring to heterodox or secondary practices that deviate from the "Great Way" of the Golden Elixir.</term> found throughout the world.

The Golden Immortal's Evidence for the Truth, Volume I

# Practice Diligently Day and Night Without Ceasing

The *Collection on Resounding the Dao* by Qiu Changchun <term>**Qiu Changchun (丘長春):** Also known as Qiu Chuji (1148–1227), a celebrated Daoist adept and founder of the Dragon Gate branch of the Quanzhen (Complete Perfection) school.</term> says:

"This is not something achieved by merely performing sitting exercises or forced posturing at dawn and dusk."

This is because the cultivation <term>**Xiulian (修煉):** The alchemical process of "refining and cultivating" the internal energies of essence, breath, and spirit.</term> of the **Great Elixir of Golden Fluid in Nine Turns** <note>The "Nine Turns" refers to the cyclical refinement of the internal elixir, where each "turn" signifies a higher level of purity and transformation.</note> is fundamentally different from the "Small Methods of Ease and Pleasure" practiced by deviant sects <note>original: 旁門 (pangmen), literally "side doors," referring to heterodox or superficial practices that offer temporary health benefits but do not lead to true spiritual realization.</note>. One must renounce worldly affairs, concentrate the mind and will with total devotion, and work tirelessly day and night; only then can success be attained.

Did not Peng Zhenyi <term>**Peng Zhenyi (彭真一):** Peng Xiao (died c. 955), a pivotal alchemist whose commentaries helped standardize the interpretation of internal alchemy during the Five Dynasties period.</term> say:

"A person of resolve must also cast aside the distractions of music and sex, strip away cravings and desires, abandon fame and profit, and seek refuge in the Numinous Mountain <note>original: 靈山 (lingshan). While it can mean a physical mountain, it often serves as a metaphor for the sacred, quiet space of the heart-mind where the Dao is found.</note>. They must break off ordinary social ties, associate only with fellow seekers of immortality, and practice in secret and seclusion without laziness by day or night. Only then can they hope for success. If one does not act in this way, it is nothing more than wasted effort."

This statement by Master Zhenyi is also extremely clear. Yet, there are those who remain ignorant of this, wishing only to spend two hours a day on the **Firing Process** <term>**Huohou (火候):** Literally "fire times," this refers to the precise timing and "heat" (intensity of focus and breath) required to successfully transmute internal energies.</term>. When they hear my teaching of "day and night without ceasing," they are all shocked and meet it with laughter.

<summary>Yu Yan cites the masters Qiu Chuji and Peng Xiao to emphasize that true internal alchemy requires total, 24-hour dedication and the renunciation of worldly desires, contrasting this with superficial "side-door" practices.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument for constant, unwavering dedication to the internal alchemical process. ## The Necessity of Unceasing Practice

To think that this path is an unbearable labor or a source of annoyance is simply to have failed to reflect deeply enough. The *Essays of the Emerald Void* <term>**Cuixu Pian (翠虛篇):** A significant text by Chen Nan (died 1213), a patriarch of the Southern School of Daoism known for emphasizing the "Thunder Magic" and internal alchemy.</term> states:

"Those who cling to rigid attachments will never grasp the truth. To practice in the morning only to stop in the evening is a sign that one’s heart lacks total sincerity."

One must not gullibly believe the common saying that the **Fire Timing** <term>**Huohou (火候):** Literally "fire times," referring to the precise intervals and intensities of internal focus and breath control required to refine the elixir.</term> consists merely of two specific periods during the day. 

Long ago, Huangfu Lüdao <note>A historical seeker of the Dao mentioned in various Quanzhen school hagiographies.</note> saw Wang Sanyi <note>A master of the internal arts.</note> setting up a tally-pot <note>A device used for measuring time or counting intervals.</note>. Huangfu asked, "What is the use of this?" 

Wang replied, "I simply wish to know the exact moments of **Midnight and Noon**." <term>**Ziwu (子午):** The two cardinal "peaks" of the daily cycle. Zi (Midnight) represents the birth of Yang energy, and Wu (Noon) represents the birth of Yin energy.</term>

Huangfu responded, "Throughout the twelve double-hours of the day, there should be no interruption. Why limit yourself to Midnight and Noon?"

In essence, the **Way of the Elixir** <term>**Dandao (丹道):** The path of internal alchemical transformation.</term> is one and the same with the **Way of Heaven** <term>**Tiandao (天道):** The natural, celestial order and the unceasing movement of the cosmos.</term>. Does the Way of Heaven ever cease its movement for even a single breath? Since the Way of Heaven never stops for even a single breath, how could the Way of the Elixir allow for a moment’s interruption?

The *Collection of Gradual Awakening* <term>**Jianwu Ji (漸悟集):** The collected poems and teachings of Ma Danyang (1123–1183), one of the "Seven Perfected" and a primary disciple of the founder of the Quanzhen school.</term> by Ma Danyang says: 

"My teacher’s grace is profound and heavy, a debt hard to repay; I swear to remain within the **walled enclosure** until death, refining the ultimate truth."

This **walled enclosure** <note>original: 環墻 (huanqiang), literally "surrounding wall." This refers to a specific form of ascetic retreat where a practitioner is walled into a small cell with only a small opening for food, symbolizing a total break from the mundane world.</note> is the means by which one cuts off all social coming and going to focus the mind entirely on **Refinement and Cultivation**.

<summary>This page emphasizes that the internal alchemical process must be continuous and mirrors the unceasing movement of the cosmos. It highlights the "walled enclosure" as a symbol of the total isolation and dedication required for success.</summary>
<keywords>Fire Timing (Huohou), Midnight and Noon (Ziwu), Way of Heaven (Tiandao), Ma Danyang, Walled Enclosure (Huanqiang), constant practice, Quanzhen School</keywords>

# The Orthodox Principles of the Golden Elixir: The Complete Book, Volume 1

Consuming the elixir for three years, one ascends lightly to travel to distant realms. One can walk through fire without being scorched and enter water without becoming wet. Capable of both manifesting and vanishing at will, one enjoys eternal joy without worry. When the Way is completed and Virtue is perfected, the practitioner waits in concealment for the appointed time. Then, the **Great One** <term>**Taiyi (太乙):** The primordial unity or the supreme celestial deity who governs the cosmic order and summons the perfected to their heavenly stations.</term> issues the summons. One moves to dwell in the Central Realm; when one's merit is full, one ascends to the heavens to receive the **Sacred Registers and Charts** <term>**Lutu (籙圖):** Celestial documents or talismans that signify a practitioner's official rank and authority within the divine hierarchy.</term>.

Cultivating the elixir <term>**Xiudan (修丹):** The internal process of refining one's essence, breath, and spirit into a "pill" of immortality.</term> requires only a moment’s effort. Why then does the text speak today of "consuming it for three years"? 

The *Essays of the Emerald Void* <note>original: "Cuixu Pian" (翠虛篇)</note> states: 

"The effort of a moment brings the cultivation to light, but to reach full maturity, one must pass through three years."

Furthermore, the "effort of a moment" refers only to the specific time of the **Formation of the Elixir** <term>**Jiedan (結丹):** The critical moment when the internal energies coalesce and crystallize into the alchemical seed.</term>. What is called the "three-year" period refers to the time after one has entered the meditation chamber for a year, during which one must continue **Nourishing through Warmth** <term>**Wenyang (溫養):** A phase of gentle, consistent internal heat used to stabilize and mature the elixir, much like a hen sitting on an egg.</term>. During this time, one must not suddenly depart or travel far. 

Chapter thirty-nine of the *Pointing at the Mystery* <note>original: "Zhixuan" (指玄)</note> by **Zhongli Zhengyang** <note>Also known as Zhongli Quan, a legendary figure and one of the Eight Immortals, considered a patriarch of the internal alchemical tradition.</note> says: 

"When the infant is young and has not yet become a man, he must rely on the mother’s grace of nurturing and raising." 

This explanation is exactly right. If one passes...

<summary>Yu Yan explains that while the actual creation of the internal elixir happens in a "moment," the practitioner must undergo a three-year period of "warm nourishment" to mature the spiritual embryo before achieving full immortality.</summary>

# Elucidation of the Seal of the Unity of the Three

<meta>Imperial Edition of the Complete Library in Four Sections (Siku Quanshu)</meta>

After these three years of refinement, the practitioner becomes as ethereal as smoke and clouds. One walks as if flying, capable of ascending lightly or traveling to distant realms. Should a great flood reach the heavens, I shall not drown; should a great drought melt metal and stone and scorch the hills, I shall not feel the heat. Existence and non-existence reside within my own power, and I dwell in eternal joy without worry. 

However, once the task of **Gentle Nourishment** <term>**Wenyang (溫養):** A critical stage in internal alchemy, often compared to an embryo being incubated. It involves maintaining a steady, "gentle fire" of breath and awareness to mature the internal elixir.</term> is complete, one must remain hidden among the world of humans to accumulate merit and build virtue. By guiding those who are lost toward enlightenment, one finally earns the right to flutter away to the celestial realms. This is why <insert>the missing text</insert> **Linggen** <note>original: "Linggen" (靈根) or "Spiritual Root," a reference to an alchemical master or a specific lineage teaching.</note> instructed **Huangfu Lüdao** to guide future students, and why the Master of Jinhua’s Grotto-Mountain warned **Yang Xubai** that he must not live in solitary seclusion. Only after one’s merit has benefited all living things may one depart as an immortal. How could one be permitted to seek only their own perfection?

The *Records of Fire* were not composed in vain; they elaborate upon the *Book of Changes* to make these principles clear.

---

In antiquity, there were six hundred chapters of the *Records of Fire* <note>original: "Huoji" (火記). These were ancient, often cryptic manuals detailing the "fire times" or the specific timing and intensity of alchemical practice.</note>. ## Four Hundred Words on the Golden Elixir, Volume One

The *Records of Fire* consist of six hundred chapters, and every chapter is similar to the next. These six hundred chapters are analogous to the sixty hexagrams <note>A reference to the sixty hexagrams of the *I Ching* used to track time, excluding the four primary hexagrams that serve as the framework.</note>. Broadly speaking, the numbers of the **Fire Timing** <term>**Huohou (火候):** The precise regulation of "internal heat" or meditative focus and breath used to transmute internal energies.</term> are represented on a small scale by the sixty hexagrams; when expanded and extended, they become the six hundred chapters. The sixty hexagrams represent the timing of a single month, whereas the six hundred chapters represent the timing of the ten months <note>The "ten months" refers to the metaphorical gestation period of the "immortal embryo" within the practitioner's body.</note>. Therefore, it is said that the *Records of Fire* were not created in vain; they "evolve the Changes" <note>original: "Yan Yi" (演易), meaning to elaborate upon the principles of the *Book of Changes* (I Ching).</note> to make the path clear.

# Numbers

At the time of the **first-quarter moon**, the **Dui hexagram** <note>representing the Lake and Joy</note> is in operation. The moon produces half of its total brightness; thus, it is called the "first quarter," and the number assigned to **Dui** is eight. At the time of the **last-quarter moon**, the **Gen hexagram** <note>representing the Mountain and Stillness</note> is in operation. The moon produces half of its darkness <note>original: *po* (魄), the "yin" or shadow-soul of the moon</note>; thus, it is called the "last quarter," and the number for **Gen** is also eight. 

If one observes these two quarters in combination, the **vessel** and **cauldron** of **Qian** and **Kun** <term>**Qian and Kun (乾坤):** The hexagrams for Pure Heaven and Pure Earth, serving as the symbolic container for the alchemical transformation.</term> are established. Within them, the medicinal substances and the **firing process** <term>**Firing Process (火候):** The precise regulation of internal focus and breath during meditation.</term> are entirely contained.

The phrase "two eights correspond to one pound; the Way of Change is upright and does not tilt" <note>A pound (jin) in this era consisted of 16 taels. 8 + 8 = 16.</note> signifies that the moon has upper and lower quarters. The first quarter represents **Metal** <note>associated with the West and the "White Tiger"</note> and accounts for half a pound, totaling one hundred and ninety-two **zhu** <term>**Zhu (銖):** The smallest unit of weight in ancient China; 384 *zhu* equaled one full pound.</term>. The last quarter represents **Water** <note>associated with the North and the essence of Yin</note> and accounts for half a pound, totaling one hundred and ninety-two **zhu**. 

When calculated together, they yield a total of three hundred and eighty-four **zhu**. The **Book of Changes** (*I Ching*) consists of sixty-four hexagrams, and each hexagram has six lines. In total, there are one hundred and ninety-two **Yang lines** and one hundred and ninety-<unclear>two</unclear> <note>The original text cuts off at "ninety," but mathematically requires "two" to reach the total of 192 Yin lines, matching the 192 Yang lines to equal the 384 lines of the I Ching.</note> **Yin lines**.

<summary>This page explains the numerological harmony between the lunar cycle, the 384 lines of the I Ching, and the weights of alchemical ingredients, emphasizing that a perfect "pound" of the elixir (384 zhu) mirrors the cosmic balance of Yin and Yang.</summary>
<keywords>numerology, Dui hexagram, Gen hexagram, first-quarter moon, last-quarter moon, zhu, Qian and Kun, firing process, 384 lines</keywords>

->### The Right Principle of the Golden Elixir<-
->#### Volume I<-

When calculated in total, these twelve months yield **384 lines** <note>original: *yao* (爻). There are 64 hexagrams in the *I Ching*, each consisting of six lines, totaling 384. These represent the various stages of the alchemical cycle.</note>. Master Wei <note>Wei Boyang, the legendary author of the *Seal of the Unity of the Three*</note> borrowed this number to discuss the wonders of the **Alchemical Way** <term>**Dan Dao (丹道):** The "Way of the Elixir," a system of meditative and physical practices intended to achieve spiritual immortality.</term>. However, his purpose was simply to illustrate the equality of **Yin and Yang** and how they must be matched in proper proportion. It is not that there are literally 384 **zhu** <term>**Zhu (銖):** A tiny ancient unit of weight; 24 *zhu* equaled one tael (*liang*).</term>, nor is there a literal requirement for 384 lines.

The *Folios on Awakening to Reality* <note>original: *Wuzhen pian* (悟真篇), a famous 11th-century alchemical poem</note> states: "When the **two eights** correspond, they naturally become intimate." Chen Suyang <note>A significant Taoist practitioner of the Southern School</note> says in his *Folios on the Ultimate Way*: "Within the silence of my meditation, I harmonize the **two eights**." This "two eights" refers to two portions of eight taels each <note>In the traditional weight system, 16 taels made one pound (*jin*); thus, two "eights" represent a perfectly balanced whole.</note>. This signifies the principle of balance and equality, where the weight is even and does not tilt or lean to one side.

If a practitioner were to follow a rigid rule of calculating every *zhu* of weight, or if they were to hold prayer beads in their hand to count every breath, they would merely be inviting hardship upon themselves. How could that be the Way of "utmost simplicity and ease"? 

As the *Folios on Returning to Life* <note>original: *Fuming pian* (復命篇)</note> says: "Directions are gathered by their kind; things are divided by their groups; the two sides rise together to form a single pound." Furthermore, the *Collection of the Great Achievement of the Golden Elixir* <note>original: *Jindan dacheng ji* (金丹大成集)</note> says: "The gate of the two eights..."

<summary>Yu Yan argues that the mathematical figures in alchemical texts, such as the 384 lines of the I Ching or the "two eights," are metaphors for the perfect balance of Yin and Yang rather than literal instructions for breath-counting or physical weights.</summary>

# Elucidation of the Seal of the Unity of the Three
## Volume One

<note>A circular cosmological diagram illustrates the lunar cycle. At the top is a small circle containing the character for the **Full Moon** <note>original: "望" (Wang)</note>. On the left is an oval labeled **First Quarter** <note>original: "上弦" (Shang-xian)</note>. On the right is an oval labeled **Last Quarter** <note>original: "下弦" (Xia-xian)</note>. At the bottom are two smaller circles labeled **New Moon** <note>original: "朔" (Shuo)</note> and **The Dark Moon** <note>original: "晦" (Hui)</note>, representing the end of the lunar month. In the center of the large circle is a vertical inscription reading: "The sun and moon join here like jade disks" <note>original: "日月合璧於此" (Ri yue he bi yu ci)</note>. This diagram visually depicts the moment of conjunction where the "True Lead" and "True Mercury" of the body meet.</note>

The numbers of **Zi** and **Wu** combine to three; the numbers of **Wu** and **Ji** are called five. Once three and five are harmonized, the **Eight Minerals** <term>**Eight Minerals (Ba-shi):** While historically referring to external alchemical ingredients like arsenic or sulfur, in this internal context, they represent the balanced structural components of the refined "immortal body."</term> are properly ordered.

**Zi** <term>**Zi (子):** The first of the Twelve Terrestrial Branches, representing the North, the element of Water, and the time of Midnight.</term> is Water and its number is one; **Wu** <term>**Wu (午):** The seventh Terrestrial Branch, representing the South, the element of Fire, and the time of Noon.</term> is Fire and its number is two. The numbers of **Zi** and **Wu** combined thus make three. 

The number of Earth is five, which is why the central positions of **Wu and Ji** <term>**Wu-Ji (戊巳):** Two of the Ten Celestial Stems associated with the element of Earth and the "Center" of the body where the elixir is formed.</term> are called five. 

When three and five are merged, they become eight; this is why the text speaks of the "**Eight Minerals**." The phrase "three and five are harmonized" signifies that Water, Fire, and Earth—these three—have merged into one. 

And yet, Earth overcomes Water, and Water overcomes Fire; how can there be harmony? <note>Yu Yan is referencing the "Overcoming Cycle" of the **Five Phases** <note>original: "五行" (Wu-xing)</note>, where Earth absorbs/blocks Water and Water extinguishes Fire. He poses this as a paradox: in nature, these elements destroy one another, but in the alchemical state of "Harmony," they support and complete each other.</note>

<summary>This page explains the numerological symbolism of the internal alchemical process, showing how the energies of Water (1), Fire (2), and Earth (5) combine to create a balanced state represented by the number eight. It introduces the paradox of how naturally opposing elements can be brought into harmony within the practitioner.</summary>

<note>A central cosmological diagram illustrates the alchemical alignment of the body's internal directions and elements. # Elucidation of the Seal of the Unity of the Three
## Volume One

The rising of the Yang and the falling of the Yin constitute the respiration <note>original: 呼吸 (huxi)</note> of Heaven and Earth. Heaven and Earth breathe within themselves; for this reason, they are eternal and long-lasting. If a human being can emulate this respiration of Heaven and Earth within their own body, they too can attain a longevity equal to that of Heaven and Earth.

The *Great Collection of the Accomplishment of the Golden Elixir* <note>original: 金丹大成集 (*Jindan Dacheng Ji*), a foundational Yuan-dynasty alchemical compendium.</note> states:
"Exhalation emerges from the Heart and the Lungs; inhalation enters the Kidneys and the Liver. By exhaling, one connects to the Root of Heaven; by inhaling, one connects to the Root of Earth. In exhalation, the dragon chants and the clouds rise; in inhalation, the tiger roars and the winds are born. This respiration of winds and clouds congeals to form the Golden Fluid." <note>The "Golden Fluid" refers to refined saliva or internal essence produced during deep meditation, seen as a precursor to the immortal elixir.</note>

This describes the natural respiration of the "One Qi" <note>The primordial vital energy that precedes the division into Yin and Yang.</note>; it is not the ordinary breathing performed by the mouth and nose.

Li Changyuan's *Primal Treasure Seal of the Primordial Chaos* <note>original: 混元寶章 (*Hunyuan Baozhang*)</note> states:
"Simply at the place of the True Person's <term>True Person (Zhenren): A realized being or immortal. In this context, it refers to the "Inner Self" or the specific energetic center where the alchemical breath originates.</term> respiration, allow the Elegant Maiden <note>original: 姹女 (chanü). An alchemical code name for the spirit or the element of Mercury/Fire, which is volatile and must be "tamed" or "returned" to the center.</note> to fly back and forth."

If a person can empty their heart-mind, concentrate their spirit, and "turn the light inward to illuminate" <note>A classic meditative technique (huiguang neizhao) of directing one's sensory attention away from the external world and toward the internal landscape.</note> the interior, then at the site of the True Person’s respiration, they may follow its rising and falling, accord with its natural rhythm, and preserve it. After a short while, inhalation and exhalation will contain and nourish one another, remaining still and steadfast.

Ordinary people do not direct their awareness to this point; consequently, their respiration does not reside there either.

Zhuangzi’s *True Scripture of Southern Florescence* states:
"The True Person breathes from the heels, while ordinary people breathe from the throat."

When breathing is centered in the throat, it is close to the surface and hurried; such breath is shallow. When it reaches the heels, it is long and continuous; such breath is deep. Through the process of refinement and cultivation <term>Refinement (Xiulian): The practice of purifying the body's internal energies—Essence, Breath, and Spirit—to attain longevity.</term>, when one's breathing becomes truly deep, then "my destiny lies within myself, and not with the Great Smelter." <note>The "Great Smelter" (original: 大冶, daye) refers to the impersonal forces of Nature or Fate that forge and eventually dissolve all living things. "Below the heart and above the kidneys; west of the liver and east of the lungs. It is neither the intestines nor the stomach..." <note>This describes the "Central Palace" or "Yellow Court," the middle point where the various elemental energies of the organs meet.</note>

When Flowing Pearl enters into Water, it congeals to form True Gold. Gold produces Water; therefore, it is said that "Flowing Pearl is the mother of Water." Earth naturally produces Gold, and Gold naturally produces Water. Now, if Earth enters into Water, then the Water is constrained by the Earth. Water is [controlled] by the Earth...

# Four Hundred Words on the Golden Elixir, Complete Book: Volume I

If [Water] is restrained, it cannot carry the Gold and rise upward. Thus it is said: "Water regards Earth as its bane <term>Bane (*gui*): In the Five Elements cycle, this refers to the "overcoming" or "controlling" relationship. Earth acts as a "bane" to Water by damming it or absorbing its flow.</term>; Earth fills Water so it cannot rise." 

If one obtains the Fire of the Vermilion Bird of the South to fiercely cook and intensely refine it, then the Fire will flourish and the Water will boil. The Gold <note>In internal alchemy, "Gold" or "Lead" often represents the True Lead, the foundational energy found within the kidneys (the Kan trigram).</note> will then follow the Water and soar upward. 

For the Vermilion Bird is the essence of Fire in the South; it holds the scales <note>original: 執衡 (*zhiheng*). This refers to the celestial balance, as the Vermilion Bird was thought to govern the balanced heat of the summer season.</note> and governs the Summer. Now, while the Kan-Water <note>original: 坎水 (*Kanshui*). Kan is the trigram representing Water and the North.</note> is being restrained and subdued by the Earth-bane, the Fire is able to cook it and cause it to boil, forcing it to rise upward. Therefore, it is said: "The Vermilion Bird is the essence of Fire; it maintains balance and regulates victory and defeat."

When the Water boils and soars upward, entering the Li-Palace <note>original: 離宮 (*Ligong*). Li is the trigram representing Fire and the South; in the body, it often corresponds to the Heart.</note> of the South, the Li-Fire is in turn overcome by the Kan-Water. When Fire is overcome by Water and extinguished, the Water itself must not be allowed to become too excessive or "overflowing." At this point, Earth is again used to overcome the Water. When Water meets Earth, it is halted. 

Therefore, it is said: "If Water is abundant, Fire is extinguished; both die and return to the Great Earth." When Water and Fire meet Earth, it is called the "Three Natures" <note>original: 三性 (*sanxing*). These are the three essential components—typically associated with the Wood/Fire (Essence/Spirit) and Metal/Water (Breath) groups—brought into harmony by the "Earth" of the practitioner's True Intent.</note>. Once the Three Natures...
