### On the Durability of the Elixir

**Giant Sesame can still prolong one's years; how much more so when the Returned Elixir enters the mouth? The nature of gold does not decay or rot; thus it is the treasure of all things. When practitioners <note>original: 術士 (*shushi*), literally "men of arts," referring here to alchemical practitioners.</note> ingest it, their lifespan becomes long and enduring.**

"Giant Sesame" <note>original: 巨勝 (*jusheng*)</note> refers to black sesame seeds. If even consuming a common plant like sesame can prolong one's life, how much more powerful is the "Golden Liquid Nine-Turned Great Returned Elixir"? This elixir is formed at the moment when the "Golden Essence" <note>In alchemy, this represents the most refined, indestructible spark of true energy or consciousness within the practitioner.</note> is at its peak of vigor. By "stealing" <term>Stealing (*qie*): A common alchemical metaphor for capturing the "True Breath" (Qi) of the universe—the primordial, creative energy—that is usually hidden from ordinary people.</term> the true breath of Heaven and Earth to congeal the Sacred Embryo <term>Sacred Embryo (*shengtai*): The spiritual "self" or immortal body that is cultivated within the practitioner's own torso through the fusion of internal energies.</term>, how could it not grant eternal life? 

Furthermore, the nature of gold is hard and unyielding; it can endure for ten thousand years without decaying. Therefore, it is the ultimate treasure among all things in existence. It is only right that the Divine Immortals prize this above all else. This is also why the elixir is specifically called "Golden," rather than an elixir of Water, Fire, Wood, or Earth <note>The author argues that only Gold represents the necessary permanence and "incorruptibility" required for immortality, unlike the other four elements of the traditional Chinese cycle.</note>.

## Earth wanders through the four seasons, guarding the boundaries and setting the compass and square

Earth has no fixed position of its own, but circulates through the intervals of *Chen*, *Xu*, *Chou*, and *Wei* <note>original: 辰戌丑未. These are the four "Earth" branches of the Chinese zodiac, each corresponding to the final month of a season, acting as a transition.</note>. In *Chen*, it is late spring; in *Wei*, it is late summer; in *Xu*, it is late autumn; and in *Chou*, it is late winter. Spring, summer, autumn, and winter are all domains where Earth wanders; therefore, it is said: "Earth wanders through the four seasons." 

Earth has the power to exhaust water and conceal fire. When it guards water, the water does not leak away; when it guards fire, the fire does not flare up into a wild blaze. In the context of our practice, we use it to guard the position of Kun <term>Kun: The hexagram representing the Receptive/Earth. In internal alchemy, it often refers to the lower "elixir field" where the "medicine" or internal energy is gathered.</term> while gathering the medicine, and to guard the position of Qian <term>Qian: The hexagram representing the Creative/Heaven. Here, it refers to the upper region or the state of pure Yang where the "alchemical fire" is circulated.</term> while moving the fire. Therefore, it is said: "Guarding the boundaries and setting the compass and square" <note>This refers to the Earth element acting as the "True Intent" (Zhenyi) or the "Matchmaker" that provides the structure and boundaries for the alchemical reaction to take place safely.</note>.

## The Golden Sand Ascends to the Cauldron

When the Golden Sand <term>Golden Sand: A metaphor for the refined internal energy or "medicine" (essence and breath) that has been purified through meditation.</term> ascends into the cauldron, it pierces through the two kidneys and is guided through the "Double Pass" along the spine <note>original: 夾脊 (*jiaji*). A crucial energetic gate located along the spine, often considered difficult for the internal energy to penetrate.</note>. It passes through the Heart Meridian and enters the Sea of Marrow <term>Sea of Marrow: A Daoist anatomical term for the brain, which is seen as a reservoir of essence.</term>, then surges through the lung points, crosses the liver, and traverses the spleen before returning once more to the Cinnabar Field <note>original: 丹田 (*dantian*). The "field" or center in the lower abdomen where the elixir is cultivated and stored.</note>. 

At the moment of this ascent, the sensations are billowing, like clouds and mist closing in from all four directions; they are as sudden as the violent arrival of wind and rain. It is as startling as just waking from a daydream, and as liberating as a chronic illness suddenly leaving the body. Spirit and essence merge in obscure union, like the physical joining of husband and wife; bone and flesh harmonize and soften, like the feeling of just stepping out of a warm bath. These are all true internal visions and experiences; they are not mere metaphors. 

*The Chapter on Returning to Destiny* says:

-> "Those who do not know will call it 'Breath'; but once obtained, its natural truth is revealed." <-
<note>original: 不知將謂氣得後自然真. This implies that while the uninitiated might think this is just a matter of deep breathing, the actual alchemical transformation is a profound physical reality.</note>

For unless one has experienced this personally, one cannot truly know it.

This internal steaming and refinement reaches the four limbs; the complexion becomes radiant, moist, and joyful. Grey hair all turns back to black, and teeth grow back in the places where they had fallen out. Old men return to the vigor of their prime; old women become as elegant as young maidens. By transforming the physical form and escaping the calamities of the world, such a person is called a True Person <term>True Person: *Zhenren*, a perfected immortal who has achieved a state of permanent harmony with the Dao and transcended mortality.</term>.

## The Results of Refining the Elixir

After obtaining the elixir, one must warmly nourish <term>warmly nourish: (*wenyang* 溫養) A critical stage in internal alchemy where the practitioner maintains a gentle, steady focus to "incubate" the internal elixir, similar to a bird sitting on an egg.</term> it day and night without allowing any interruption. When this is done, the True Breath <note>original: 真氣 (*zhenqi*). The primordial energy of the body.</note> will steam and permeate throughout the four limbs. Naturally, the spirit will become clear and the breath refreshed; the complexion will turn joyful and lustrous. White hair will return to black, and teeth that have fallen out will grow once more. The old man returns to being a sturdy youth, and the elderly woman becomes again a young maiden <term>young maiden: (*chanu* 姹女) While literally meaning a beautiful girl, in alchemical texts this often serves as a metaphor for refined, "true" Yin energy.</term>. 

From this point on, one transforms the physical form and transcends the world, leaping out of the birdcage <note>A common metaphor in Daoism for the restrictive boundaries of mundane, mortal existence.</note> of the mundane. Such a person is then called a Realized Person <term>Realized Person: (*zhenren* 真人) A Daoist immortal or "True Person" who has achieved union with the Dao and moved beyond the limitations of death.</term>. 

I have often argued that since humans stand alongside Heaven and Earth as the Three Powers <term>Three Powers: (*sancai* 三才) The traditional Chinese cosmological triad of Heaven, Earth, and Humanity, implying that humans are a central pillar of the universe.</term>, it should be possible for us to share the same beginning and end as Heaven and Earth. Yet, why is it that Heaven and Earth are without end, while the life of a human does not even reach one hundred years? 

This is because a human living between Heaven and Earth is merely one object among all other things. Because humans are more "numinous" <note>original: 靈 (*ling*). Refers to spiritual intelligence or being "quick of spirit."</note> than other creatures, they are specifically called "human," but how could they normally expect to stand equal to Heaven and Earth? 

However, if one "seizes the mechanism" of Heaven and Earth <note>A phrase suggesting that the alchemist learns the secret laws of nature and uses them to their advantage.</note> in order to cultivate and complete the Great Elixir of Golden Liquid <note>original: 金液大丹 (*jinyi dadan*). The ultimate goal of alchemy: a refined internal state that grants immortality.</note>, then sharing the same beginning and end as Heaven and Earth is...

<summary>This page describes the physical rejuvenation resulting from the "warm nourishment" of the internal elixir and poses a philosophical question about why humans, despite being one of the Three Powers, do not naturally share the longevity of the universe.</summary>

# Elucidation of the Seal of the Unity of the Three | Volume 1

->**The Seal of the Unity of the Three**<-

One is then called a **Realized Person** <term>Realized Person: (*zhenren* 真人) A Daoist immortal or "True Human" who has achieved a state of perfect harmony with the Dao, transcending the limitations of the physical body and death.</term>. Once one has become a Realized Person, they transcend the mundane and enter the sacred; they no longer possess their former physical shape. They wander freely <note>original: *xiaoyao* (逍遥). A foundational Daoist concept, most famously described by Zhuangzi, referring to a state of absolute spiritual freedom and spontaneous movement.</note> beyond the material realm and forever bid farewell to the hardships of the mortal world.

### The Principle of Categorical Resonance

When white lead powder <note>original: *hufen* (胡粉). Historically, this was a white pigment made from lead; its transformation back into lead in a furnace serves as a metaphor for returning to a primordial state.</note> is cast into the fire, its color is destroyed, and it returns to its original state of molten **lead**. When snow meets a warm bath, it dissolves and is interpreted as the **Great Mystery Gold** <term>Great Mystery Gold: (*taixuan jin* 太玄金) A poetic term for the refined internal elixir, symbolizing the ultimate, incorruptible spiritual essence.</term>.

Using cinnabar <note>original: *sha* (砂). Mercury sulfide, the primary raw material in external alchemy, used here as a metaphor for the "seed" of the internal elixir.</note> as the master, it receives harmony from mercury. Transformation proceeds from its true essence; the beginning and the end are naturally and mutually dependent. If you wish to become an immortal through **ingestion** <term>ingestion: (*fushi* 服食) The practice of "eating" or "consuming" alchemical preparations. In internal alchemy, this refers to swallowing the refined internal energy (the "elixir") into the lower dantian.</term>, you must use substances of the same category <note>original: *tonglei* (同類). This is the "Principle of Categories," asserting that the alchemist must use materials that share a common nature to achieve success.</note>.

To plant grain, one must use millet; to hatch chickens, one must use their own eggs. By using its own kind to assist nature, things are easily forged and refined. 

*   How could a fish’s eye ever become a pearl? 
*   Weeds cannot grow into a fine catalpa tree <note>original: *jia* (檟). A high-quality wood used for making fine furniture or tea, representing superior potential.</note>.

Those of the same kind follow one another; if things are mismatched, no treasure can be formed. Therefore:

*   Swallows and sparrows do not give birth to phoenixes.
*   Foxes and rabbits do not nurse horses.
*   Water does not flow upward toward the heat.
*   Fire does not move downward to moisten.

<note>This section emphasizes that the internal elixir cannot be made from "lowly" or "foreign" materials. Just as nature follows strict categories—fire rising and water falling—the alchemist must use the "True Lead" and "True Mercury" found within the human body rather than external, unrelated substances.</note>

<summary>This page defines the "Realized Person" as one who has transcended the physical world and details the alchemical law of "categorical resonance," arguing that the elixir can only be created by combining substances of the same natural kind.</summary>
<keywords>Realized Person (Zhenren), Categorical Resonance (Tonglei), Cinnabar, Mercury, Great Mystery Gold, Internal Alchemy, Natural Law</keywords>

--- Page 122 ---
<meta>This page continues the explanation of "categorical resonance"—the alchemical law that substances must belong to the same natural category to affect one another—by providing physical examples like lead and snow to illustrate the concept of "returning to the source."</meta>

->Imperial Edition of the Complete Library in Four Sections | Volume 1<-

White lead powder <note>original: *hufen* (胡粉). A pigment produced by the chemical transformation of lead. In alchemy, its ability to revert to lead symbolizes the potential to return any substance to its primordial state.</note> is originally created by firing black lead; if one throws it back into a fire, it reverts once more into lead. Ice and snow are formed by the congealing of **Yin** energy; if one denches them with warm water, they dissolve and transform back into water. Why is this? It is because the principle of "returning to the root and reverting to the source" <note>original: *fanben huanyuan* (返本還源). A central Daoist tenet suggesting that spiritual cultivation is a process of reversing aging and decay to return to the state of the primordial Dao.</note> cannot be otherwise.

The method of refining the **Internal Gold** <term>Internal Gold: (*lianjin* 煉金) Here referring to the refinement of the "Golden Elixir" within the body, rather than the literal creation of metal.</term> takes cinnabar as its primary agent, which is then harmonized with mercury. Because mercury is born from within cinnabar, they are substances of the same category. The reason the beginning and the end are mutually dependent and able to produce transformation is entirely due to their **True Essence**.

The *Chapters on Awakening to the Truth* <note>original: *Wuzhen pian* (悟真篇). One of the most influential texts of Internal Alchemy, written by Zhang Boduan in the 11th century.</note> states:

"If a bamboo pole is broken, you must use bamboo to repair it;
To hatch a chicken, one must use an egg.
Working with ten thousand things of different categories is a mere waste of effort;
How can that compare to **True Lead** <term>True Lead: (*zhenqian* 真鉛) In internal alchemy, this represents the "True Yang" hidden within the "Yin" of the body, often associated with the kidneys or the breath.</term> joining with the **Sacred Mechanism**?" <term>Sacred Mechanism: (*shengji* 聖機) The pivotal point of cosmic timing or the hidden workings of the Dao that allow for spiritual transformation.</term>

This means that when **True Mercury** <term>True Mercury: (*zhengong* 真汞) Represents the "True Yin" hidden within "Yang," associated with the heart or the spirit.</term> obtains True Lead, the energies of **Yin** and **Yang** resonate because they belong to the same category. It is just like planting grain: one must use its own seeds. Or like hatching a chicken: one must use its own egg. When things follow their own kind, then...

<summary>Yu Yan explains the principle of "categorical resonance," arguing that spiritual transformation requires using the "True" versions of internal Yin and Yang, just as physical materials like lead or ice return to their original forms when heated.</summary>
<keywords>Categorical Resonance, True Lead, True Mercury, Returning to the Source, Wuzhen pian, Cinnabar, Yin and Yang</keywords>

--- Page 123 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse on "categorical resonance" (tonglei), expanding from physical examples of lead and water to biological and elemental impossibilities to prove that alchemical transformation requires substances of the same nature.</meta>

[By Sima Chengzhen] <note>Sima Chengzhen (647–735) was a high-ranking Daoist patriarch of the Shangqing school. His name here suggests this commentary or section follows his lineage of thought.</note>

This Way is natural; it completes its transformations without toiling or straining one's strength. If one were to abandon things of the same category and instead seek out other substances, it would be as futile as trying to turn a fish eye into a pearl, or expecting common mugwort and fleabane to grow into a fine catalpa tree. <note>The "fish eye" is a classic Chinese metaphor for a worthless imitation of a precious pearl, while mugwort represents common weeds compared to the catalpa, a noble wood used for fine carving.</note> Since their categories are not the same, how could any transformation occur? 

It is also like a swallow giving birth to a swallow, or a sparrow giving birth to a sparrow—if one commanded them to give birth to a phoenix, they could not do so. A fox nurses foxes, and a rabbit nurses rabbits—if one commanded them to nurse a horse, they could not do so. The nature of fire is to flame upward; can one make it moisten downward? The nature of water is to moisten downward; can one make it flame upward? 

Therefore, if one wishes to become an **Immortal** through the practice of **Ingestion** <term>Ingestion: (*fushi* 服食) The ancient practice of consuming refined minerals, herbs, or internal breaths to achieve spiritual transcendence.</term>, one should rely on things of the same category. When those of the same category are brought together to work with one another, there is nothing that cannot be turned into a treasure. 

However, my own body possesses its own **Heaven and Earth** and its own **Yin and Yang**. Yet the *Chapters on Returning to the Source* <note>original: *Huanyuan pian* (還源篇). A text attributed to the Southern Lineage master Shi Tai, emphasizing the internal nature of the alchemical ingredients.</note> says: "Seize the marrow of the other **Heaven and Earth**; in a brief moment of **Conjunction** <term>Conjunction: (*jiaogou* 交姤) The mystical union of the internal Yin and Yang, often described in sexual or marital metaphors.</term>, one must steal the **Qi** <term>Qi: (*qi* 氣) The primordial breath or life force that circulates through the body and the cosmos.</term> of **Heaven and Earth**."

<summary>This page argues that alchemical transformation is only possible between substances of the same natural category, using biological metaphors to show that things cannot produce what is not in their nature. It concludes by introducing the idea of stealing the "marrow" of the universe's energy during internal meditation.</summary>

<keywords>categorical resonance, Sima Chengzhen, Ingestion, Immortality, Yin and Yang, Qi, Conjunction, internal alchemy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the argument that the "same category" (tonglei) required for alchemy is not found in external substances or other people, but within the internal alignment of the practitioner's own vital breaths. # Imperial Edition of the Complete Library of the Four Treasuries: Volume One

...The breath of the **Sun** and **Moon** serves as the **Holy Father** and **Spirit Mother**. Conversely, the breath within my own physical body is merely the product of bone and blood—the breath of mortal parents. Therefore, one must use the breath of the **True Parents** <note>original: "聖父靈母" (Holy Father and Spirit Mother). In this context, they refer to the cosmic Yin and Yang (Sun and Moon) rather than biological parents.</note> to transform this physical body inherited from mortal parents. By doing so, one becomes a **True Immortal** of **Pure Yang** <term>Pure Yang: (*chunyang* 純陽) A state of being entirely free of dense, "heavy" Yin energy, representing the spiritual perfection of an Immortal.</term>, achieving a lifespan equal to that of **Heaven and Earth**.

However, some people are deluded in their search for "things of the same category." They do not realize that they themselves are identical in nature to **Heaven and Earth** <note>original: "乾坤" (Qian and Kun). These are the first two hexagrams of the *I Ching*, representing the pure creative and receptive forces of the universe.</note>. Instead, they look outside of their own bodies to find this "category." They falsely identify young women as the **True Lead** <term>True Lead: (*zhen qian* 真鉛) Usually a metaphor for the primordial spark of life (Yang) within the body, here being misinterpreted as a literal external source.</term>. They then invoke the legends of the **Yellow Emperor** and the **Dark and Plain Ladies** <note>The "Xuan-Su" (玄素) refers to the Dark Lady and the Plain Lady, mythological figures who supposedly taught the Yellow Emperor sexual techniques for longevity. Yu Yan is criticizing those who use these legends to justify "bedroom arts."</note> to dress up their wicked theories and cater to the lustful desires of high-ranking officials.

Because of this, many wealthy families and noble bureaucrats who practice these arts frequently meet with untimely deaths. This was not limited to the Tang Dynasty Chancellor **Xiahou Zi** alone. <note>Xiahou Zi was a high-ranking Tang official who was infamous for his devotion to these "sexual arts," which were widely blamed for his physical decline.</note>

For this reason, **Ge Zhichuan** <note>The courtesy name of Ge Hong (283–343), author of the *Baopuzi* (The Master Who Embraces Simplicity).</note> wrote in his *Baopuzi* that such practices are like trying to carry boiling soup in a cup made of ice, or attempting to store fire in a bundle of feathers. Furthermore, **Tao Yinju** <note>The "Recluse of Huayang," Tao Hongjing (456–536), a key figure in the Shangqing school of Daoism.</note> stated in his *Declarations of the Perfected* that these errors are like "embracing a piece of jade while leaping into a fire," or like "golden coffins and straw dogs"—things that may look precious or ritualistic but lead only to destruction or are discarded once their temporary use is over. 

<summary>Yu Yan clarifies that true alchemical transformation uses cosmic breaths (Sun/Moon) to refine the mortal body, and issues a stern warning against "sexual alchemy" (bedroom arts), citing historical failures and the warnings of great masters like Ge Hong and Tao Hongjing.</summary>

# Elucidation of the Seal of the Unity of the Three: Volume One

...The low acts of these people are no better than the behavior of **dogs and pigs**. The Tang dynasty poet **Li Yuxi** <note>original: "李玉谿" (Li Yuxi), the courtesy name of Li Shangyin (c. 813–858). He was known for his complex poetry and interest in Daoism.</note> famously labeled such practitioners "seeds of hell." This is precisely why, from ancient times to the present, all authentic **alchemical scriptures** <note>original: "丹書" (*danshu*), books concerning the Elixir.</note> condemn these techniques with the utmost vehemence. These practices harm others and, in the end, the practitioners destroy their own bodies as well.

The **Divine Scripture of Southern Florescence** <note>original: "南華真經" (*Nanhua Zhenjing*), the honorific title for the *Zhuangzi*, a foundational Daoist text.</note> states: "Do not toil your physical form, and do not agitate your **essence** <term>Essence: (*jing* 精) The refined, foundational energy of the physical body; in Daoist practice, it must be preserved and transformed rather than depleted.</term>; only then can you achieve long life." I have never heard of any legitimate path involving so-called **sexual techniques** <note>original: "御女之說" (*yunü zhi shuo*), literally "theories of managing women," a euphemism for sexual practices intended to "plunder" energy.</note>. Who was it that first created this "stairway to calamity"? It remains a major hindrance to this day, defiling the reputation of the **Former Sages** and leading later students into deep delusion. Among all the "side doors" and diverse arts, this is surely the most harmful to the **True Way**.

In this world, there are many scholars of high standing and great talent who, unfortunately, never encounter a **true master**. They rely solely on their own cleverness to interpret the texts, following the literal words to invent their own misguided methods. They waste their efforts <note>original: "耗火" (wasting the fire), a metaphor for wasting time and life-energy.</note> and exhaust their wealth and property. Because they lack the **true transmission** <note>original: "端緒" (the clue or starting thread), referring to the oral instructions needed to begin practice.</note>, they have no basis for their work; they lose all sense of proportion and cannot maintain the necessary discipline. They spend their lives pounding and processing:

*   **Blue Vitriol** <term>Blue Vitriol: (*shidan* 石膽) Copper sulfate, a bright blue mineral used in external alchemy.</term>
*   **Mica** <note>A silicate mineral that peels into thin, shimmering layers.</note>
*   **Alum and Magnetite**
*   **Sulfur**

They burn **Yuzhang Clay** <note>A specific type of fine clay used to seal crucibles to prevent the escape of vapors.</note> and attempt to refine **mercury**, heating and smelting the substances in the forge to make them "fly" <note>original: "飛" (*fei*), referring to sublimation, where a solid turns into vapor and reforms as a purified solid.</note>...

<summary>Yu Yan forcefully condemns sexual alchemy as a "seed of hell" that contradicts the Zhuangzi’s teachings on preserving energy. He then critiques "literary" scholars who bankrupt themselves attempting complex chemical experiments without secret oral instructions.</summary>

# Elucidation of the Seal of the Unity of the Three: Volume One

## Correct Faith in the Golden Elixir: Author's Preface

...If mineral and copper are used as the catalysts, their diverse natures are not of the same kind; how then could they ever agree to fuse into a single body? A thousand such attempts will surely lead to ten thousand failures. Wishing to be clever, the practitioner instead becomes foolish. Relying on mere luck, they never meet a true master.

The **Sage** <note>original: "聖人" (*shengren*). In this context, it refers to the legendary authors of alchemical wisdom, such as Wei Boyang.</note> alone knows this truth. Some practice until their hair turns white, yet in the middle of the path, they begin to harbor doubts. Turning their backs on the **Way** <term>The Way: (*Dao* 道) The fundamental principle of the universe and the goal of all alchemical transformation.</term>, they cling to lost paths; leaving the correct road, they enter deviant tracks. Their view is as narrow as looking through a reed—they lack broad vision and find it difficult to estimate what is to come.

The *Folios on Understanding Reality* <note>original: "悟真篇" (*Wuzhen pian*), a seminal 11th-century text on internal alchemy by Zhang Boduan.</note> says:

"Even if your intelligence surpasses that of **Yan and Min** <note>original: "顏閔" (Yan Min), referring to Yan Hui and Min Ziqian, the most virtuous and brilliant disciples of Confucius.</note>, do not engage in forced guesswork if you have not met a **True Person** <term>True Person: (*Zhenren* 真人) A Daoist immortal or a fully realized master who has achieved union with the Dao.</term>. It is simply because the alchemical scriptures do not contain the **oral instructions** <note>original: "口訣" (*koujue*), the "pith instructions" or secret keys passed verbally from teacher to student to unlock the metaphors in written texts.</note>; where else then could you learn to form the **Spirit Embryo**?" <term>Spirit Embryo: (*Lingtai* 靈胎) In internal alchemy, this represents the crystallization of one's refined essence and spirit, signifying the birth of a new, immortal self within the practitioner.</term>

Truly, in this world, there is no shortage of high-talented and studious men, but those who encounter a **True Master** and receive the **correct transmission** are few indeed. There are those who burn and refine medicines made of the **Three Yellows and Four Divinities** <note>original: "三黃四神" (*sanhuang sishen*). "Three Yellows" typically refers to sulfur, realgar, and orpiment; "Four Divinities" refers to four specific mineral catalysts (often mercury, lead, etc.). These are materials of external laboratory alchemy.</note>, delusionally believing that the Way lies in these substances. Little do they know that the **Five**... <note>The text likely continues to discuss the Five Elements (*Wuxing*).</note>

**Gold and the eight minerals** <note>original: "金八石" (*jin ba shi*). This refers to the 'Great Returned Elixir,' the final stage of alchemical refinement where the spirit returns to its original state of purity.</note>. They imagine that external alchemy can be transformed into the internal medicine, but in truth, metals and stones have no relation to it."

For the **Golden Fluid Great Return Elixir** of the immortals is the ultimate medicine that is "born from nothingness" <note>original: "無中生有" (*wu zhong sheng you*). A Daoist principle suggesting that the spiritual elixir does not come from physical matter, but from the void of the Dao.</note>. What are called "cinnabar" and "**mercury**" <term>Mercury: (*shuiyin* 水銀) Also called 'quicksilver.' In internal alchemy, it represents the 'True Yin' or the fluid essence of the mind (thought/intent).</term> are merely established images and metaphors. 

Alas, the people of the world do not recognize **True Lead** <term>True Lead: (*zhen qian* 真鉛) A metaphor for the primordial vitality (Qi) or the true essence found within the body, which is the actual 'ingredient' for internal alchemy.</term>. They take ordinary cinnabar and mercury, often wasting fuel and squandering their wealth, yet in the end, they achieve no success. Consequently, they reach old age in a state of total bewilderment, and conversely...

I begin with a sigh for the Void. Alas! The **True Tao** <term>True Tao: (*zhendao* 真道) The authentic path of spiritual cultivation, which Yu Yan argues is found within the self rather than in external chemical experiments.</term> is simple and not complex, yet the ultimate words are bland and flavorless. Who among people believes it? Who among people practices it? Even some who obtain the correct transmission develop doubts halfway through their journey; thus, there are those who depart from the correct path and enter into heterodox bypasses.

As for the Supreme Sages, none surpass **Fuxi** <note>The legendary first sovereign of ancient China, credited with creating the fundamental symbols of Chinese philosophy.</note>, who first drew the **Eight Trigrams** <term>Eight Trigrams: (*bagua* 八卦) Eight symbols consisting of three lines (broken or solid) used to represent the fundamental principles of reality and the flow of Change.</term> by modeling them after Heaven and Earth. **King Wen** <note>The founder of the Zhou Dynasty, who expanded the Trigrams into the sixty-four hexagrams while imprisoned.</note>, the ancestor of emperors, followed this lineage and elaborated the line statements. The Master [Confucius], the hero among many sages, provided the **Ten Wings** <term>Ten Wings: (*shiyi* 十翼) A collection of philosophical commentaries on the *I Ching* (Book of Changes) traditionally attributed to Confucius, which "gave wings" to the original core text.</term> to support it. These three lords were raised up by Heaven; they rose in succession and governed according to their times. While there were differences in their specific steps and progression, their **merit and virtue** <note>original: "功德" (*gongde*). A term denoting both the inner spiritual achievement and the outward benefit a sage provides to the world.</note> were no different. Their creations followed in one another's footsteps, calculating and measuring...

<meta>This page continues the introduction to Yu Yan's commentary. Having established the "True Tao" as internal and simple on the previous page, Yu Yan now begins a historical and cosmological lineage, grounding the "Seal of the Unity of the Three" in the authority of the ancient sages who created the I Ching.</meta>

->周易參同<-
->*Penned by Wu Yan*<-

# Record of Examining the Divisions

**Fuxi** <note>The legendary first sovereign of China, credited with discovering the patterns of nature.</note> followed the patterns of Heaven and Earth to create the *Changes* (*I Ching*), first drawing the **Eight Trigrams** <term>Eight Trigrams: (*bagua* 八卦) Eight symbols consisting of three lines, either broken (Yin) or solid (Yang), representing the fundamental building blocks of reality.</term>. In his **Pre-Heaven Diagram** <term>Pre-Heaven: (*xiantian* 先天) The primordial, ideal arrangement of the universe before time and change began.</term>, the **Heaven** trigram (Qian) is at the top and the **Earth** trigram (Kun) is at the bottom; **Fire** (Li, representing the Sun) and **Water** (Kan, representing the Moon) are arrayed in the East and West; **Thunder** (Zhen), **Wind** (Xun), **Mountain** (Gen), and **Lake** (Dui) occupy the four corners. Could it be that this occurred without following a specific natural order?

**King Wen** <note>The founder of the Zhou Dynasty, who expanded the trigrams into the sixty-four hexagrams while imprisoned.</note> followed the forms of the trigrams to develop the *Changes*, and thus composed the written judgments. In his **Post-Heaven Diagram** <term>Post-Heaven: (*houtian* 後天) The arrangement of the world in its state of constant motion, seasonal cycles, and physical transformation.</term>, the two Wood elements—**Thunder** (Zhen) and **Wind** (Xun)—give birth to **Fire** (Li), which is then followed by **Earth** (Kun) to store it; the two Metal elements—**Lake** (Dui) and **Heaven** (Qian)—give birth to **Water** (Kan), which is then followed by **Earth** (Gen) to bring it to a halt. Could it be that this, too, occurred without following a specific natural order?

**Confucius** followed in the footsteps of Fuxi and King Wen to extol the *Changes*, and subsequently composed the **Ten Wings** <term>Ten Wings: (*shiyi* 十翼) A collection of ten commentaries traditionally attributed to Confucius that explain the metaphysical and ethical depths of the *I Ching*.</term>. Although he held no rank of Emperor or King during his own time, he has served as the Teacher for Emperors and Kings for ten thousand generations. This is the lineage of the various sages...

<summary>Yu Yan traces the cosmological evolution of the I Ching from the primordial "Pre-Heaven" arrangement of Fuxi to the "Post-Heaven" cycle of King Wen and the ethical commentaries of Confucius.</summary>
<keywords>Fuxi, King Wen, Confucius, Pre-Heaven, Post-Heaven, Eight Trigrams, Ten Wings, I Ching, Five Elements</keywords>

--- Page 130 ---
<meta>This page concludes the historical lineage of the I Ching’s development, linking the ancient sages Fuxi, King Wen, and Confucius to Wei Boyang, the author of the Cantong Qi. It establishes the "Seal of the Unity of the Three" as a work that follows the rigorous structural precedents of the ancient classics.</meta>

->**Imperially Commissioned Complete Library of the Four Treasuries, Volume One**<-

[...] is the masculine one. These three gentlemen <note>Referring to Fuxi, King Wen, and Confucius, mentioned collectively as the authors of the three layers of the I Ching.</note> are all great sages of antiquity. **Fuxi** was born in the distant, primordial past; **King Wen** was born at the end of the Shang Dynasty; **Confucius** was born during the late Zhou Dynasty. Although they rose to prominence in different eras to govern their times, and their methods of advancement may have varied in degree, if one compares their achievements and virtues, the former and later sages follow the same standard. They are fundamentally no different from one another. 

Furthermore, regarding the drawing of the **Eight Trigrams** <term>Eight Trigrams: (*bagua* 八卦) The fundamental symbols representing natural forces like Heaven, Earth, Fire, and Water.</term>, the expansion of the **Line Statements** <term>Line Statements: (*yaoci* 爻辭) The poetic and divinatory texts associated with each individual line of a hexagram.</term>, and the composition of the **Ten Wings** <term>Ten Wings: (*shiyi* 十翼) A collection of commentaries on the *I Ching*, traditionally attributed to Confucius, which explain its philosophical and cosmological meaning.</term>, there is not a single idle word in any chapter, nor a single idle character in any word. Their measurement of importance and nuance was conducted with extreme scrutiny. This is because their creative works were always based on the foundations laid by those who came before.

Now, Lord Wei <note>Wei Boyang (approx. 2nd century CE), the semi-legendary alchemist and author of the source text, the *Cantong qi*.</note> composed this book, the ***Seal of the Unity of the Three***, and he found it necessary to rely on the hexagram imagery of the ***Changes*** (*I Ching*) to illuminate his teachings. This can truly be described as building upon the work of his predecessors.

->That which has form is easy to measure,<-
->But that which lacks omens is difficult to contemplate.<-
->In undertaking any task, one now has a model to follow,<-
->For he has established this book for the sake of the world.<-

<summary>Yu Yan argues that the author of the Cantong Qi, Wei Boyang, followed the tradition of the ancient sages by using the structural logic of the I Ching to explain the mysteries of alchemy. He positions the text as a definitive, reliable guide for the world.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the introduction to the text, transitioning from the historical lineage of the Three Sages to the specific necessity of Lord Wei’s written work. It emphasizes that while the "Golden Elixir" is formless and beyond ordinary logic, the written word—guided by the lineage of masters—is the essential map for future seekers.</meta>

# Elucidation of the Seal of the Unity of the Three
## Volume One

->First Section<-

Without the benefit of innate knowledge, one depends entirely upon the enlightenment provided by a master. This realization is as clear as pulling back a heavy curtain, or opening one’s eyes wide to gaze out from a high tower.

Matters that possess physical form and visible omens can be measured and planned for; however, the **Golden Liquid Great Elixir** <term>Golden Liquid Great Elixir: (*jinyue taidan* 金液太丹) The ultimate substance of immortality in internal alchemy, representing the refined union of spirit and energy.</term> is a matter without form or omens. It cannot be measured or planned through ordinary calculation. If it were not expressed through words, how would later generations have any method to follow? This is precisely why the *Seal of the Unity of the Three* was composed. 

Yet, regarding the composition of the *Seal of the Unity of the Three*, could it be that Lord Wei <note>Wei Boyang, the legendary 2nd-century author of the *Cantong Qi*.</note> was simply putting forth his own private opinions? He did nothing more than record the teachings transmitted to him by his own master. The ***Scripture of Western Ascension*** <term>Scripture of Western Ascension: (*Xisheng jing* 西升經) A classic Taoist scripture detailing the departure of Laozi to the West and his teachings on the Dao.</term> says: "If a student does not find an **Enlightened Master** <term>Enlightened Master: (*mingshi* 明師) A teacher who has personally realized the truth and can provide direct guidance beyond the literal text.</term>, how can they resolve their doubts and difficulties?" 

Even for a single mundane craft or worldly technique, one must undergo trials and seek out a teacher before grasping its essence. How much more so for this **Golden Liquid Great Elixir**, which is the path to **Longevity and Transcendence** <term>Longevity and Transcendence: (*changsheng chaotuo* 長生超脫) The dual goal of Taoist practice: to preserve physical health (longevity) and ultimately liberate the spirit from the cycle of birth and death (transcendence).</term>? Should not students humble their hearts and travel to visit teachers, hoping for a fated encounter?

<summary>This page argues that the formless secrets of alchemy require both a written tradition and an enlightened teacher to be understood. It justifies the existence of the text as a necessary map for a path that cannot be navigated by ordinary logic alone.</summary>
<keywords>Golden Liquid Great Elixir, Enlightened Master, Scripture of Western Ascension, Wei Boyang, oral transmission, transcendence, alchemical pedagogy</keywords>

# Imperial Edition of the Complete Library in the Four Branches of Literature
## Volume One

Ge Zhichuan <note>Ge Hong (283–343 CE), the famous Jin Dynasty scholar and author of the *Baopuzi*.</note> states in his *Biographies of the Immortals*: "Liu Zheng sought the arts of **nurturing life** <term>nurturing life: (*yangsheng* 養生) A tradition of health and longevity practices involving meditation, breathwork, and diet.</term>. He diligently sought out unusual knowledge, never considering a thousand miles too far to travel. If there was anyone superior to himself, even if they were a servant or a guest, he would surely serve them as a master." 

If people today could truly be like Master Liu and not be ashamed to ask those below them, then true immortals would naturally arrive at their door to reveal the secrets of the **Cinnabar Cave** <term>Cinnabar Cave: (*danxue* 丹穴) A metaphor for the secret location of the elixir within the body or the core mystery of the alchemical process.</term>. Once one receives the **oral instructions** <term>oral instructions: (*koujue* 口訣) Esoteric teachings passed verbally from master to student to explain the "blind spots" in written texts.</term>, all doubts are completely dispelled. It is as bright as pulling back a heavy curtain to remove what was hidden, and as vast as climbing a high tower and gazing out over the distant wilderness. To have one’s heart and eyes opened and enlightened in this way—is it not a great joy?

The six hundred chapters of the *Fire Records* <note>The *Fire Records* (*Huoji* 火記) are ancient, likely lost, alchemical texts that detail the timing of the alchemical process.</note> all lead toward the same goal.

The *Chapters on Awakening to the Truth* <note>The *Wuzhen pian*, a foundational 11th-century text of internal alchemy by Zhang Boduan.</note> says:

->Treatises, classics, and songs speak of the ultimate truth,<-
->Yet they do not commit the **fire-phasing** to writing.<- <term>fire-phasing: (*huohou* 火候) The specific timing and intensity of the internal "heat" or focus required during meditation to refine the elixir.</term>
->If you wish to know the oral instructions for the mysterious points,<-
->You must discuss them in detail with the immortals.<-

Later students do not realize the **true fish-trap** <term>true fish-trap: (*zhenquan* 真筌) A reference to the *Zhuangzi*; the trap is the means to catch the fish (truth). Once the truth is found, the trap (the words) can be discarded.</term>; they see only...

<summary>This page emphasizes the necessity of humility and a master's oral instruction in the study of internal alchemy, specifically regarding the "fire-phasing" which is never fully committed to text. It cites the example of Liu Zheng and the "Chapters on Awakening to the Truth" to reinforce that scriptures are merely tools to reach a truth that must be activated by a teacher.</summary>

# Elucidation of the Seal of the Unity of the Three

Regarding the tradition of the six hundred chapters of the **Fire Records** <note>The *Fire Records* (*Huoji* 火記) are ancient, now-lost alchemical texts that provided schedules for heating the elixir.</note>, there are none who are not bewildered and at a loss, believing that **fire-phasing** <term>fire-phasing: (*huohou* 火候) The precise timing and regulation of "heat"—whether literal fire in external alchemy or breath and intention in internal alchemy—to transform the ingredients.</term> is incredibly complex and difficult. They do not realize that the six hundred chapters are simply like the sixty **hexagrams** <term>hexagrams: (*gua* 卦) The six-lined symbols from the *I Ching* (Book of Changes) used here to represent different stages of a temporal cycle.</term>. Sixty hexagrams constitute the timing for one month; six hundred chapters are the timing for ten months. Because one month uses sixty hexagrams, and each hexagram follows the same principle, the six hundred chapters of the ten months are all similar to one another. Therefore, it is said: "The destination they aim for is the same and no different."

***

These words are solemn and weighty, yet the people of the world do not reflect deeply upon them nor seek out their source and stream. The hidden and the manifest originally dwell together. While I might privately speak of this for the sake of the worthy, how would I dare lightly commit it to a book? I wish to bind my tongue and say nothing, yet to allow the Way to be cut off is a crime deserving of punishment. To write my feelings upon bamboo and silk, however, brings the fear of leaking the **Heavenly tally** <term>Heavenly tally: (*tianfu* 天符) A secret celestial mechanism or divine mandate. Revealing it to the "unworthy" was traditionally thought to bring spiritual retribution.</term>. Hesitating, my sighs increase; looking up and down, I constantly reflect. The **kiln and forge** <term>kiln and forge: (*taoye* 陶冶) A metaphor for the process of spiritual or physical refinement, likening the alchemist to a potter or blacksmith shaping raw material into a vessel.</term> has... <note>original: "陶冶有..." The sentence is interrupted by the page break; it likely continues to describe the creative or transformative power of the Dao.</note>

<summary>Yu Yan explains that the "six hundred chapters" of alchemical timing are simpler than they appear, representing a repeating monthly cycle. He then expresses his moral dilemma regarding whether to keep these transformative secrets hidden or risk divine punishment by publishing them.</summary>

## Part One

*I cannot bear to fully detail every regulation and method,*
*So I have briefly outlined the main principles;*
*The branches and twigs can now be seen spreading out.*

From ancient times to the present, **alchemical books** <term>alchemical books: (*danshu* 丹書) Literally "cinnabar books," these are texts concerning the preparation of the Elixir of Immortality, whether through laboratory chemistry or internal meditative cultivation.</term> have been so numerous that they "make the oxen sweat and fill the house to the rafters." <note>This is a classic Chinese idiom (*hanniu chongdong* 汗牛充棟) used to describe a massive library that would exhaust a pack animal to carry.</note> With thousands of titles and tens of thousands of characters, they employ endless metaphors and analogies. How could one not call this a weighty matter? 

However, if one seeks out their source and follows their stream, they are nothing more than **Yin and Yang** <term>Yin and Yang: (陰陽) The two fundamental, complementary forces of the universe—the dark and light, receptive and active, feminine and masculine.</term>. These two arise from the same source but have different names. The ancient immortals "poured out their hearts and spilled their gall" <note>original: "傾肝瀝膽" (qinggan lidan). This idiom means to speak with absolute sincerity and reveal one’s innermost secrets.</note> to inform the people of the world. Yet, because the people’s "karmic blessings and connections" are shallow, they do not give themselves the leisure to reflect deeply on these matters.

Now, **Lord Wei** <note>Wei Boyang (魏伯陽), the 2nd-century author of the *Seal of the Unity of the Three*, often called the "Ancestor of Alchemical Texts."</note> composed this book to point out the origins of the **cauldron and vessel** <term>cauldron and vessel: (*dingqi* 鼎器) The apparatus used for alchemy. In "internal" alchemy, this refers to the practitioner's own body and the energetic centers used to "cook" the elixir.</term>, the sources of the **medicinal ingredients** <term>medicinal ingredients: (*yaowu* 藥物) The substances transformed in the alchemical process, such as lead and mercury, or internally, the "Three Treasures" of Essence, Breath, and Spirit.</term>, and the regulations of **fire-phasing** <term>fire-phasing: (*huohou* 火候) The precise timing and intensity of the heat—or the intensity of one's meditative focus—required at different stages of the process.</term>. He also covers the secrets of existence and non-existence, host and guest, and the sequence of what comes before and after. Every word is thorough and every character is solid. This work was established for the sake of the worthy people of the world; how could he have been willing to write such a work lightly, merely to "fish for a hollow reputation"? One must understand that this book was written for those who are enlightened...

<summary>Yu Yan argues that despite the overwhelming number of alchemical texts, the core of the Great Work is simply Yin and Yang. He praises the original author, Wei Boyang, for providing a sincere and practical guide for serious practitioners.</summary>

<unclear>In ancient</unclear> times, when things were passed down, those with understanding valued them. However, a farmer might obtain a **crimson bow** <note>original: "彤弓" (tonggong). A ceremonial bow typically granted by an emperor to a high official as a sign of merit; here it represents a precious object used for a trivial task.</note> only to use it to drive away birds, or a man from the south might obtain a **grass raincoat** only to use it to cushion the firewood on his back. **The Great Way of the Golden Elixir** <term>Golden Elixir: (*jindan* 金丹) In internal alchemy, this refers to the perfected state of one's internal energies, synthesized into a "pill" of immortality.</term> is such that only the worthy can truly delight in it. This is not something I, though possessing knowledge, should speak of too much.

The *Sublime Mysterious Regulation of the Most High* <note>original: "太上玄科" (Taishang Xuanke). A reference to celestial laws or codes of conduct governing the transmission of esoteric knowledge.</note> says:

> To meet the right person and not transmit the teaching is to lose the Way of Heaven;
> To recklessly transmit to the wrong person is to leak the treasures of Heaven.
> Transmitting to the right person brings merit to one’s own body;
> Recklessly transmitting causes seven generations of ancestors to suffer the trials of the underworld.

Therefore, **Lord Wei** <note>Refers to Wei Boyang (魏伯陽), the Han-dynasty author of the *Seal of the Unity of the Three*.</note> was on the verge of "tying his tongue" and remaining silent. Yet, he feared that if he did not speak a single word, the **lineage of the Tao** <term>lineage of the Tao: (*daomai* 道脉) The "pulse" or continuous transmission of spiritual truth from master to student.</term> would be severed, and he would be guilty of a great failing. But if he wrote his feelings upon bamboo and silk, he feared he would leak the **Heavenly Tally** <term>Heavenly Tally: (*tianfu* 天符) A metaphor for the secret mandates or patterns of the universe that are usually hidden from the uninitiated.</term> and receive divine punishment.

He thus hesitated and sighed repeatedly, looking up and down in deep contemplation. He originally desired to speak clearly and directly to inform students, but he truly did not dare to lightly leak the **true mechanism** <note>original: "真機" (zhenji). The subtle, essential spark of the alchemical process.</note>. Consequently, he tentatively described the general outline of the **medicinal ingredients** and the **firing process** <term>firing process: (*huohou* 火候) Literally "fire timing," this refers to the precise schedule and intensity of heat (or meditative focus) required to transform the elixir.</term> so that later generations might grasp the meaning through his words.

<summary>Yu Yan explains Wei Boyang's struggle: he feared both the karmic debt of hiding the Way and the divine punishment for revealing it. Ultimately, Wei chose to write in metaphors, focusing on ingredients and timing to guide the worthy without explicitly leaking the "true mechanism."</summary>

...that perhaps this Way would not perish from its transmission. Having not dared to speak of it openly, yet unable to bear keeping it secret in silence, he could not avoid lifting its **grand outline** <term>grand outline: (*honggang* 宏綱) the overarching principles or primary structure of the alchemical system.</term> and grasping its **essential pivot** <term>essential pivot: (*jiyao* 機要) the crucial "trigger" or mechanism that makes the internal transformation possible.</term> in order to **smelt and mold** <term>smelt and mold: (*taoye* 陶冶) literally the work of a potter or smith; here it refers to the cultivation of the self according to alchemical laws.</term> the regulations, scattering them throughout the three chapters like the **luxuriant spreading** <term>luxuriant spreading: (*fushu* 扶疎) the graceful way tree branches extend; here it describes how the complex details of the teaching are organized but hidden in the text.</term> of branches.

Use Gold as the embankment, that the Water may enter and flow freely.
The count of Gold is fifteen, and the number of Water is also thus.
Approaching the furnace, determine the **weights** <term>weights: (*zhuliang* 銖兩) ancient Chinese units of measurement; a *zhu* was 1/24th of a tael/ounce.</term>: five parts of water with two left over.
The two are taken as True Gold, heavy as it was at the beginning.
Thereupon the Earth does not enter, but joins with the two.
The three substances contain and receive one another, changing and transforming with a spirit-like state.

***

"Use Gold as the embankment, that the Water may enter and flow freely" means: use the half-pound of Gold from the **first quarter moon** <term>first quarter moon: (*shangxian* 上弦) literally "upper bowstring," the phase of the waxing moon representing the rising of Yang energy.</term> to serve as the outer furnace’s **embankment** <term>embankment: (*difang* 隄防) a dike or dam; metaphorically, the firm "Gold" of the mind or intent that contains the fluid "Water" of the essence.</term> on the southeastern wall. Then, the half-pound of Water from the **last quarter moon** <term>last quarter moon: (*xiaxian* 下弦) literally "lower bowstring," the phase of the waning moon representing the gathering of Yin energy.</term> flows down from the west and enters into—

While the previous page introduced the metaphor of Gold acting as an embankment for Water, this page delves into the numerology of the lunar cycle—using the thirty days of the month to represent the waxing and waning of internal energies (Yang and Yin)—and the precise "weights" required for the internal elixir to form.</meta>

# Elucidation of the Seal of the Unity of the Three

The **Northern Internal Furnace** <term>Northern Internal Furnace: (*beifang neilu* 北方内爐) In the Five Elements system, North corresponds to Water and the Kidneys. The "internal furnace" refers to the meditative space within the body where the "Inner Elixir" is forged.</term> is such that its power is not forced; rather, it moves with a leisurely and self-composed grace. The count of **Gold** <note>original: *jin* 金, often translated as Metal or Gold; here representing the primordial Yang energy.</note> reaching fifteen refers to the period from the first day of the lunar month to the fifteenth. This is what is meant by the "half-pound of Gold" of the **First Quarter** <term>First Quarter: (*shangxian* 上弦) Literally the "upper bowstring," the waxing moon. In alchemy, this represents the gradual accumulation of Yang energy.</term>. The number of Water is likewise determined by the period from the sixteenth day to the thirtieth; this is what is meant by the "half-pound of Water" of the **Last Quarter** <term>Last Quarter: (*xiaxian* 下弦) Literally the "lower bowstring," the waning moon, representing the return of Yin energy.</term>.

In the method of the Elixir, one first uses the **Civil** fire to ascend, and then uses the **Military** fire to descend. <term>Civil and Military: (*wen* 文 and *wu* 武) Technical terms for meditative breathing. "Civil" is gentle and slow, used for nourishing; "Military" is forceful and rapid, used for transformation.</term> If one does not proceed in this manner, the energy cannot reach deep down into the **Nine Springs** <note>The "Nine Springs" (*jiuquan* 九泉) is a traditional Chinese name for the underworld or the deepest depths of the earth; in internal alchemy, it refers to the lowest energy centers of the body.</note> below. Therefore it is said: *Approaching the furnace, determine the weights; with five parts of water, there is a surplus.*

Now, Gold and Water are each half-portions, which together combine to make the "two-eights" that form the sum of one **pound** <note>In the traditional Chinese system, one pound (*jin*) consists of sixteen ounces (*liang*). Therefore, two "eight-ounce" portions (Gold and Water) perfectly complete the unit of one pound, symbolizing a state of perfect balance.</note>. One closing and one opening, coming and going without end—this is the **True Yin and Yang** of our own bodies. However, the Water must be a half-pound with a slight surplus, and the Gold must not be allowed to lack its own half-pound weight. Thus it is said: *The two are taken as True Gold, heavy as it was at the very beginning.* As for why the **Earth** <note>The element of Earth (*tu* 土) acts as the central stabilizer or "mediator" in alchemical reactions, but the author here is explaining why it might appear to be absent or "not enter" during specific phases of the mixing of Gold and Water.</note> does not then enter...

He emphasizes the use of Civil and Military fire to circulate energy and the importance of precise "weights" (eight ounces each) to achieve the perfect pound of the Elixir.</summary>

# Imperial Commissioned Complete Library of the Four Treasuries | Volume I

Circulating within the **tripod and furnace** <term>Tripod and furnace: (*dingqi* 鼎器) The vessel where the elixir is formed. In internal alchemy, this refers to the practitioner's body and the specific energetic centers used in meditation.</term>, there is only the **First Quarter’s** half-pound of **Gold** and the **Last Quarter’s** half-pound of **Water**; however, **Earth** <note>original: *tu* 土. In the Five Elements, Earth represents the center, stability, and the "True Intention" of the practitioner.</note> has no fixed position. 

When it is said that Earth "does not enter," it does not mean it is literally absent. Rather, because Earth has no fixed location, there is nowhere it does not go, and nowhere it does not enter. For example, among the twenty-four positions of Heaven and Earth <note>The twenty-four positions refer to the twenty-four directions of the compass or the solar terms of the year.</note>, there is no mention of the celestial stems **Wu** or **Ji** <term>Wu and Ji: (戊己) Two of the ten celestial stems that correspond specifically to the element of Earth. They represent the "Central Earth" that harmonizes the other elements.</term>. Does this mean *Wu* and *Ji* are truly missing? 

In fact, from the positions of **Water and the North** <note>original: *ren-zi* 壬子, directions/times associated with the peak of Yin and Water.</note> to the positions of **Heaven and the Northwest** <note>original: *qian-hai* 乾亥, directions/times associated with the completion of the cycle.</note>, through all the shifting movements and irregular risings and fallings, there is no place that is not the wandering ground of *Wu* and *Ji*. 

The two elements—Gold and Water—exist alongside Earth. Throughout the entire space of the tripod and furnace, all is Earth, and both Gold and Water travel in company with it. When Gold and Water travel together with Earth, the three substances contain and receive one another, blending together to become one. 

When they blend into one, it is like **"binding the clouds over ten thousand acres of green mountains"** <note>A poetic metaphor for stabilizing the "breath" or the volatile thoughts of the mind.</note> or **"fishing out the wheel of the moon from the emerald pool."** <note>A common image for the realization of the Golden Elixir or the "true self" emerging from the depths of the body's internal energies.</note> In this state, the manner of transformation becomes truly divine. As the *Chapter on the Ultimate Way* says:

When Earth, Gold, and Water fuse, they achieve a divine state of unity described through poetic metaphors of nature.</summary>

## The Seal of the Unity of the Three: Middle Volume

Ascending and descending are named **Gold** and **Water**; the timing of their movement, associated with the trigram **Xun** <term>Xun: (巽) One of the eight trigrams of the *I Ching*, representing Wind. In internal alchemy, it symbolizes the "bellows" or the subtle breath that directs the internal energy.</term>, is called the **True Wind** <note>original: *zhen feng* 真風. This refers to the refined breath or focused intention used to circulate energy through the body's meridians.</note>. Generally, that which ascends is Gold, and that which descends is Water; however, the agent that facilitates this very ascending and descending is **Earth** <note>Building on the previous page, Earth represents the "True Intention" (真意) that stabilizes the volatile energies of Gold (Yang) and Water (Yin).</note>.

Nevertheless, these three substances—Gold, Water, and Earth—are nothing more than established images and metaphors. To speak of it ultimately: the **Great Dao** <term>Dao: (道) The Way; the ultimate, formless reality and the source of all existence.</term> has always been divorced from names and appearances <note>original: *ming xiang* 名相. A term used to describe the conceptual labels and physical forms that humans use to categorize reality, which ultimately fall short of the truth.</note>. True Immortals are fundamentally free of "flowers and grass" <note>A poetic metaphor suggesting that those who have attained the Dao are beyond artificial constructs, superficialities, or the ornamental language used in alchemical texts.</note>. 

What "Gold" or "Water" could there truly be? What "Earth" could there truly be?

<summary>Yu Yan explains that while 'Gold,' 'Water,' and 'Earth' describe the directional flow of internal energy, these terms are ultimately metaphors for a nameless, formless Dao.</summary>

# Elucidation of the Seal of the Unity of the Three

Page Sixty-four

->Below there is the <term>Great Yang breath<note>original: 太陽氣 (tàiyáng qì). In internal alchemy, this refers to the pure, active masculine energy within the body, often associated with the sun.</note></term> concealed and steaming; in a brief moment, it first liquefies and then congeals, called the **Yellow Carriage Horse**.<-

> The *Book of Jade Lingzhi* <note>An ancient text on Daoist longevity and alchemical theory.</note> states: Whenever <term>refining the elixir<note>original: 煉丹 (liàndān). The process of transforming internal energies—essence, breath, and spirit—into a spiritual "pill" of immortality.</note></term>, one must start the fire according to the <term>Yang breath<note>original: 陽氣 (yángqì). The vital, active energy associated with light, heat, and expansion.</note></term> of the <term>*Zi* hour<note>original: 子時 (zǐshí). The period between 11:00 PM and 1:00 AM, marking the "Midnight" of the daily cycle when the first stirrings of Yang energy emerge from the depths of Yin.</note></term>; only then is the fire’s power complete.
> To start the fire at any other time is ineffective. This is because at the *Zi* hour, the Great Yang (the sun’s energy) is located in the North.
> In the human body, the breath reaches the <term>Tailbone Gate<note>original: 尾閭關 (wěilǘ guān). The lowest "pass" or "gate" at the base of the spine. In Daoist practice, energy must be forced through this point to begin its ascent up the back.</note></term>. If one starts the fire at this time, the internal and external are harmonized; one may then "steal the mechanism" of Heaven and Earth to <term>complete the elixir<note>original: 成丹 (chéngdān). The successful crystallization of the alchemical work.</note></term>.
> At first, the Great Yang is below, where Water and Fire copulate; the two breaths are <term>indistinctly mingling<note>original: 絪縕 (yīnyùn). A classic term describing the generative, swirling state of primordial matter or energy just before it takes a specific form.</note></term>, steaming to become liquid.
> Next, the fire within the water erupts, the Yang breath gradually blazes, and the liquid then congeals. From within this, the <term>Golden Flower<note>original: 金華 (jīnhuá). A metaphorical term for the purified essence or the "light" of the original spirit.</note></term> is forced out.
> This is named <term>True Lead<note>original: 真鉛 (zhēnqiān). A key alchemical metaphor representing the "Water" within the "Fire" or the primordial essence found within the body's depths.</note></term>. As it is put into operation and ascends, it rises vigorously like a chariot traveling upon the <term>Yellow Path<note>original: 黃道 (huángdào). Traditionally the ecliptic path of the sun; in internal alchemy, it refers to the central path or the proper meridian through which the essence ascends.</note></term>. For this reason, it is called the **Yellow Carriage Horse**.

# Orthodox Lineage of Complete Perfection: Volume One

When the years and months are about to end, one’s inner nature is destroyed and the years of life are harmed.

On the thirtieth day of the twelfth lunar month, the celestial cycle is nearly complete; the sun reaches the end of its station, and the moon reaches the end of its record. If we speak of this in terms of the <term>Alchemical Path<note>original: 丹道 (dāndào). The specific internal practices and theories used to achieve spiritual immortality.</note></term>, the human body possesses its own <term>Cosmic Cycle<note>original: 一周天 (yī zhōutiān). Often called the "Microcosmic Orbit," this refers to the circulation of energy through the main meridians of the body, mirroring the movement of the heavens.</note></term>, which is no different from that of Heaven and Earth.

<gloss>The physical form becomes as ash and soil, appearing like dust in a sunlit window.</gloss>

An ancient song says: "Use lead, yet do not use lead; one must work within lead. But when it comes time to use lead, using lead is still a mistake." It also says: "Lead is the mother of the sprout; the sprout is the child of lead. Once you have obtained the <term>Golden Flower<note>original: 金華 (jīnhuá). A symbol of the refined, illuminated spirit that emerges from the alchemical process.</note></term>, discard the lead and do not use it." Its meaning is profound. 

People only know that at the <term>*Zi* hour<note>Midnight, or the moment of the first stirring of energy.</note></term>, the <term>Kidney Breath<note>original: 腎氣 (shènqì). The foundational energy stored in the lower body, associated with the element of Water.</note></term> arises. When it undergoes <term>fire-refining<note>original: 烹煉 (pēngliàn). The process of "cooking" or purifying internal energies through meditation and breathwork.</note></term>, it congeals to become a liquid. They then mistakenly believe this to be the <term>True Lead<note>original: 真鉛 (zhēnqiān). In internal alchemy, this is not the metal, but the "original essence" or foundational vitality of the body.</note></term> and wish to use it to "transform" the <term>True Mercury<note>original: 真汞 (zhēngǒng). Symbolizes the spirit or the mind, which must be stabilized by the "Lead" of vitality.</note></term> of the <term>Li Palace<note>original: 離宮 (lígōng). The heart center, associated with the fire trigram (☲) and the spirit.</note></term>. They fail to consider...

# Elucidation of the Seal of the Unity of the Three: Page 65

Once the substance has turned into a liquid, it possesses both form and physical quality. Its body is heavy and turbid; how could it possibly flow against the natural current and ascend? 

When the <term>Immortals<note>original: 神仙 (shénxiān). Spiritually perfected beings who have achieved longevity or transcendence through alchemical practice.</note></term> craft the elixir, they do no more at this stage than ignite the fire. By applying heat from below, they stimulate the movement of its <term>Breath<note>original: 氣 (qì). The vital energy or "vapor" released when the alchemical substance is heated.</note></term>. 

Once the strength of the fire is abundant, the Breath surges upward in a vast expanse, resembling the way clouds rise from mountains and rivers. When it reaches the <term>Mud Pill Palace<note>original: 泥丸 (níwán). A Daoist term for the Upper Cinnabar Field located in the center of the brain, the seat of the spirit.</note></term>, it transforms into a "sweet rain" that descends into the <term>Twelve-Storied Pagoda<note>original: 重樓 (chónglóu). An alchemical name for the throat or trachea, through which the refined essence descends back into the body.</note></term>. Ultimately, the physical dregs of the substance are never actually used. 

This is precisely what is meant in the alchemical methods by "extracting the clear and discarding the turbid." That which is clear floats and remains above; this is what is described as "appearing like dust dancing in a sunlit window." That which is turbid sinks and remains below; this is what is called "the physical form becoming as ash and soil." 

Those who practice <term>External Alchemy<note>original: 外丹 (wàidān). The laboratory practice of refining minerals and herbs in a crucible.</note></term> collect the substance that flies up and congeals on the lid of the tripod, naming it "Dust of the Sunlit Window." <term>Lord Wei<note>original: 魏公 (Wèi Gōng). Referring to Wei Boyang, the legendary 2nd-century author of the *Cantong Qi*.</note></term> used this concept to illuminate the principles of <term>Internal Alchemy<note>original: 內丹 (nèidān). The practice of using the human body itself as the laboratory to refine one's internal energy and spirit.</note></term>, intending for students to apply these analogies and expand their understanding.

<summary>Yu Yan explains that the alchemical elixir is not the heavy physical liquid itself, but the refined vapor (the "clear") that rises through heat, leaving the "turbid" dregs behind. This internal process is compared to the laboratory collection of sublimated powder on a crucible lid.</summary>

<keywords>Internal Alchemy (Neidan), External Alchemy (Waidan), Mud Pill Palace (Niwan), Twelve-Storied Pagoda (Chonglou), Refinement, Wei Boyang, Qi, Sublimation</keywords>

# "Grinding, Preparing, and Combining Them; Rushing into the Crimson Gate"

"Grinding, preparing, and combining them" refers to the process where substances from two different places are ground down and brought together in a single <term>Stove<note>original: 爐 (lú). In internal alchemy, this typically represents the Lower Cinnabar Field (Dantian), where the "fire" of transformation is ignited.</note></term>. There, a pool of <term>Divine Water<note>original: 神水 (shénshuǐ). A metaphor for the refined essence or internal secretions produced during meditation.</note></term> congeals to form the <term>True Curd<note>original: 真酥 (zhēnsū). A symbolic term for the first solidifying stage of the elixir, often compared to the richness of clarified butter.</note></term>. 

"Rushing into the crimson gate" means capturing the essence and marrow of the <term>Rabbit and Crow<note>original: 兔烏 (tù wū). Traditional symbols for the Moon (the Yin rabbit) and the Sun (the Yang crow). They represent the dualities of Yin and Yang, or Lead and Mercury, that must be unified.</note></term> and urgently placing them into the <term>Cauldron<note>original: 鼎 (dǐng). The upper vessel of transformation, often associated with the head or the heart area in internal practice.</note></term> to be fired. The Great Medicine of the Golden Elixir is produced within <term>Kun<note>original: 坤 (kūn). The trigram for Earth, representing the receptive, feminine principle and the location of the Stove.</note></term> and planted within <term>Qian<note>original: 乾 (qián). The trigram for Heaven, representing the creative, masculine principle and the location of the Cauldron.</note></term>. 

Qian resides above as the Cauldron; Kun resides below as the Stove. Without intense cooking and extreme forging, the substance cannot emerge from the Stove; without backward movement and reverse rotation, it cannot ascend to the Cauldron.

> The *Chapters on Awakening to the Truth* says: "Seize them with both hands and make them fight to the death, until they transform into a single lump of <term>Purple Golden Frost<note>original: 紫金霜 (zǐjīn shuāng). A poetic name for the highly refined, crystalline state of the alchemical elixir.</note></term>."
> 
> It also says: "The <term>River Chariot<note>original: 河車 (héchē). A metaphor for the 'Microcosmic Orbit,' the practice of circulating vital energy (Qi) up the spine and down the front of the body.</note></term> dares not pause for even a moment, as it transports the substance to the summit of the <term>Kunlun Peak<note>original: 崑崙 (kūnlún). An alchemical name for the top of the head (the Upper Dantian), named after the mythical mountain home of the gods.</note></term>."

This covers the "Post-Heaven" <note>original: 後天 (hòutiān). Refers to the state of existence after birth, involving conscious effort and physical breath-work.</note> practice of applying effort, which differs from the time of producing the medicine in the primordial "Pre-Heaven" <note>original: 先天 (xiāntiān). The state of pure, original energy before physical manifestation.</note> state. Within this stage, there is further the practice of "observing the heart and drawing in the spirit"...

That the <term>Two Uses<note>original: 二用 (èr yòng). Associated with the trigram Xun (Wind), this represents the rhythmic inhalation and exhalation that fuels the internal fire.</note></term> to draw in the True Spirit?

### Securely Plugging the Opening to Ensure Completeness

The *Collection on the Great Achievement of the Golden Elixir* says: "Know the proper time to start the work. Once you have gathered the ingredients, you must securely seal the <term>Divine Hut<note>original: 神廬 (shénlú). A metaphorical name for the residence of the spirit within the body, which must be kept 'airtight' to prevent the vital energy from leaking out.</note></term> and never let it leak out easily." 

It also says: "Move the energy back to the summit of the head to form the <term>Three Flowers<note>original: 三花 (sānhuā). Represents the 'gathering of the three flowers at the crown,' where a practitioner's refined Essence, Breath, and Spirit unify into a single state of realization.</note></term>. Firmly shut the <term>Jade Gate<note>original: 玉關 (yùguān). An energetic 'pass' or gate within the body—often associated with the base of the spine or the back of the head—that must be 'locked' to prevent the loss of refined energy.</note></term> with a golden lock." 

Generally, when the <term>Golden Cinnabar<note>original: 金砂 (jīnshā). The refined substance of the elixir as it begins to rise through the body's channels.</note></term> rises into the <term>Cauldron<note>original: 鼎 (dǐng). The upper center of transformation, usually located in the head (the Upper Cinnabar Field).</note></term>, one must seal and secure it with the utmost care and secrecy. Only then can the <term>Sacred Embryo<note>original: 聖胎 (shèngtāi). The spiritual 'child' or new immortal self that is conceived and nurtured within the practitioner through the alchemical process.</note></term> reach a state of wholeness and completion.

The 'field' in the lower abdomen where energy is concentrated and transformed.</note></term> is flourishing, clouds steam and mists gather; a wind arises in the <term>Mud Pill<note>original: 泥丸 (níwán). The Daoist name for the upper energy center located in the brain.</note></term>, and there are indeed sounds like a humming Dragon and a roaring Tiger.

Those in the <term>Nine-Fold Heavens<note>original: 九華天上 (jiǔhuá tiānshàng). A poetic reference to the highest celestial realms or a state of high spiritual attainment.</note></term> will know..."
