# Elucidation of the Seal of the Unity of the Three
## Volume One

...at night, wind and thunder shake the ten thousand mountains; those who have experienced this for themselves will naturally know it.

-> **Begin with the Civil to allow cultivation; finish with the Martial to bring it to fruition.** <-

At the start of <term>Gathering the Medicine<note>original: 採藥 (cǎiyào). The foundational practice of identifying and capturing the vital internal essences within the body to begin the alchemical transformation.</note></term>, one must concentrate the spirit and gather the breath, harmonizing the nasal inhalation and exhalation so they respond to the movement of the hand. 

When the <term>Spirit and Breath<note>original: 神氣 (shénqì). The union of consciousness (Shen) and vital energy (Qi).</note></term> enter their <term>Root<note>original: 根 (gēn). Refers to the origin point of vitality, typically the Lower Cinnabar Field (Dantian) or the "Humble Hut."</note></term>, if you close the breath too tightly, you err by being too "urgent"; if you release it too loosely, you err by being too "dissipated." One must simply make the breath continuous and unbroken, never allowing for any interruption. 

After the spirit has been concentrated for a long time, it will naturally congeal; after the breath has been held for a long time, it will naturally settle. After a short while, thunder crashes at the <term>Wind Gate<note>original: 巽戶 (xùnhù). Literally the "Xun Gate." In the I Ching, the Xun trigram represents Wind; in alchemy, this refers to the respiratory process and the entry point of the internal breath.</note></term>, and the Dragon leaps while the Tiger soars. This drives the energy back through the <term>Tail Gate<note>original: 尾穴 (wěixué). Also known as the Wei Lü, the lowest gate of the spine near the coccyx.</note></term>, trailing flames through the void as it is driven into the <term>Heavenly Pathway<note>original: 天衢 (tiānqú). A poetic name for the Du meridian, the energetic path running up the spine to the head.</note></term>, surging straight upward.

<term>Wang Baoyi<note>A commentator or alchemical master cited by Yu Yan to provide authority on the firing process.</note></term> says: "The <term>Civil Fire<note>original: 文火 (wénhuǒ). A gentle, slow, and rhythmic heating process used in the early and middle stages of the work.</note></term> is the fire of generation; the <term>Martial Fire<note>original: 武火 (wǔhuǒ). A fierce, rapid, and intense heat used to finalize the elixir or overcome energetic blockages.</note></term> is the fire of fruition." This is because the process begins with generation and ends with fruition; starting with the Civil and ending with the Martial, these two stages cannot be treated as a single, uniform path.

<summary>Yu Yan describes the specific breathing techniques required to circulate internal energy from the lower torso up the spine, emphasizing the transition from gentle "Civil Fire" to intense "Martial Fire."</summary>

# Observe with increased caution; carefully investigate to adjust the cold and warmth.

"Observing with increased caution" means to <term>seal the openings and lower the curtains<note>original: 塞兌垂簾 (sāi duì chuí lián). A meditative instruction to close the mouth ("the opening") and lower the eyelids ("the curtains") to prevent the internal energy from leaking out through the senses.</note></term>, containing one's inner light in silence. One waits for the <term>Qi of Heaven and Earth<note>original: 天地之氣 (tiāndì zhī qì). The primordial vital energy that the practitioner seeks to harness from within their own body.</note></term> to approach; only then does one stabilize the breath to harvest it, gathering the "fire" of concentration to "cook" the essence. Throughout this, one must strictly observe whether the internal medicine is "old" or "tender" <note>In alchemical practice, "tender" (nèn) refers to energy that is too young or weak to be harvested, while "old" (lǎo) refers to energy that has passed its peak and become "stale." The practitioner seeks the perfect moment of maturity.</note>; this process must never be handled carelessly. 

The *Chapters on Awakening to Reality* states: "Success depends entirely on the power of cultivation; a hair's breadth of difference, and the <term>Golden Elixir<note>original: 金丹 (jīndān). The refined state of spiritual and physical immortality or perfection.</note></term> will not form." How could one possibly treat this lightly?

"Carefully investigating to adjust the cold and warmth" means to harmonize the strength of the <term>Firing Process<note>original: 火候 (huǒhòu). The specific timing and intensity of heat (breath and focus) applied during meditation to transform internal energies.</note></term>. One must investigate with careful deliberation, moving from gentle to fierce in a gradual manner. One must not advance with frantic haste or sudden movements. 

The *Chapters on Returning to Life* says: "The firing process must be firm, steady, and carefully examined; the blowing of the breath depends entirely on the <term>Wind from the Direction of Xun<note>original: 巽方風 (xùnfāng fēng). In the I Ching, the trigram Xun represents Wind and is associated with the breath. This passage emphasizes using rhythmic breathing to stoke the internal heat.</note></term>." How could one ever justify a sudden, rushed advance?

# Revolving through the twelve joints; when the stages are complete, observe even more closely.

The "twelve joints" refer to the <term>Twelve Earthly Branches<note>original: 十二辰 (shí'èr chén). These are the twelve segments used in traditional Chinese cosmology—such as Zi, Chou, Yin, etc.—to divide the day into two-hour periods and the year into months. In alchemy, they represent stages of energy movement.</note></term> of the cosmic orbit. Within our own bodies, these represent the specific positions and timings of the <term>Firing Process<note>original: 火候 (huǒhòu). The precise regulation of breath and meditative focus used to "cook" the internal elixir.</note></term>. 

The twelve stages of the Firing Process ascend from the <term>Zi position<note>original: 子 (zǐ). Midnight or the winter solstice; the moment when the first spark of active Yang energy returns.</note></term> and descend until the <term>Wu position<note>original: 午 (wǔ). Noon or the summer solstice; the peak of Yang energy and the beginning of the Yin descent.</note></term>. This cycle revolves around the exterior of the <term>Cauldron<note>original: 鼎器 (dǐngqì). A metaphorical vessel located in the lower abdomen where the practitioner’s vital essence is refined.</note></term>, just as the twelve branches revolve through the heavens. When the twelve stages of the fire have all been traversed and the full cosmic orbit is complete, the <term>Celestial Handle<note>original: 天岡 (tiāngāng). The handle of the Big Dipper, which serves as a cosmic clock, pointing to the current "season" of the internal work.</note></term> returns to point once more at the *Zi* position. Therefore, the text says: "Revolving through the twelve joints; when the stages are complete, observe even more closely."

The *Chapters on Emerald Emptiness* <note>original: 翠虛篇 (Cuìxū piān). A collection of poems attributed to the Southern Lineage master Chen Nan.</note> states: "The <term>Zhen Hexagram<note>original: 震卦 (zhèn guà). Representing 'Thunder,' the first movement of life-energy in the spring or at dawn.</note></term> travels and returns to the western <term>Dui region<note>original: 兑鄉 (duì xiāng). Representing 'Lake' or autumn, the stage where the energy is gathered and harvested.</note></term>; the <term>Fair Maiden<note>original: 姹女 (chànǚ). An alchemical code word for the 'Fluid of the Heart' or refined Mercury/Spirit.</note></term> of the Three Yangs toys with her bright pendants. The <term>Xun Wind<note>original: 巽風 (xùn fēng). Representing the gentle, consistent breathing used to fan the internal fire.</note></term> blows through the coral trees; entering the <term>Gen<note>original: 艮 (gèn). The 'Mountain' hexagram, signifying stillness and the cessation of movement.</note></term> and returning to the <term>Kun<note>original: 坤 (kūn). The 'Earth' hexagram, signifying total receptivity and the void.</note></term>, another <term>Yang<note>original: 陽 (yáng). The active, creative principle of life.</note></term> begins." This passage shares the same underlying meaning as our text.

# When the breath is exhausted, life is about to end; in the state of cessation and death, the soul and spirit perish.

<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of the internal alchemical process, transitioning from the timing of the "Firing Process" to the physical and spiritual sensations experienced when the Elixir finally congeals. It addresses the paradoxical state where the practitioner appears to be dead, yet is reaching the pinnacle of life.</meta>

Now, in the practice of refining the **Golden Elixir**, one seeks to attain longevity. Why, then, does **Master Wei** <note>Wei Boyang, the purported author of the original *Seal of the Unity of the Three*.</note> claim that "the breath is exhausted and life is about to end," or speak of "death and the vanishing of the souls"? The *Emerald Void* <note>original: 翠虚篇 (*Cuìxū piān*). A classic text of the Southern School of Daoist alchemy, attributed to Chen Nan.</note> says:

->Urge the hundred vessels to return to their source;<-
->When the pulse stops and breath halts, the Elixir begins to form.<-

This is because at the moment the **Golden Liquid** congeals, the <term>Jade Traverse and the Armillary Sphere<note>original: 璇璣玉衡 (*xuánjī yùhéng*). These were ancient astronomical instruments used to observe the stars. In alchemy, they represent the internal "celestial mechanism" or the rhythmic circulation of energy in the body.</note></term> momentarily stop their rotation. The "sun-soul" and "moon-soul" <note>original: 日魂月魄 (*rìhún yuèpò*). The *Hun* (spiritual/Yang) and *Po* (animal/Yin) souls, here correlated with the energies of the Sun and Moon.</note> both sink into the <term>Dharma Sea of the North<note>original: 北方法海底 (*běifāng fǎhǎidǐ*). In the Five Elements system, the North corresponds to Water and the kidneys; this refers to the lower "Cinnabar Field" or the void from which life emerges.</note></term>, where they are extinguished and hidden. Therefore, it is said that "the breath is exhausted and life is about to end," and "death and the vanishing of the souls." 

What is referred to here as "death" is not actually death. At this moment, one **Returns to the Root and Resumes Life** <note>original: 歸根復命 (*guīgēn fùmìng*). A phrase from the *Daodejing* describing the return to a state of primordial stillness and potential.</note>; the Spirit congeals and the Vital Essence solidifies. The eight vessels all come to a standstill and respiration ceases entirely. The breath is so faint it seems severed. 

This state of reviving after such a severance is what the *Collection of Supreme Clarity* <note>original: 上清集 (*Shàngqīng jí*). A compilation of alchemical and Daoist writings, likely referencing the works of Bai Yuchan.</note> describes as:

->“Having undergone the **Great Death**, one now truly lives.”<-

Indeed! If you wish to know the time when the **Great Medicine** is formed: it is when the six pulses all stop and the breath no longer gallops. Such is the nature of this scene...

<summary>Yu Yan explains the paradoxical state of the "Great Death," where the practitioner's breath and pulse stop during the final stages of Elixir formation, signaling a spiritual rebirth rather than physical expiration.</summary>
<keywords>Golden Elixir, Master Wei, Great Death, Great Medicine, breath-stopping, returning to the root, internal alchemy, Southern School</keywords>

# Elucidation of the Seal of the Unity of the Three
## Imperial Edition of the Complete Library of the Four Treasuries, Volume Two

If one has not personally experienced this state, how can one presume to speak of it?

->The color shifts and turns further into purple;<-
->Brilliantly, the **Returned Elixir** is formed.<-

The *Emerald Void* <note>original: 翠虚篇 (*Cuìxū piān*). A Southern School Daoist text attributed to Chen Nan (d. 1213).</note> says: "Why is it that one sheds the yellow and puts on the purple? It is only because the **River Carriage** <note>original: 河車 (*héchē*). A metaphor for the internal circulation of energy (qi) up the spine and down the front of the body.</note> has turned many times." Generally, when the "Firing Process" of the **Nine Cycles** <note>original: 九轉火候 (*jiǔzhuǎn huǒhòu*). The complete alchemical cycle; nine is the number of ultimate Yang and completion.</note> has reached its full count, the **Returned Elixir** becomes brilliantly luminous, transforming into the color of purple-gold.

Ingesting it in a measure of "one-nine" <term>spatula<note>original: 刀圭 (*dāoguī*). An ancient alchemical measuring tool, roughly the size of a small spatula or the tip of a blade. In internal alchemy, it represents a minute but potent dose of the internal nectar.</note></term> is most miraculous.

The *Emerald Void* says: 
"Gathering and refining it in less than a moment, a single breath vaguely passes through the **Three Passes** <note>original: 三關 (*sānguān*). The three critical points along the spine—the lower back (Jade Pillow), the mid-back (Silver River), and the upper neck—through which energy must be pushed.</note>. Through the **Three Passes**, the breath flows back and forth without end; a single white vessel flows toward the <term>Muddy Pellet<note>original: 泥丸 (*níwán*). A Daoist term for the upper Cinnabar Field located in the center of the brain, considered the seat of the spirit.</note></term>. Atop the **Muddy Pellet** sits a purple-gold tripod; inside the tripod is a mass of purple-gold. It transforms into **Jade Nectar** <note>original: 玉漿 (*yùjiāng*). A refined form of saliva or internal fluid experienced during deep meditation, described as tasting sweet and cool.</note> and flows into the mouth, fragrant and sweet, refreshing the tip of the tongue."

<summary>This page describes the final maturation of the alchemical Elixir, where the energy transforms from "yellow" to "purple-gold" and manifests physically as a sweet "Jade Nectar" flowing from the brain to the mouth.</summary>

Swallowing it and ingesting it, it enters the **Five Internal Organs** <note>original: 五內 (*wǔnèi*). Refers to the heart, liver, spleen, lungs, and kidneys.</note>. The viscera and bowels become profoundly free-flowing, and the body attains a state of health and peacefulness. What is called a **spatula-tip** <term>daogui<note>original: 刀圭 (*dāoguī*). An ancient measurement for powdered medicine. Here, it refers to the tiny, precise "dose" of internal energy that triggers the final transformation.</note></term> is merely a tiny amount, like the tip of a blade or the corner of a jade tablet. 

When success is finally achieved, the transformations are myriad and the wonders are beyond measure; this is not something that the marginal paths or "lesser techniques" <note>original: 旁門小術 (*pángmén xiǎoshù*). A common term in Daoist literature for practices considered superficial, external, or incomplete compared to the "Great Medicine" of Internal Alchemy.</note> can hope to equal. Is it not fitting, then, to call this "Divine"?

### Deducing the Numbers of the Five Elements: Concise and Uncomplicated

The *Book of Changes* <note>original: 易 (*Yì*), referring to the *I Ching* or *Classic of Changes*.</note> says: "Heaven is one, Earth is two; Heaven is three, Earth is four; Heaven is five, Earth is six; Heaven is seven, Earth is eight; Heaven is nine, Earth is ten." These are the **Generation and Completion Numbers** <note>original: 生成數 (*shēngchéng shù*). In Chinese numerology, the numbers 1 through 5 "generate" the elements, while 6 through 10 "complete" them.</note> of the **Five Elements**. 

The *Master of Flowing Brilliance* <note>original: 子華子 (*Zǐhuázǐ*). A Daoist philosophical text attributed to a master of the same name, likely dating from the Song Dynasty.</note> says: "Of the great numbers of Heaven and Earth, none exceeds five, and none is more central than five." This is because five is the number of **Soil** <note>original: 土數 (*tǔshù*). In the Five Elements system, Soil/Earth represents the center and the stabilizing force that harmonizes the other four elements.</note>, and its position is in the center. It combines with the "One" of Northern Water...

<summary>This page describes the sensory experience of ingesting the completed elixir—comparing it to honey and orchids—and begins a cosmological explanation of the Five Elements using numerology from the Book of Changes.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of the numerology of the Five Elements, specifically explaining how the "Generating Numbers" (1-4) combine with the central "Five" of Earth to create the "Completion Numbers" (6-9), illustrating the cosmological interdependence of all elements on the Center (Earth).</meta>

->**By Imperial Decree: The Complete Library of the Four Treasuries**<-
->**Elucidation of the *Seal of the Unity of the Three***<-

[Continuing from the previous page's discussion of the Generation and Completion Numbers of the...] **Five Elements**: when the 1 of the North is joined with the central 5, it forms **6**; when the 2 of the Southern Fire is joined with it, it forms **7**; when the 3 of the Eastern Wood is joined with it, it forms **8**; and when the 4 of the Western Metal is joined with it, it forms **9**.

**Nine** is the ultimate limit of numbers. All numbers under heaven reach their conclusion at nine. If we speak of the sequence of nine numbers, **Five** resides in the very center of 1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 7, 8, and 9; it is truly the **Central Number** <term>zhōngshù<note>original: 中數. In the *Hetu* (Yellow River Map) cosmology, 5 is the "generating" number of Earth and sits at the center of the diagram.</note></term>. 

In the fundamental nature of numbers, there is no "ten." What is called the **Completion Number of Earth**, which is ten, is actually the 1 of the North, the 2 of the South, the 3 of the East, and the 4 of the West all gathered together in the center, where they transform and become **ten**. 

Therefore, if one takes the **5** of the center and disperses it to the four directions to create 6, 7, 8, and 9, then Water, Fire, Wood, and Metal all rely upon **Earth** <note>In internal alchemy, this signifies that the various energies of the body (represented by the four directions/elements) cannot achieve their "completed" or "immortal" state without the stabilizing influence of the "True Earth" or the meditative center.</note> to reach their completion. 

Conversely, if one takes the 1, 2, 3, and 4 of the four directions and returns them to the center to form ten, then Water, Fire, Wood, and Metal all **return to the root and restore the origin** <note>original: 返本還源 (*fǎn běn huán yuán*). This is a vital alchemical phrase describing the reversal of the aging and dispersal process, bringing the "Five Agents" back into a single, unified primordial state.</note>, meeting together within the Earth.

Oh! <note>original: 吁 (*xū*). An archaic exclamation of wonder or realization.</note>

<summary>This page explains the numerological foundations of the Five Elements using the Hetu (Yellow River Map) system, emphasizing how the central element, Earth (represented by the number 5), is the essential catalyst for the completion and eventual reunification of all other energies.</summary>

# Volume One

->**How profound!**<-

The ***Chapters on Awakening to Reality*** <note>The *Wuzhen pian*, a foundational 11th-century text on internal alchemy by Zhang Boduan.</note> state: "The two substances are always formed when the child gives birth to the mother; the Five Elements must all enter the center." <note>original: "二物總因兒產母五行全要入中央". In alchemical theory, "the child producing the mother" refers to the "reversal" process where the practitioner traces existence back to its source, returning the elements to the "mother" or the void.</note>

The ***Precious Chapters on the Primal Chaos*** state: "The four positions are clearly used in reversal; the Five Elements rise together and return together."

All of these quotes elucidate this same principle. As for the numbers of the generation and completion of the **Five Elements** <term>wǔxíng<note>The five phases of Wood, Fire, Earth, Metal, and Water that govern all cosmic and bodily processes.</note></term>, although even a young child only three feet tall can recite and know them, how many people truly understand where their meaning "lands"? 

If one does not know where the meaning rests, how can one achieve their practical application? From this, we see that the essentials of the **Way** <term>Dào<note>The ultimate principle underlying the universe.</note></term> are subtle and mysterious, and the **Heavenly Mechanism** <term>tiānjī<note>original: 天機. The secret, fundamental workings of nature and the cosmos that are usually hidden from the uninitiated.</note></term> is profound and far-reaching. To those who have attained realization, the truth is found only in simplicity and ease; but for those who are confused, the path becomes increasingly complex and difficult.

<summary>Yu Yan argues that while the basic numerology of the Five Elements is common knowledge, the "reversal" of these elements to the center—the secret of internal alchemy—remains a profound mystery accessible only to the wise.</summary>

<meta>The text continues the examination of the "Seal of the Unity of the Three" by explaining the cosmological basis for internal alchemy. # Volume One

Lifting water to splash fire, the bright light is suddenly extinguished. The sun and moon clash and eclipse, usually occurring between the end of the lunar month and the new moon. When water is abundant, the *Kan* <note>The trigram ☵ representing the moon, water, and the inner Yin within Yang</note> hexagram invades the Yang; when fire fades, the *Li* <note>The trigram ☲ representing the sun, fire, and the inner Yang within Yin</note> hexagram makes the day dark. Yin and Yang consume and feed upon each other; this interaction is the natural way of the Dao.

Shao Kangjie <note>Shao Yong (1011–1077), a renowned philosopher who sought to explain the universe through mathematical and hexagram cycles</note> says in his *Book of Supreme World Ordering Principles* <note>original: "Huangji Jingshi Shu," a seminal work on cosmological numerology</note>: “The mutual eclipsing of the sun and moon is the intersection of their numerical cycles. When the sun faces the moon <note>at the full moon</note>, the moon is eclipsed; when the moon covers the sun <note>at the new moon</note>, the sun is eclipsed. This is like the mutual overcoming of water and fire.” 

Now, if one lifts water to splash fire, the fire is overcome by the water, and the firelight is suddenly extinguished. When the Great Yin <note>The Moon</note> covers the Great Yang <note>The Sun</note>, the Yang is defeated by the Yin, and the sunlight grows dark in the middle of the day. Why is this? When Yin and Yang interact, they must inevitably "consume" <note>original: "相食" (mutual eating); a traditional term for an eclipse where one body appears to devour the other</note> each other; this is the natural spontaneity of the Dao. 

Lord Wei <note>Wei Boyang, the legendary author of the source text, often called the "Father of Alchemy"</note> borrows the mechanism of solar and lunar eclipses to clarify the mysteries of the Alchemical Way <note><term>Dan Dao</term>: the "Way of the Elixir," referring here to Internal Alchemy (Neidan), where the practitioner's own body and spirit are the ingredients</note>, and simultaneously brings up the theory of the mutual overcoming of water and fire to illustrate this further.

<summary>This page explains that eclipses and the "mutual consumption" of sun and moon are natural expressions of the Dao, serving as a macrocosmic model for the interactions of Yin and Yang in internal alchemy.</summary>
<keywords>Shao Yong, Wei Boyang, eclipses, Yin and Yang, Kan and Li, Internal Alchemy, Dao, water and fire</keywords>

--- Page 157 ---
<meta>The text continues from the previous discussion regarding the astronomical cycles of the sun and moon. Having established the mathematical and natural basis for eclipses using Shao Yong's theories, the author now transitions into the practical application of these cosmic timings for internal alchemical cultivation.</meta>

->### Elucidation of the Seal of the Unity of the Three<-

This explanation can be considered quite detailed. However, solar and lunar eclipses always occur in the interval between the end of the old moon and the start of the new <note>original: "晦朔之間" (*huishuo zhijian*); this refers to the dark phase of the moon when it is invisible to the eye, symbolizing the point of absolute stillness before rebirth</note>. Those who refine the **Golden Elixir** <term>Jindan: The "pill" of immortality, a metaphor for the refined spiritual essence created within the practitioner's body</term> seek to "steal" the mechanics of Heaven and Earth and "seize" the creative power of nature. How, then, could they not seek to understand this "interval between the old and new moon"?

Names are used to define the "emotions," while "style names" <note>original: *zi*; a secondary name given at adulthood, used here to suggest a deeper or secondary aspect of a single substance</note> are tied to the "inner nature." When we say that "Metal comes to return to the beginning of Nature," it is only then that it can truly be called the **Returned Elixir** <note>original: *Huandan*; also translated as the Reverted Elixir, signifying the restoration of the soul to its original, pure state</note>. 

The *Chapter on Returning to Life* <note>original: *Fuming pian*, a famous work of the Southern School of Taoist Alchemy</note> says: "One thing is divided into two; if one can know these two, they can be named." These "two" are simply Metal and Wood.

Metal and Wood are essentially not two separate bodies. Therefore, if Metal is the name, then Wood is the style name; if Wood is the inner nature, then Metal is the emotion. In reality, they are a single thing. For **Lord Metal** <note>original: *Jin Gong*; an alchemical nickname for "True Lead," representing the hidden Yang essence within the body</note> was originally a son of the Eastern House, but he was sent to live with a western neighbor in a borrowed body. While in this state <note>separated from his origin</note>, it cannot yet be called the **Returned Elixir**. Only when he is poured back into the **Furnace of Creation of Eastern Yang**, returning home to meet the **Green Maiden** <note>original: *Qing E*; a poetic name for "True Mercury," representing the Yin essence</note>, will they produce a bright pearl that resembles the moon.

<summary>This page explains that the timing of alchemical refinement must mimic the dark moon phase (the interval between the old and new moon). It uses the metaphors of Metal and Wood, and the "Lord Metal" and "Green Maiden," to describe the unification of Yin and Yang forces required to create the Golden Elixir.</summary>

<keywords>Golden Elixir, Metal and Wood, Lord Metal, Green Maiden, Returned Elixir, Yin and Yang, Eastern Yang, Internal Alchemy</keywords>

->### Elucidation of the Seal of the Unity of the Three<-

->#### Volume I####<-

...only then is it complete, and can it be called the **Returned Elixir** <term>Huandan: Also known as the Reverted Elixir, this refers to the process of returning the human spirit to its original, primordial state of purity</term>. Some say that when one is "silent and unmoving" and the emotions return to the inner nature, it is called the Returned Elixir. This is indeed correct; however, it is not the original meaning of Lord Wei’s <note>Lord Wei refers to Wei Boyang, the 2nd-century author of the *Cantong qi*, the foundational text of Taoist alchemy</note> chapter concerning the "Emotions of Metal and the Nature of Wood."

I do not dare to speak empty words. I follow the writings of the Sages: the *Ancient Records* <note>original: *Guji*</note> manifest the **Dragon and Tiger**; the **Yellow Emperor** <term>Huangdi: The mythical ancestor of Chinese civilization and a patron of the internal arts</term> praised the **Golden Flower**; the Prince of **Huainan** refined the **Autumn Stone** <term>Qiushi: A substance in external alchemy, used here as a metaphor for concentrated essence</term>; and **Wang Yang** celebrated the **Yellow Sprout** <note>original: *Huangya*; a symbolic name for the first visible sign of the elixir forming within the body</note>.

The Dragon and Tiger of the *Ancient Records*, the Golden Flower of the Yellow Emperor, the Autumn Stone of Huainan, and the Yellow Sprout of Wang Yang—these are all nothing more than **symbolic titles** <note>original: *tuohao*; metaphorical names used to "house" or hide profound spiritual secrets from the uninitiated</note> used to convey subtle intentions. Now, when Lord Wei composed this book, how could he have been willing to create something out of thin air or indulge in baseless personal theories? He was also following the subtle intentions of the ancients. Master Zhu <note>Zhu Xi (1130–1200), the most influential Neo-Confucian scholar, who wrote a famous commentary on the *Cantong qi* under a pseudonym</note> said that the words used in this text are all based on ancient books, and this was certainly no empty claim.

<summary>Yu Yan defends the use of metaphorical language in alchemy by citing historical precedents from the Yellow Emperor and other sages, arguing that these "symbolic titles" are rooted in ancient tradition rather than personal invention.</summary>
<keywords>Returned Elixir (Huandan), Wei Boyang, Dragon and Tiger, Yellow Sprout, Zhu Xi, symbolic titles (tuohao), Metal and Wood</keywords>

->### Elucidation of the Seal of the Unity of the Three<-

->**The worthy can maintain and practice it; the unworthy should not be part of it.**<-

**Guiguzi** <note>Master of the Ghost Valley, a legendary sage-strategist often associated with hidden arts and the ability to read people</note> traveled with **Master Zihua** <note>Zihua Zi; a philosopher and sage whose teachings were recorded in a text of the same name</note> for twelve years. When his **attainment was complete** <term>Yecheng: This refers to the completion of his spiritual and technical studies in the Daoist arts</term>, he took his leave to return home. 

Master Zihua warned him, saying: "Regarding what you have mastered now, I have no criticisms to offer. However, your disposition is to take in knowledge broadly and give it away indiscriminately. I fear that you will fail to be selective in your choices. 

There will be those who will strip away your outer fortifications to build their own palace courts <note>This is a metaphor for disciples who take the external forms of a teacher's knowledge to build their own reputation or schools without understanding the core spirit</note>. They will climb into your carriage and ride it to gallop through the outskirts. They will take that which you treat as a **precious jade disc** <note>original: *Bi*; a circular jade ritual object symbolizing heaven and spiritual authority</note> and smash it into five pieces. 

Now, the **Way** <term>Dao: The ultimate principle of the universe and the path to spiritual immortality</term> certainly abhors non-transmission; if it is not passed on, the Way is obstructed. Yet, the Way also abhors failing to find the right person to receive the transmission. If the right recipient is not found, the Way becomes diseased. Now you are departing; reflect on how to be cautious."

<summary>This page uses a dialogue between Guiguzi and Master Zihua to emphasize the "Golden Rule" of alchemy: spiritual secrets must be shared to keep the tradition alive, but giving them to the "unworthy" destroys the integrity of the teaching.</summary>

->### Imperially Commissioned Complete Library of the Four Treasuries<-

->#### Volume I<-

"...if it were given to the right people, then I would have no criticism of you." <note>This concludes the warning from Master Zihua to Guiguzi found on the previous page, regarding the danger of teaching the unworthy.</note>

Now, **Lord Wei** <note>Wei Boyang, the 2nd-century author of the *Cantong qi*</note> says that the worthy can maintain and practice this Way, while the unworthy should not be part of it; his concern in this matter is indeed deep. 

Generally, the **unworthy** <term>Buxiao: Literally "not resembling" the sages; those who lack the moral and spiritual capacity to follow the Dao</term> are necessarily **frivolous and shallow** <term>Tiaobo: Characterized by a lack of seriousness and a tendency toward superficiality</term>. If they are frivolous and shallow, they must lack righteousness. Cases where such people do not eventually "draw the bow to shoot Yi" <note>original: *She Yi*; a reference to the legend of Pang Meng, who killed his archery teacher Hou Yi. "This Way is most divine and most sacred; do not let it leak out or be treated lightly. Success relies entirely on virtue and conduct being in harmony." 

One's speech must be guarded to avoid what is forbidden. One must understand that the **Great Way of the Golden Elixir** <term>Jindan Dadao: The practice of internal alchemy (Neidan) aimed at spiritual transformation and immortality</term> can only be transmitted to the worthy. If a person is not the right candidate, not only should you refuse to speak of it with them, but you should also keep your distance from them. 

Alas! Throughout history, those who have "transcended the world" and whose lives are recorded in our traditions were all heroes who could **control cranes and ride the mists and clouds** <note>original: *Konghe yu yanxia*; these are traditional poetic images describing the freedom and power of an Immortal</note>. Such achievements are certainly not for the foolish or the clumsy. How could those unworthy ones ever be capable of knowing this? 

From ancient times to the present, the Way has come from a single source. In conversation and in that which is planned...

<meta>This page continues the commentary on the accessibility of alchemical secrets. ->### Elucidation of the Seal of the Unity of the Three<-

->#### Page Seventy-four<-

"When one attains the **One**, the ten thousand affairs are finished." <note>This is a fundamental Daoist principle: 'The One' represents the primordial unity of the Dao. Attaining it means returning to the source of all existence, rendered here as 'finishing' or 'completing' all worldly complications.</note> How great is the **One**! Is it not the source of a thousand scriptures and ten thousand discourses, the ancestor of a thousand transformations and ten thousand changes? 

How true it is! Under heaven there is no second Way, and the sages have no second mind. While there are ten thousand volumes of immortal scriptures, their words all harmonize; the **Golden Elixir** <term>Jindan: In internal alchemy, this is the refined state of one's internal energies (Jing, Qi, and Shen) fused into a singular, indestructible spiritual seed.</term> is this very root and lineage.

Let the student exert themselves further, keeping this in mind and contemplating it deeply. These **most essential words** are clearly revealed; they are **bright and clear** <note>original: "Zhaozhao"; meaning luminous, manifest, or strikingly obvious</note> and do not deceive me.

**Lord Wei** <note>Wei Boyang, the legendary author of the text</note> states that if a student can repeatedly **savor and ponder** <term>Wanwei: To 'play with the flavor' of a text; a meditative style of reading where one digests the meaning slowly to find hidden depths.</term> this book, once they have grasped its meaning in their heart and spirit, they will know that my most essential words are indeed quite revealed and bright, without a single thread of deception or concealment. 

Alas! Lord Wei’s desire to guide later students is truly earnest. How could a student not believe and accept Lord Wei’s words, recite and maintain Lord Wei’s book, and keep it in mind for deep contemplation?

<summary>Yu Yan emphasizes the central importance of 'the One' as the root of all alchemical practice and reassures readers that Lord Wei Boyang’s instructions are clear and honest for the diligent seeker.</summary>

# Elucidation of the Seal of the Unity of the Three, Volume One

->### Elucidation of the Seal of the Unity of the Three<-

->#### Page Seventy-four<-

"When one attains the **One**, the ten thousand affairs are finished." <note>This is a fundamental Daoist principle: 'The One' represents the primordial unity of the Dao. Attaining it means returning to the source of all existence, rendered here as 'finishing' or 'completing' all worldly complications.</note> How great is the **One**! Is it not the source of a thousand scriptures and ten thousand discourses, the ancestor of a thousand transformations and ten thousand changes? 

How true it is! Under heaven there is no second Way, and the sages have no second mind. While there are ten thousand volumes of immortal scriptures, their words all harmonize; the **Golden Elixir** <term>Jindan: In internal alchemy, this is the refined state of one's internal energies (Jing, Qi, and Shen) fused into a singular, indestructible spiritual seed.</term> is this very root and lineage.

Let the student exert themselves further, keeping this in mind and contemplating it deeply. These **most essential words** are clearly revealed; they are **bright and clear** <note>original: "Zhaozhao"; meaning luminous, manifest, or strikingly obvious</note> and do not deceive me.

**Lord Wei** <note>Wei Boyang, the legendary author of the text</note> states that if a student can repeatedly **savor and ponder** <term>Wanwei: To 'play with the flavor' of a text; a meditative style of reading where one digests the meaning slowly to find hidden depths.</term> this book, once they have grasped its meaning in their heart and spirit, they will know that my most essential words are indeed quite revealed and...
